## Thursday 26 May 2022

## OT05: EVs in Immunity and Inflammation

## Chair: Steffi Bosch, PhD – IECM, Oniris, INRAE, USC1383, Nantes, France

## Chair: Duaa Dakhlallah

OT05.02.

## Extracellular vesicles released from Leishmania infected cells (LiEVs) induce macrophage polarization to the M2 type in infected tissues

Peter E. Kima; Lisa E. Emerson; Austin Sheppe; Mariola J. Edelmann

University of Florida, Gainesville, USA

Introduction: Visceral leishmaniasis is a neglected tropical disease that causes significant morbidity and mortality. Iconic images of the distended abdomen of infected individuals due to an enlarged liver and spleen underscores the tissue remodeling in these infections. It is not known how Leishmania‐derived molecules contribute to this disease presentation. To address this knowledge gap, we hypothesized that extracellular vesicles (EVs) released from Leishmania‐infected cells (LiEVs) that contain parasite derived molecules, induce cellular responses that promote tissue remodeling.

Methods: Infection in animals was tracked by changes in liver weight and by histochemical analyses of infected livers. To evaluate the responses of macrophages in vitro, peritoneal exudate cell (PEC) responses to LiEVs was obtained by RT‐PCR amplification of M1 targets (iNOS, IFNγ and TNFα) and M2 targets (Arginase 1 (Arg‐1), IL‐10 and IL‐4R). For in vivo analysis, F4/80+ cells were monitored by immunohistochemical staining for markers of M1 or M2 polarization subsets.

Results: After overnight incubation of PECs, EVs prepared from Salmonella‐infected RAW264.7 macrophages or LPS treatment induced robust expression of the M1 markers iNOS, IFNγ or TNFα. In contrast, LiEVs induced the expression of Arg‐1, IL‐10, and IL‐4. The Salmonella derived EVs did not induce expression of M2 targets in PECs. Next, in vivo studies revealed a 3‐ and 4‐fold increase of F4/80+ cells in L. donovani‐infected livers at 20‐ and 42‐days Post Infection (PI), respectively, as compared to uninfected livers. Immunohistochemical analysis showed that IL‐4R expression by F4/80+ cells in infected livers was widespread as compared to iNOS that was expressed more discretely by cells within granulomas.

Summary/Conclusion: The in vitro studies suggested that LiEVs released from Leishmania‐infected cells have the capacity to induce macrophage polarization to the M2 type. The in vivo studies that showed more widespread IL4R expression of macrophages in infected tissue, provided strong evidence of M2 polarization in L. donovani infected tissues, which would ensure parasite persistence.

Funding: This work was supported by U. S. Public Health Grants R03 AI‐135610 (MJE) and R56 AI143293 (PEK).

Keywords: Infection; tissue distribution; macrophage polarization, Leishmania

OT05.07.

## Inflammatory extracellular vesicle subpopulations contribute to blood–brain barrier dysfunction

Baharak Hosseinkhani
1; Gayel Duran1; Cindy Hoeks1; Isabel Pintelon2; Jean‐Pierre Timmermans2; Doryssa Hermans1; Kübra Bekar1; Melissa Schepers1; Paulien Baeten1; Luc Michiels3; Niels Hellings1; Bieke Broux1

1Neuro‐Immune Connections and Repair Lab, Department of Immunology and Infection, Biomedical Research Institute, UHasselt, Diepenbeek, Belgium, Hasselt, Belgium; 2Laboratory of Cell Biology & Histology Antwerp Centre for Advanced Microscopy (ACAM) University Antwerp Universiteitsplein 1, Antwerp 2610, Belgium, Antwerpen, Belgium; 3Biomedical Research Institute, UHasselt, Diepenbeek, Belgium, Hasselt, Belgium

Introduction: Introduction: In multiple sclerosis (MS), migration of autoreactive T lymphocytes across the leaky and inflamed blood‐brain barrier (BBB) into the CNS is a key initiation event of demyelination and neurodegeneration. However, it remains unclear whether and how certain extracellular vesicle (EV)‐subpopulations contribute to blood‐brain barrier dysfunction in the early stages of MS. To this end, we aimed to unravel the content of BBB endothelial cell (EC)‐ derived EV subpopulations and their function in the breaching of the BBB.

Methods: Methods: Following MISEV2018, TEM, NTA, and western blot were used to characterize the purified inflammation‐associated BBB‐EV subpopulations from TNF‐α/ IFN‐γ treated BBB‐EC (hCMEC/D3 cells) based on their sedimentation speeds (2K, 10K, and 100K). Protein arrays were used to discover the immunomodulatory content of subpopulations and their target cells. In addition, the functional impact of EV‐size‐based subpopulations on the integrity of BBB and the loss of barrier properties were assessed using TEER, qPCR, and western blotting.

Results: Results: We demonstrated that BBB‐ECs, upon inflammation, release two distinct populations of heterogeneous EV, with a tendency towards higher secretion of small EV. Moreover, the expression pattern of cytokines and adhesion markers were significantly different in BBB‐EV subsets, which are biologically packed with key chemoattractant cytokines mediating T cell migration. In addition, BBB‐EV subpopulations (particularly large EV) can cause opening of the BBB and loss of barrier properties through affecting the expression of the junctional molecules Claudin‐5 and ZO‐1.

Summary/Conclusion: Conclusion: Further research into the underlying mechanisms of BBB‐EV in changing the paracellular permeability of BBB‐ ECs might unravel novel therapeutic targets in MS.

Funding: This work was co‐financed by the Belgian Charcot Foundation, Fonds voor Wetenschappelijk Onderzoek (FWO), Stichting MS Research, Multiple Sclerosis International Federation (MSIF), MoveS

Keywords: multiple sclerosis, blood‐brain barrier dysfunction, Inflammation, extracellular vesicle subpopulations

## OT09: Various Approaches for Therapeutic Applications of EVs

## Chair: Yanis Mouloud, n/a – Institute for Transfusion Medicine, University Hospital Essen, University of Duisburg‐Essen, Essen, Germany

## Chair: Soazig Le Lay, n/a – Inserm U1087 Institut du Thorax Nantes & SFR ICAT Univ Angers

OT09.04.

## Fibroblast Growth Factor‐2 bound to specific dermal fibroblasts‐derived extracellular vesicles is protected from degradation: relevance to skin wound healing

Isabelle Petit
1; Ayelet Levy2; Soline Estrach3; Chloé Féral4; Andrea Gonçalves Trentin5; Florent Dingli6; Damarys Loew6; Jieqiong Qu7; Huiqing Zhou7; Clotilde Théry8; Céline Prunier1; Daniel Aberdam9; Olivier Ferrigno1

1Sorbonne university, INSERM U938, CRSA, Paris, France; 2INSERM U976, Paris, France; 3INSERM, Nice, France; 4Université Côte d'Azur, INSERM, CNRS, IRCAN, Faculté de Médecine, Nice, France; 5Federal University of Santa Catarina, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil; 6Institut Curie, PSL Research University, LSMP ‐ Curie CoreTech Mass Spectrometry Proteomics, Paris, France; 7Radboud University, Nijmegen, Netherlands; 8Institut Curie, PSL Research University, INSERM U932, Paris, France; 9INSERM U1138, Centre des Cordeliers, Paris, France

Introduction: Fibroblast growth factor‐2 (FGF2) plays key roles in cutaneous tissue repair but its natural low stability prevents the development of its use in therapeutic for impaired skin repair (diabetic limb ulcers, burned epidermis). In light of the emerging roles of extracellular vesicles (EVs) in the secretion and transport of cytokines, and the importance of FGF2 in skin repair, we investigated the interplay between EVs and FGF2.

Methods: EVs were isolated by classical differential centrifugation from human dermal fibroblasts (DF) cultured in absence (CTL‐EVs) or presence of FGF2 (FGF2‐EVs) to mimic fibroblast activation during skin wound repair. Size‐exclusion columns were used to further purify EVs from soluble proteins. EVs were analyzed by western blot, ELISA and cytometry and tested in proliferation and migration assays. Immunoprecipitation with CD63 or CD81‐coated beads was performed to isolate EV populations. RNA‐seq analysis on EV‐treated fibroblasts and proteomic profile of EVs were also performed.

Results: We found that EVs isolated from DF cultured in presence of FGF2 harbor FGF2 at their outer surface. Moreover, FGF2 can bind purified EVs in absence of cells. Remarkably, FGF2 binding to EVs is restricted to a specific subpopulation of EVs, which do not express CD63 and CD81 markers. Treatment of DF with FGF2‐EVs activated AKT and ERK signaling pathways and increased in vitro cell proliferation and migration. Local injection of FGF2‐EVs improved cutaneous wound healing in mice. We further demonstrated that binding to EVs protects FGF2 from both thermal and proteolytic degradation, thus maintaining FGF2 function.

Summary/Conclusion: These results reveal a novel aspect of EV function suggesting that a subpopulation of dermal fibroblast‐derived EVs can protect FGF2 from degradation thus increasing its stability. They also suggest EVs as potential tool for delivering FGF2 in skin healing therapies.

Funding: This work was supported by grants from the French Agency for Research "Oh Risque" (iTraNS), FRM, “Région Ile‐de‐France” and SILAB industry.

Keywords: skin, FGF2, wound healing, extracellular vesicles

OT09.05.

## Targeted Therapy by Antibody Displaying Extracellular Vesicles

Oscar P.B Wiklander
1; Doste Mamand2; Rim Jawad Wiklander1; Angus Lennaard2; Heena Sharma3; Dhanu Gupta2; Xiuming Liang4; Jeremy Bost2; Antje Zickler1; Wenyi Zheng4; Joel Nordin2; André Görgens1; Samir El Andaloussi4

1Department of Laboratory Medicine, Karolinska Institutet, Stockholm, Sweden, Stockholm, Sweden; 2Department of laboratory medicine, Karolinska Institutet, Stockholm, Sweden; 3Evox Therapeutics Limited, Oxford Science Park, Oxford, UK., Oxford, United Kingdom; 4Department of Laboratory Medicine, Karolinska Institutet, Stockholm, Sweden, Huddinge, Sweden

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have emerged as important mediators of biological signals between cells that regulate several important biological processes. EVs can be engineered to display targeting and therapeutic moieties and can shuttle cargo over biological barriers. EVs can thus act as natural vectors and offers a unique platform for a new class of therapeutics. Here, we introduce the use of antibody (mAb) decorated therapeutic EVs (Fc‐EVs).

Methods: Following optimization by a systematic comparison of different Fc‐binding moieties and EV sorting domains, the broad potential of this platform is shown by mAb guided targeting in vitro and in vivo with focus on using the Fc‐EVs platform as a targeted cancer therapy.

Results: We show that this approach renders highly efficient targeting of EVs to HER2‐positive breast cancer cells and to malignant melanoma cells by the display of HER2‐ and PDL1‐mAb, respectively. As proof of concept, Fc‐EVs were loaded with the chemotherapeutic drug doxorubicin and decorated with the immune checkpoint inhibitor PD‐L1‐Ab to treat malignant melanoma in vivo. The targeting approach with tumor‐specific mAb allows for a dual therapeutic role as it 1) directs the doxorubicin loaded EVs to the tumor and 2) the PD‐L1‐Ab itself function as a therapeutic intervention by blocking the immunosuppressive PD1/PD‐L1 axis. In fact, Fc‐EVs displaying PDL1‐Ab significantly accumulated in tumor tissue and, when loaded with doxorubicin, resulted in a significant lower tumor burden, and a pronounced increased survival of tumor bearing mice.

Summary/Conclusion: Here, we take advantage of the ability of EVs as a delivery vector and decorate them with mAb by equipping the EVs with an mAb binding moiety. We here show efficient tumor targeting and treatment in vitro and in vivo. Considering that these Fc‐EVs can be decorated with different types of mAb, they have the potential to targeted virtually any tissue of interest.

Funding: Center for Medical Innovation (CIMED, FoUI‐963452), KI Research Foundation Grant.

Keywords: Exosomes, Extracellular vesicles, EVs, HER2, PD‐L1, PD1, melanoma, Targeting, cancer, immunotherapy

## Friday 27 May 2022

## OF13: Neuronal and Neurodegenerative Diseases

## Chair: Andrew F. Hill, PhD, DVC Research and Impact, Victoria University

## Chair: Graça Raposo, Institut Curie UMR144, PSL Research University

OF13.03.

## Helicobactor pylori‐derived outer membrane vesicles contribute to Alzheimer's disease pathogenesis via C3‐C3aR signaling
Junhua Xie1; Freddy Haesebrouck2; Lien Van Hoecke1; Roosmarijn E. Vandenbroucke
1

1Ghent University and VIB, Zwijnaarde, Belgium; 2Ghent University, Zwijnaarde, Belgium

Introduction: The gut microbiota represents a diverse and dynamic population of microorganisms that can influence the health of the host. Increasing evidence supports the role of the gut microbiota as a key player in the pathogenesis of neurodegenerative diseases, including Alzheimer's disease (AD). Unfortunately, the mechanisms behind the interplay between gut pathogens and AD is still elusive. It is known that bacteria‐derived outer membrane vesicles (OMVs) act as natural carriers of virulence factors that are central players in the pathogenesis of the bacteria. Helicobacter pylori (H. pylori) is a common gastric pathogen and H. pylori infection has been shown to increase the risk of AD.

Methods: Using Cre loading, TdT reporter mice and confocal imaging, we studied whether orally gavaged, Cre recombinase loaded, H. pylori‐derived OMVs reach the brain and specific brain cells. AppNL‐G‐F mice Alzheimer's disease mice were used to study the impact of the OMVs on disease progression, including amyloid beta (Aβ) analysis, via staining and ELISA, and cognitive functioning, using the novel object recognition test. Microglial activation and complement component 3 (C3)‐C3a receptor (C3aR) signaling was studied via imaging and inhibitor studies. Synaptic function was assessed via immunostainings and long‐term potentiation (LTP) measurements on brain slices.

Results: We show that H. pylori OMVs reach the brain, induce activation of glial cells and neuronal dysfunction, ultimately leading to exacerbated amyloid beta (Aβ) pathology and cognitive decline. Mechanistically, we identified a critical role for the C3/C3aR signaling in mediating the interactions between astrocytes, microglia and neurons upon the presence of gut H. pylori OMVs.

Summary/Conclusion: Taken together, our study reveals that H. pylori‐derived OMVs have a detrimental effect on brain functionality and accelerate AD development via C3‐C3aR signaling.

Keywords: Helicobacter pylori, OMVs, Alzheimer's disease, complement

## OF14: Novel Approaches in Studying EV Nucleic Acids

## Chair: Metka Lenassi, PhD, Faculty of Medicine, University of Ljubljana, Ljubljana, Slovenia

## Chair: Dr. Esther N.M Nolte‐'t Hoen, Division of Cell Biology, Metabolism & Cancer, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University

OF14.03.

## RNA editing‐based assay for precise evaluation of EV‐mediated mRNA transfer

Masaharu Somiya
1; Shun'ichi Kuroda2

1Osaka University, Seattle, USA; 2SANKEN, Osaka University, Ibaraki, Japan

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are expected to deliver various species of RNA including mRNA. EV‐mediated mRNA delivery has been usually evaluated using reporter assays which use luminescence or fluorescence proteins as a reporter. In the reporter assay, if EVs deliver mRNA into recipient cells the translation and expression of the reporter gene can be detected. However, conventional mRNA reporter assay can easily overestimate the efficiency of mRNA transfer because of the “pseudo‐transfer” of reporter proteins from donor cells to recipient cells. In this study, we developed the mRNA delivery assay which uses RNA editing to eliminate the contamination of reporter proteins in the EV samples from donor cells.

Methods: Human embryonic kidney HEK293T cells were transfected to express reporter gene (NanoLuc) and EVs were isolated from the culture supernatant by ultracentrifugation. Raw supernatant or isolated EVs were applied to the recipient HEK293T cells and the NanoLuc activity in the recipient cells was measured after 24 hours. For the RNA editing‐based assay, recipient cells were transfected to express an RNA editing enzyme (dPspCas13b‐hADDR2), single guide RNA, and siRNA before the treatment with supernatant or EVs.

Results: We confirmed that the supernatant and isolated EVs from donor cells expressing NanoLuc contained substantial amounts of NanoLuc proteins, and the contaminated proteins can be transferred to the recipient cells regardless of the functional mRNA delivery. To avoid the contamination of NanoLuc protein in EV samples, the 12th Trp codon (UGG) of NanoLuc was replaced to stop codon (UAG), so that donor cells only express reporter non‐functional mRNA but proteins. When the NanoLuc (W12stop) mRNA is delivered to the recipient cells that express RNA editing enzyme, the stop codon was converted to the original UGG codon by A‐to‐I(G) editing and functional NanoLuc expression can be expressed. This system dramatically decreased the background signal derived from contaminating NanoLuc proteins over 100‐fold to a negligible level. By using this assay, even in the presence of a known delivery enhancer of EVs, a virus‐derived glycoprotein VSV‐G, we did not observe evidence of EV‐mediated functional mRNA delivery into recipient cells.

Summary/Conclusion: RNA editing‐based assay developed in this study would be a convenient way to verify the EV‐mediated mRNA delivery in virtually any cell line.

Funding: This study was supported by JSPS KAKENHI (18K18386 and 20K15790) and “Dynamic Alliance for Open Innovation Bridging Human, Environment and Materials” (MEXT).

Keywords: mRNA delivery; RNA editing; cargo delivery

OF14.06.

## Studying small extracellular vesicles DNA function and packaging mechanisms

Venkatesh Kumar Chetty
1; Jamal Ghanam2; Dirk Reinhardt2; Basant Kumar K. Thakur2

1University Hospital Essen, Essen, Germany; 2Universitätsklinikum Essen, Essen, Germany

Introduction: Although DNA has been associated with small EVs (EV‐DNA), little is known about the mechanisms of EV‐DNA packaging and their biological function. These studies are hampered due to the lack of an efficient EV isolation method allowing separation of small EVs from free‐circulating DNA and apoptotic bodies, and a reliable tool for accurate EV‐DNA tracking and quantitation. We aimed to develop a monitoring and analysis tool to understand the mechanism(s) of EV‐DNA packaging and its downstream functions.

Methods: To isolate sEVs devoid of free‐circulated components, we combined Tangential flow filtration, Size exclusion chromatography, and Ultrafiltration (TSU). TSU‐sEVs were characterized according to MISEV2018 guidelines. EdU was utilized to label EV‐DNA cargo released from host cells; thereby, EV‐DNA function can be evaluated in the recipient cells. Furthermore, we implemented 3D imaging tools for accurate EV‐DNA quantitation by avoiding artifacts generated from 2D microscopy. Next, HEK293T‐TSU‐sEVs were enriched after knocking down 30 genes related to tumor suppression, DNA repair, and hypoxia to study the possible mechanisms of EV‐DNA packaging.

Results: Our results showed that the TSU method provides sEVs free from apoptotic bodies and cell‐free DNA with high yield and purity. Next, we revealed that DNA‐associated with sEVs is double‐stranded, predominantly linked with histones, and localized inside the vesicles. Using 3D analysis tool, we found the distribution of EV‐DNA localized in various recipient cell compartments overcoming the cell membrane barrier. This analysis tool can be implemented to understand the exact EV‐DNA function in cancer.
Analysis of enriched HEK293T‐TSU‐sEVs after the knockdown showed that EV‐DNA yield is increased significantly after deactivating hypoxia‐related genes like VHL, VEGFA, and VEGFB. To further examine the role of hypoxia on EV‐DNA release and packaging, we isolated sEVs under hypoxic conditions and determined that the EV‐DNA yield of hypoxic sEVs was significantly higher than normoxic sEVs revealing the hypoxia involvement in EV‐DNA packaging.

Summary/Conclusion: Altogether, we demonstrated an efficient sEV isolation method with new tools to understand EV‐DNA function. Furthermore, we determined the role of hypoxia in regulating EV‐DNA packaging.

Funding: This research was funded by the Stiftung Universitätsmedizin Essen; Deutsche Forschungsge‐meinschaft, DFG TH2012/1‐1 and Deutsche Kinderkrebsstiftung, DKS 2018.17.

## OF15: Metabolism Obesity Nutrition

## Chair: Soazig Le Lay, n/a – Inserm U1087 Institut du Thorax Nantes & SFR ICAT Univ Angers

## Chair: Olivier P. BLANC‐BRUDE, PhD – Paris Center for Cardiovascular Research, Institut National de la Santé et de la Recherche Médicale U970, Université de Paris Cité, France

OF15.05.

## Reprogramming of endothelial cell metabolism by cancer cell derived extracellular vesicles

Joël E.J Beaumont
1; Lydie Barbeau2; Kim Savelkouls2; Kim Kampen2; Marijke I. Zonneveld2; Tom G.H Keulers3; Kasper M.A. Rouschop2

1Department of Radiotherapy, GROW‐School for Oncology and Reproduction, Maastricht University, Stein, Netherlands; 2Department of Radiotherapy, GROW‐School for Oncology and Reproduction, Maastricht University, Maastricht, Netherlands; 3Department of Radiotherapy, GROW‐School for Oncology and Reproduction, Maastricht University, maastricht, Netherlands

Introduction: Tumor cells manipulate their surroundings via the release of EV to provide a growth supportive tumor microenvironment (TME), e.g. by stimulating angiogenesis to increase delivery of nutrients/oxygen. Activation of angiogenesis is associated with a metabolic switch in endothelial cells (EC), yet mechanisms driving this switch are relatively unknown. Hypoxia in solid tumors is associated with an aggressive disease. It induces angiogenesis and alters the molecular EV cargo. Here, we investigate the effects of (hypoxic) EV on the metabolic reprogramming of the TME.

Methods: Colorectal cancer‐ (HT29), glioblastoma‐ (U87), and breast cancer‐ (MDA‐MB‐231) cells were cultured under normoxia, moderate hypoxia (0.2% O2) or severe hypoxia (< 0.02% O2) for 24h with 5% EV depleted serum. EV were isolated by size exclusion chromatography. EV uptake was analysed by FACS and confocal imaging using CFSE labelling. Glucose uptake and lactate secretion were measured after 24h EV stimulation. For metabolomics, EC were stimulated for 48h, supplemented with 13C‐labeled glucose. De novo protein synthesis was assessed by click‐it chemistry.

Results: HT29, U87 and MDA derived EV increase glucose uptake in EC, cancer cells, monocytes and fibroblasts without increasing lactate production. The magnitude of this effect is cell type dependent. EV uptake analysis showed that all EV types are taken up by EC. Interestingly, HT29/U87 EV and MDA EV show clear differences in staining pattern, suggesting differences in uptake mechanism. Metabolic analysis of EC shows increased synthesis and uptake of amino acids (AA) after stimulation with HT29 and MDA, derived EV. Despite this increase, intracellular AA levels remain unaltered suggesting increased usage. In line, EV stimulated EC demonstrate increased de novo protein synthesis.

Summary/Conclusion: Depending on the specific needs of a tumor and its environmental conditions (oxygenation), cancer cells manipulate the metabolism of TME cells to support tumor growth.

Funding: Zeldzame Ziekten Fonds
KWF Kankerbestrijding

## Saturday 28 May 2022

## OS28: Late‐Breaking Session: EV Methodology Advances

## Chair: Tanina Arab, PhD – Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine

## Chair: David Juncker, MDPhD – Department of Biomedical Engineering, Faculty of Medicine and Health Sciences, McGill University

OS28.02.

## Assessing lipophilic dye promiscuity in staining extracellular vesicles and lipoprotein particles

Olesia Gololobova
1; Wyatt Vreeland2; Kenneth W. Witwer1

1Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Baltimore, USA; 2NIST, Gaithersburg, USA

Introduction: Extracellular vesicle (EV) uptake and biodistribution studies have commonly used fluorescent lipid dyes for EV labeling and tracking. However, confounders to the interpretation of lipid dye studies include the presence of free fluorescent particles, dissociation of fluorescence tag from the extracellular vesicles, and possible exchange of dye between extracellular vesicles and co‐isolated particles such as lipoproteins and protein aggregates. In this study, we evaluated EV and lipoprotein labeling by several lipid dyes. We also tested signal release and mixing.

Methods: ACD‐anticoagulated human plasma was processed per ISTH/ISEV recommendations to remove cells, then platelets. HDL and LDL were prepared from 1 mL of human plasma using LDL/VLDL and HDL Purification Kits (Cell BioLabs). EVs were also prepared from Expi293F cell culture (Thermo Fisher) by differential ultracentrifugation, size exclusion chromatography with qEV10 70nm SEC columns (Izon), and Amicon 15 Ultra 100 kD filters. LDL and HDL were characterized by asymmetric flow field‐flow fractionation (AF4) and transmission electron microscopy. Lipoproteins and EVs were characterized by Western blot for the presence of ApoB100 (LDL), ApoAI (HDL), and CD9/CD81 (EVs). All particles were labeled with PKH67, MemGlow 488 or 560, DiO, and Exoria. We attempted to remove unincorporated dye using Sephadex G‐25 columns. Signals were read by a fluorescence plate reader and single‐particle tetraspanins assays (SP‐IRIS: ExoView).

Results: 40‐45% of particles were labeled by PKH67, DiO, and Exoria, and up to 80% by MemGlow and the majority of LDL and HDL were also labeled. In up to 72 hours of storage at 4C, the signal release was not observed from lipoprotein particles or EVs. After mixing two populations of EVs stained with dyes with different excitation wavelengths, SP‐IRIS detected a small population of events with dual signals.

Summary/Conclusion: Our results suggest that strict purification of EVs after labeling with lipophilic dyes is required before mixing with other particles, especially in biodistribution studies, where unincorporated dye may label lipoprotein particles and possibly other entities. Dye transfer between particles is also possible, although our current results cannot distinguish between association, fusion, and transfer. Additional investigations of incubation temperature, time, and orthogonal labeling are warranted.

Keywords: EV labeling, Lipophilic dyes, Biodistribution, Lipoproteins

OS28.03.

## Capture and detection of extracellular microvesicles based on coordination complexes/phosphatidylserine interactions
Phoulinh chantavong1; Jérôme Dejeu1; Aurore Thibon‐Pourret1; Hugues Bonnet1; Catherine Belle2; Angéline VAN DER HEYDEN
3

1University Grenoble alpes, Grenoble, France; 2Centre national de la recherche scientifique, Grenoble, France; 3Université Grenoble Alpes, GRENOBLE Cedex 9, France

Introduction: Extracellular Vesicles (EVs) are currently the subject of intense investigations because of their high potential for diagnostic and therapeutic applications. Among these EVs, microvesicles (MVs) are of particular interest in the early diagnosis of cardiovascular diseases. While targeting of externalized phosphatidylserine (PS) by Annexin V is a common approach to analyse MVs, this approach suffers of several drawbacks, especially Ca2+ dependence. In this context, we propose the use of coordination complexes as new chemosensors for MVs capture.

Methods: A set of bimetallic complexes based on a ligand with dipicolylamine arms linked by a phenoxo spacer has been synthesized. The recognition between complexes and PS was studied using phospholipids under different types of organizations: monomers using short‐chain phospholipids, model vesicles formed by extrusion and MVs. MVs were isolated from human plasma by differential centrifugation and characterized by DLS, TRPS and prothrombinase assay. Interaction in solution with monomers were studied by 31P NMR. After immobilization of the complexes on solid surfaces, interactions were studied by Surface Plasmon Resonance (SPR).

Results: Specific recognition of PS monomers by the coordination complexes was demonstrated by 31P NMR and SPR. After optimisations of the sensing surface, SPR was used to evaluate the capture of model vesicles of defined size and PS compositions. Distinction of the interactions between model vesicles containing or not PS was demonstrated. Finally, MVs capture by the bimetallic complexes grafted on the surface was also evidenced.

Summary/Conclusion: The coordination complexes have demonstrated their capacity to recognise PS presented as monomers or entrapped on lipid bilayers. These recognition properties were successfully used for MVs capture and detection.

Funding: This work was supported by the French research agency (ANR‐16‐CE29‐0009‐01) including PhD fellowship for P. Chantavong.

Keywords: coordination complexes phosphatidylserine recognition microvesicles

OS28.04.

## Differences in tetraspanin distribution on EVs from paired human milk and serum samples identified by multiplexed flow cytometry
Alberta Giovanazzi1; Marca H.M Wauben
2; Martijn van Herwijnen3

1Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, Netherlands; 2Division of Cell Biology, Metabolism & Cancer, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands; 3Utrecht University, Utrecht, Netherlands

Introduction: EV surface protein signatures are related to their cell of origin and biogenesis route. In recent years, it became clear that tetraspanins (e.g. CD9, CD63 and CD81) heterogeneously distribute on EVs from diverse cellular sources and intracellular origin. In this study, we assessed the distribution of tetraspanins and specific surface proteins on EVs isolated from milk and serum obtained from the same donor.

Methods: EVs were isolated from nine paired human milk and serum samples by differential centrifugation, density gradient and size exclusion chromatography. After NTA quantification, 5*10⁸ EVs were subjected to MACSPlex Exosome multiplex platform analysis and simultaneous capture of EVs on 37 antibody‐coated bead populations was detected using either a cocktail of CD9, CD63, and CD81 antibodies or the individual tetraspanin antibodies. Protein signals with a mean fluorescence intensity (MFI) > MFI of isotype controls were considered positive.

Results: The proteins identified on milk EVs only (i.e. EPCAM, MCAM, CD14, CD3) are mainly co‐expressed with CD81, while proteins unique for serum EVs (i.e. CD31, CD40, CD41b, CD42a, CD62P) are exclusively co‐expressed with CD9 and CD63. In general, MFI signals using the CD81 detection antibody, but not the CD9 or CD63 antibody, was significantly higher on EVs from milk compared to serum.
Irrespective of the biofluid analysed, CD44 is co‐expressed exclusively with CD81, while CD63 is absent on HLA‐I expressing EVs. CD105 MFI was comparable on milk and serum EVs when detected with the tetraspanin antibody cocktail, however in serum EVs CD105 is co‐expressed with CD63 and CD81, while in milk EVs CD105 is co‐expressed with CD9 and CD81. Conversely, for HLA‐II expressing EVs, which overall signal was higher in milk EVs compared to serum EVs, a similar distribution pattern on single tetraspanins was observed between the two biofluids.

Summary/Conclusion: In conclusion, the tetraspanin distribution and specific tetraspanin‐protein co‐expression varies on EVs purified from paired milk and serum samples. This information can be used to define specific EV surface protein profiles for biomarker discovery.

Funding: This research is conducted under TRAIN‐EV project, funded by EU Horizon2020 Marie Sklodowska Curie Innovative Training Network (MSCA‐ITN) programme (grant agreement No 722148).

Keywords: Tetraspanins, multiplexed analysis, milk EVs, serum EVs, biomarkers, surface protein profiling

OS28.05.

## Make do and mend ‐ Free Flow Electrophoresis (FFE) as a novel approach for isolating extracellular vesicles

Christian Preußer
1; Tobias Tertel2; Gerhard Weber3; Johannes Graumann4; Bernd Giebel2; Elke Pogge von Strandmann5

1Institute for Tumor Immunology, EV Core Facility, Center for Tumor Biology and Immunology (ZTI), Philipps University of Marburg, Marburg, Germany, Marburg, Germany; 2Institute for Transfusion Medicine, University Hospital Essen, University of Duisburg‐Essen, Essen, Germany, Essen, Germany; 3FFE Service GmbH, Feldkirchen, Germany; 4Institute of Translational Proteomics, Biochemical/Pharmacological Center, Philipps University Marburg, Marburg, Germany; 5Institute for Tumor Immunology, Center for Tumor Biology and Immunology (ZTI), Philipps University of Marburg, Marburg, Germany, Marburg, Germany

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are ubiquitous particles released by all cells. Although EVs are well‐known, the isolation of EVs, especially from primary biofluids remains challenging. Most isolation methods rely on the biophysical properties of EVs, such as size or density. However, a balance must be found between quality and quantity, and the functional integrity of EVs is not always preserved by all methods. In addition, the specific isolation of various subpopulations is a major task.

Methods: To isolate EVs in high purity from the peritoneal fluid (ascites), of ovarian cancer patients, the ascites was pre‐cleared by a centrifugation step followed by free‐flow electrophoresis (FFE). This method represents an established, matrix‐free approach for separating different analytes with inherent differences in charge density and/or pI value. Here we applied a protocol using a series of buffer media with different pH values which allowed us to collect 96 fractions. To characterize the composition of the individual samples in more detail, we have used different state‐of‐the‐art methods such as nano‐flow and imaging flow cytometry in addition to classical approaches.

Results: We have successfully applied the FFE approach for the isolation of EVs from ascites. We could show that the tetraspanin‐positive events were mainly found to be enriched in a few distinct fractions. This observation is further supported by the analysis of the respective fractions using nano‐flow cytometry, western blot analysis, and electron microscopy. A detailed analysis of the EV‐associated proteins by mass spectrometry will be discussed.

Summary/Conclusion: Here we described a novel approach for isolating EVs from ascites. This methodical approach applies to other biofluids such as plasma, thereby representing a new efficient and rapid way of isolating native and high‐purity EVs from intricate primary samples.

Funding: This work was supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (GRK 2573/1)

Keywords: free‐flow electrophoresis, native EV isolation

OS28.06.

## Accurate differentiation of extracellular vesicles and lipoproteins by interferometric nanoparticle tracking analysis

Anna Kashkanova
1; Jan Van Deun2; Martin Blessing1; Marie Reischke1; Vahid Sandoghdar1; Andreas Baur2

1Max Planck Institute for the Science of Light, Erlangen, Germany; 2Universitätsklinikum Erlangen, Erlangen, Germany

Introduction: Pathological conditions often lead to a dysregulation of the circulating extracellular vesicle (EV) population, including in number and size. While nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) is widely used for EV measurement, it is biased towards larger sizes and cannot distinguish between similarly‐sized particles of different origin. This is especially problematic for plasma‐derived EVs, as lipoproteins outnumber EVs by several orders of magnitude and often contaminate EV preps. We developed a novel technique called interferometric NTA (iNTA) and show that it can accurately distinguish EVs from lipoproteins across a wide size range.

Methods: In iNTA, the light back‐scattered by a particle is interfered with light reflected by the coverglass, leading to enhanced sensitivity for small particle sizes. Recording trajectories at high frame rates (5 kHz) and measuring the amount of back‐scattered light allows for accurate estimation of both size and refractive index (RI) for each individual particle. iNTA was compared to classical NTA by using artificial mixtures of density gradient‐purified cell culture EVs (RI < 1.4) and VLDLs (RI>1.4) as well as plasma samples, purified by both size‐exclusion (SEC) and dual mode chromatography (DMC). ApoB100 and CD63 ELISA were used to quantify levels of VLDL and EV, respectively.

Results: Using mixtures of purified EVs and VLDLs, we found that iNTA can accurately measure their relative concentrations, as opposed to NTA. We show that iNTA can clearly measure an increase in lipoproteins in chromatography‐purified plasma samples of healthy volunteers after a lipid‐reach meal. EVs purified by DMC, which is designed to remove VLDLs, resulted in significantly lower lipoprotein numbers than the same samples processed by SEC. Purified EVs spiked into these samples were accurately measured by iNTA only. In addition, we demonstrated that iNTA is applicable to the whole plasma.

Summary/Conclusion: iNTA can accurately quantify bona fide EVs in blood plasma‐derived samples of varying complexity. The ease of implementation and measurement could make it a tool in any EV lab.

Funding: AK acknowledges support from Christiane Nüsslein‐Volhard‐Stiftung.

Keywords: NTA, Size, Refractive index

## OS31: Late‐Breaking Session: Inflammation and immune cells

## Chair: Hernando A del Portillo – ISGlobal Institute for Global Health, Hospital Clínic ‐ Universitat de Barcelona, Barcelona, and Institut d'Investigació Germans Trias i Pujol (IGTP), Badalona/Barcelona, Spain, and Institució Catalana de Recerca i Estudis Avançats (ICREA), Barcelo

## A Protein Corona on Human Platelet‐Derived Extracellular Vesicles can Promote Regenerative Functions
Fausto Gueths Gomes1; André Cronemberger Andrade
2; Martin Wolf2; Sarah Hochmann2; Linda Krisch2; Christof Regl3; Nicole Maeding2; Rodolphe W. Poupardin2; Patricia Ebner‐Peking2; Christian G Huber4; Nicole Meisner‐Kober3; Katharina Schllmoser5; Dirk Strunk2

1Department of Transfusion Medicine, Paracelsus Medical University, Salzburg, Austria, Salzburg, Austria; 2Cell Therapy Institute, Spinal Cord Injury and Tissue Regeneration Center Salzburg (SCI ‐ TReCS), Paracelsus Medical University (PMU), Salzburg, Austria, Salzburg, Austria; 3Department for Biosciences, Paris Lodron University Salzburg, Austria, Salzburg, Austria; 4Dept. of Biosciences, Paris Lodron University Salzburg, Salzburg, Austria; 5Department of Transfusion Medicine and SCI‐TReCS, PMU, Salzburg, Austria, Salzburg, Austria

Introduction: Human‐platelet lysate (HPL) is gaining attention as a raw material for regenerative medicine. HPL is rich in growth factors and known to promote cell growth more efficiently than fetal bovine serum in vitro. We separated extracellular vesicles (EVs) from soluble factors of HPL to understand the mode of action during skin organoid formation and immunomodulation as model systems for tissue regeneration.

Methods: EVs were isolated from HPL supernatant concentrated by tangential‐flow filtration (TFF‐EVs) and further purified by size‐exclusion chromatography (TSEC‐EVs) separating EVs from (lipo) protein‐enriched soluble fractions (TSEC‐sol.F.). Samples were characterized by tunable resistive pulse sensing, western blot, tandem mass‐tag proteomics, and super‐resolution microscopy, and functionally tested during organoid formation and immunomodulation.

Results: We identified three major protein clusters by proteomics separating TSEC‐EVs from HPL clustering with TFF‐sol.F. and TFF‐EVs clustering with TSEC sol.F. Functional evaluation of HPL‐derived TFF‐EVs showed significantly improved skin‐organoid formation and inhibition of T‐cell proliferation compared to TSEC‐EVs lacking the corona or to TSEC‐sol.F. Reconstituting the corona on TSEC‐EVs with TSEC soluble fractions re‐established a functionality comparable to TFF‐EV. Zeta potential and super‐resolution imaging confirmed corona formation. Thus, corona depletion on SEC‐EVs could be artificially reconstituted by TSEC late fractions in add‐back experiments showing a similar effect compared to TFF‐EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: HPL‐derived TFF‐EVs are highly efficient in promoting skin organoid formation and inhibiting T‐cell proliferation. TFF is permissive technology enabling scalable enrichment and separation functional EVs and soluble factors. Mechanistically, depletion of the TFF‐EV protein corona by SEC or ultracentrifugation abrogated functionality indicating a novel mode of action. This also opens the possibility to engineer EVs with selected proteins for specific therapeutic applications.

Funding: The authors were supported by funding from the European Union's Horizon 2020, EV‐TT‐Bpro, Cancer Cluster II, EV‐Quant, STEBS, and EV‐TT.

Keywords: human platelet lysate (HPL); extracellular vesicles (EVs); EV corona; skin regeneration; immune modulation; angiogenesis

## Blood cell‐derived extracellular vesicles induce a pro‐inflammatory phenotype in tubular epithelial cells of proliferative Lupus Nephritis patients

S. Rubina Baglio
1; Michel Tsang‐A‐Sjoe2; M.A.J. van Eijndhoven3; Leontien Bosch3; Niala Masoumi3; Joris J. Roelofs4; Ekaterina S. Jordanova5; Kirstin M. Heutinck6; Kenny de Wildt3; Nils J. Groenewegen3; R.J.M. ten Berge6; Raymond M. Schiffelers7; J.R.T. van Weering8; Sandra A. Verkuijlen3; I.E.M. Bultink2; Yongsoo Kim3; Jaap M. Middeldorp3; Alexandre E. Voskuyl2; Dirk M. Pegtel3

1Department of Pathology, Cancer Center Amsterdam, Amsterdam UMC location Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, amsterdam, Netherlands; 2Department of Rheumatology and Clinical Immunology, Amsterdam UMC location Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 3Department of Pathology, Cancer Center Amsterdam, Amsterdam UMC location Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 4Department of Pathology, Amsterdam UMC location AMC, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 5Center for Gynaecologic Oncology Amsterdam, Department of Gynaecology, Amsterdam UMC location Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 6Department of Experimental Immunology, Amsterdam UMC location AMC, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 7University Medical Center Utrecht, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands; 8Department of Functional Genomics, Center for Neurogenomics and Cognitive Research, Neuroscience Campus Amsterdam, VU University, Amsterdam, Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Introduction: Proliferative lupus nephritis (LN) is a severe condition causing impaired kidney function that occurs in 40‐60% of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) patients. LN is characterized by a marked interferon (IFN) response in tubular epithelial cells (TEC), but the biological trigger has remained unclear. We previously showed that extracellular vesicles (EVs) released by B cells are enriched in polymerase III (Pol III) transcripts of endogenous and viral (EBV) origin. These transcripts carry pathogen‐associated molecular patterns (PAMPs) that can be recognized by innate RNA sensors. We hypothesize that in LN, due to glomerular damage, these inflammatory EVs disproportionately reach the tubular epithelium inducing an IFN response that worsens the disease.

Methods: Gene set enrichment analysis was applied to identify key pathways and cell signatures in proliferative LN. RNA‐seq was used to define the pol‐III trascriptome of B cell EVs. In situ hybridization was employed to reveal the presence of EBV transcripts in human tissues. In vitro assays were used to unravel the mechanism of internalization and the functional effect of B cell EVs on TEC cells.

Results: scRNA‐seq analysis of proliferative LN identified enrichment of blood cell, including (EBV‐infected) B lymphocyte, transcripts in TEC cells. Notably, the same blood cell signatures were enriched in plasma EVs. SLE patient plasma and purified EVs contained the EBV‐encoded pol III‐transcript EBER1, which selectively accumulated in the cytoplasm of TEC from Lupus but not IgA nephritis biopsies. Functional assays showed uptake of B cell EVs by primary TECs in a phosphatidylserine (PtdSer) and KIM‐1‐dependent manner. Crucially, B cell EVs deliver EBERs into endosomes of primary TECs driving IFNb, TNFa and IL6 production through TLR3 activation.

Summary/Conclusion: In SLE patients, blood cell‐secreted, PtDSer+, inflammatory EVs target TEC cells via KIM‐1 inducing an IFN‐mediated inflammatory response.

OS31.03.

## Effects of extracellular vesicles derived from oral bacteria on osteoclast differentiation and activation

Hyun Young Kim
1; Min‐Kyoung Song2; Younggap Lim3; Ji Sun Jang4; Hong‐Hee Kim4; Bong‐Kyu Choi3

1Department of Oral Microbiology and Immunology, Dental Research Institute, School of Dentistry, Seoul National University, Seoul, Republic of Korea; 2Department of Internal Medicine, Seoul National University Hospital, Seoul, Republic of Korea, Seoul, Republic of Korea; 3Department of Oral Microbiology and Immunology, School of Dentistry, Seoul National University, Seoul, Republic of Korea., Seoul, Republic of Korea; 4Department of Cell and Developmental Biology, Dental Research Institute, School of Dentistry, Seoul National University, Seoul, Republic of Korea., Seoul, Republic of Korea

Introduction: Dysbiosis of oral microbiota is play an important role in progression of periodontitis which is characterized by chronic inflammation, alveolar bone loss and systemic diseases. Bacterial extracellular vesicles (EVs) contain various bioactive molecules and showed diverse effects on host environments depending on the bacterial species. Recently, we reported that EVs derived from Gram‐positive periodontal pathogen, Filifactor alocis have osteoclastogenic activity. In the present study, we determined the osteoclastogenic potency and immunostimulatory activity of EVs derived from Gram‐negative periodontal pathogens (Porphyromonas gingivalis and Tannerella forsythia), an oral commensal (Streptococcus oralis) and a gut probiotic strain (Lactobacillus reuteri).

Methods: Bacterial EVs were purified by density gradient ultracentrifugation using OptiPrep reagentTM. Morphology and size of bacterial EVs were analyzed by transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA), respectively. Osteoclast differentiation by bacterial EVs was determined by tartrate‐resistant acid phosphatase (TRAP) staining. Immunostimulatory activity of bacterial EVs was determined by cytokine array and enzyme‐linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA). Toll‐like receptor (TLR)‐activating ability of bacterial EVs was measured using CHO/CD14 reporter cells. Inactivation of bacterial lipoprotein and lipopolysaccharide (LPS) from EVs was performed using lipoprotein lipase and polymyxin B, respectively.

Results: EVs from P. gingivalis, T. forsythia, and S. oralis increased osteoclast differentiation and osteoclstogenic cytokine expression in osteoclast precursors, whereas EVs from L. reuteri did not. EVs from P. gingivalis, T. forsythia, and S. oralis preferentially activated TLR2 rather than TLR4 and induced osteoclsatogenesis mainly through TLR2. Osteoclastogenic effects of EVs from P. gingivalis and T. forsythia were reduced by both lipoprotein lipase and polymyxin B, while osteoclastogenic effects of EVs from S. oralis and F. alocis were reduced by lipoprotein lipase alone.

Summary/Conclusion: EVs from periodontal pathogens and oral commensal, but not probiotic strain, showed osteoclastogenic potency. TLR2 is major receptor for osteoclast differentiation by EVs from oral bacteria including periodontal pathogens.

Funding: This work was supported by grants from the National Research Foundation of Korea (NRF‐ 2021R1A2C1003952 and NRF‐2022R1C1C2007752).

Keywords: Bacterial extracellular vesicle, Periodontal pathogen, Osteoclast, Toll‐like receptor 2

## Heterogeneus Induction of Interferon Pathway in the Tumor Microenvironment by Different Populations of Cancer‐Derived Extracellular Vesicles

Krizia Sagini
1; Diana Kitka
2; Blandine F. Victor3; Minhyung Kim4; Sungyong You4; Fayyaz Sutterwala3; Catherine Grasso3; Caroline Jefferies5; Jlenia Guarnerio3; Paola de Candia6; Karen Cavassani3; Tatyana Vagner4; Dolores Di Vizio4

1Institute for Cancer Research, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway; 2cedars sinai medical center, Los Angeles, USA; 3cedars sinai medical center, los angeles, USA; 4Department of Surgery, Division of Cancer Biology and Therapeutics, Cedars‐Sinai Medical Center, Los Angeles, CA, USA, Los Angeles, USA; 5cedars‐sinai medical center, los angeles, USA; 6Dipartimento di Medicina Molecolare e Biotecnologie Mediche, Università Degli Studi di Napoli, Naples, Italy

Introduction: Cancer‐derived EVs come in different sizes that can be separated by differential centrifugation followed by density gradient resulting in different fractions containing different cargo. Along with nanovesicles, cancer cells shed abnormally large EVs (> 1 m in diameter) whose role in cancer metastasis has not been fully elucidated, since most of the attention has been placed to small EVs.

Methods: UC followed by iodixanol gradient centrifugation to purify the vesicles. TRPS, RNA‐seq, PCR flow cytometry, single cell RNA‐Seq

Results: We demonstrated by RNA‐Seq that bone stromal cells exhibit an anti‐viral, type I interferon, response when exposed to EVs from cancer cells. While protein amount was similar in the two EV fractions obtained from the same number of cells, the number of L‐EVs was 3 orders of magnitude smaller than the number of S‐EVs. Therefore, we dosed them for our experiments based on the number of donor cells. Both S‐ and L‐EVs induced a profound alteration of transcripts in stromal cells but the L‐EV elicited a significantly more remarkable response. Several of the mRNAs induced by L‐EVs codify for cytokines involved in tumor progression (CXCL1, CXCL10, CCL5, IL6). Additionally, neutrophils exposed to the conditioned medium of stromal cells treated with EVs exhibited increased expression of Arg1, Lox1, and IL10, which suggests a switch to an immune‐suppressive phenotype. The result was stronger for L‐EVs vs S‐EVs. These neutrophils also gained stronger migratory abilities toward the stroma when exposed to L‐EVs. We are now comparing the role of specific nucleic acids in L‐ and S‐EVs and of key nuclei acid sensor pathways (TLRs, RIG‐I, MDA5, cGAS/STING and AIM2) in mediating the interferon response induced by L‐ and S‐EVs to understand whether different EV populations have specific pathways of activation of the anti‐viral response.

Summary/Conclusion: Our results provide novel insight into how tumor derived EVs and in particular L‐EVs alter bone stroma cells by prompting a pro‐inflammatory type‐I interferon response and recruiting neutrophils having an immune‐suppressive phenotype, which may drive bone metastasis in prostate cancer.

Funding: Support: National Institutes of Health R01CA218526 (DDV); DoD PC150836 (DDV)

Keywords: large and small extracellular vesicles, EV heterogeneity, cancer progression

OS31.05.

## Morphine‐mediated neuroinflammaging: Role of Astrocyte‐derived extracellular vesicles

Shilpa Buch
1; Susmita Sil2; Divya Chemparthy3; Seema Singh3; Muthukumar Kannan3; Shannon Callen3

1University of Nebraska Medical Center, Omaha, USA; 2Department of Pharmacology & Experimental Neuroscience, Omaha, USA; 3UNMC, Omaha, USA

Introduction: Heroin abuse has been implicated in accelerated aging, specifically impacting age‐sensitive brain functional networks, in heroin addicts. Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are well‐recognized conduits for transferring cellular cargo across recipient neuronal cells, leading to neuronal synaptodendritic injury. While the direct role of morphine‐induced neuronal synaptodendritic injury has been well‐documented, whether glial‐neuronal interactions also play a role in neuronal senesenscenece remains an enigma. We thus sought to examine whether morphine‐stimulated astrocyte‐derived EVs (M‐ADEVs) could carry the SASP cargoes to recipient neurons and mediate synaptodendritic injury.

Methods: We have employed EV isolation by ultracentrifugation, neuronal injury by Neurolucida and behavior assessment by frailty assay, maze study & locomotor behavior.

Results: Morphine exposure induced senescence phenotype (upregulation of p16, p21, inflammatory cytokines, oxidative stress, β‐ gal activity and cell cyclce arrest) & inflammation in human primary astrocytes in vitro. Intriguingly, astrocytic SASP upregulation was regulated by long noncoding (lnc)RNA BACE1AS, which was proven by gene silencing approach. Further, M‐ADEVs were also demonstrated to carry the SASP cargoes , a process that was again regulated by lncRNA BACE1AS. Uptake of M‐ADEVs by neurons was shown to induce neuronal senescence & synaptodendritic injury. In vivo validation further demonstrated that morphine administered mice exhibited inflammageing phenotype and cognitive deficits in both the frontal cortex and hippocampus.

Summary/Conclusion: This study underscores the role of lncRNA BACE1AS in regulating astrocytic‐neuronal senescence and the role of M‐ADEVs in mediating synaptodegeneration leading to cognitive impairments associated with ageing.

Funding: This work was supported by NSCAR to Dr. S Buch.

Keywords: BACE1AS, Morphine, ADEVs, ageing, synaptodegeneration, cognitive study

## EV‐TRACK 2.0: an optimized framework for transparent and reproducible extracellular vesicle research

Quentin Roux
1; Sarah Deville1; Sandor Dedeyne1; Jan Van Deun2; Sofie Van Dorpe1; Nele De Langhe1; Niké Guilbert1; Amélie Vander Cruyssen1; Felix De Vuyst1; Cláudio A. Pinheiro3; Robin Boiy1; Mohammad Ghiasloo4; Sam Calis1; Jodi Moons1; Phaedra Van de Vyver1; Olivier De Wever1; An Hendrix3

1Laboratory of Experimental Cancer Research, Department of Human Structure and Repair, Ghent University, Cancer Research Institute Ghent, Ghent Belgium., Gent, Belgium; 2Universitätsklinikum Erlangen, Erlangen, Germany; 3Laboratory of Experimental Cancer Research, Department of Human Structure and Repair, Ghent University, Cancer Research Institute Ghent, Ghent Belgium., Ghent, Belgium; 4Department of Plastic and Reconstructive Surgery, Ghent University Hospital, Gent, Belgium

Introduction: EV‐TRACK (https://evtrack.org/) is a free online community‐powered knowledgebase endorsed by ISEV. It centralizes curated experimental parameters of EV experiments reported in literature in the ‘EV‐METRIC’ as a guidance tool to support transparency and reproducibility. To keep up with the latest advances in EV research, we present EV‐TRACK 2.0 with seven upgraded enabling features to stimulate and further facilitate its use by EV researchers.

Methods: To launch EV‐TRACK 2.0, we developed a user‐friendly submission platform to facilitate study reporting. We updated the queried experimental parameters considering emerging methodologies, MISEV guidelines, reports from ISEV task forces, and other reporting initiatives. We revised the EV‐METRIC to inform researchers and provide guidance about all experimental parameters entered to the submission platform. Finally, driven by community participation the number of entries in the knowledgebase substantially enlarged.

Results: From the 115 experimental parameters surveyed for 1,226 studies originally included in the EV‐TRACK knowledgebase released in 2017, the experimental parameter checklist and the number of reported EV studies have more than doubled with now over 5000 experiments accessible. Yearly an increasing number of pre‐publications has been submitted, resulting in over 500 submissions of which more than half have been published to date. Both the number of experiments per study and the EV‐METRIC increased over time, reflecting increased adherence to the community's reporting and minimal information guidelines.

Summary/Conclusion: Developed for and endorsed by the EV community, EV‐TRACK 2.0 aims to continue supporting an interpretable and reproducible EV research where experiments are transparently reported and guidelines timely centralized.

Funding: European Union's Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska‐Curie grant agreement No [722148].

Keywords: EV‐TRACK; Transparent Reporting; Reproducibility; Rigor and Standardization; Extracellular vesicles

OS32.02.

## Filtering out platelets from human plasma to reveal extracellular vesicle‐associated miRNA profiles

Jillian Bracht
1; Mandy Los1; M.A.J. van Eijndhoven2; Britta A. Bettin3; Edwin van der Pol3; Dirk M. Pegtel2; Rienk Nieuwland4

1Department of Clinical Chemistry, Vesicle Observation Center, Amsterdam UMC, University of Amsterdam, Amsterdam, the Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 2Department of Pathology, Cancer Center Amsterdam, Amsterdam UMC location Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 3Department of Clinical Chemistry, Vesicle Observation Center, Department of Biomedical Engineering and Physics, Amsterdam UMC, University of Amsterdam, Amsterdam, the Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 4Department of Clinical Chemistry; Vesicle Observation Center; Amsterdam University Medical Center, University of Amsterdam, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Introduction: To prepare plasma for studies of extracellular vesicles (EVs), blood is centrifuged to remove cells and cellular debris. To which extent the removal of cells, including platelets, is successful, depends on the applied centrifugation protocol. In daily practice, plasma still contains platelets and erythrocyte ghosts, which may contain genetic material and fragment during freeze‐thaw cycles. Because cells and ghosts co‐migrate with EVs during size‐exclusion chromatography (SEC), their presence can interfere with downstream analysis of EVs. Here we studied the effect of filtration to remove platelets and ghosts from plasma and SEC fractions on downstream miRNA analyses.

Methods: EDTA blood was prepared by (1) 2x centrifugation (2500g, 15 min, no brake; plasma collected 10 mm above the buffy coat/cell pellet), and by (2) 2x centrifugation (900g, 7 min + 2500g, 10 min; max brake; plasma collected approx. 10 mm above buffy coat/cell pellet). Cell and EV concentrations were measured by flow cytometry (BD FACSCanto and Apogee A60‐Micro), and let7a‐5p, miR‐21‐5p, and miR‐223‐3p were measured by qPCR in fresh and frozen‐thawed human plasma before and after SEC2B, with and without filtration using 800 nm polycarbonate filters.

Results: Plasma prepared by protocol 1 and 2 contained ∼10ˆ6 and 10ˆ7 platelets/mL, respectively. Plasma also contained ∼10ˆ6 erythrocytes ghosts/mL. The concentration of platelets decreased after one freeze‐thaw cycle, which coincided with an increase in “platelet‐derived EVs” of 150‐1000 nm. EV‐enriched SEC2B fractions contained ∼10ˆ5 platelets‐ and erythrocyte ghosts/mL. Filtration of fresh plasma or SEC fractions lowered platelet and ghost concentrations >98% without affecting the EV concentration. Filtering resulted in ∼2 to 4‐fold (ΔCt=1‐2) reductions in let7a‐5p, miR‐21‐5p and platelet‐associated miR‐223‐3p. The miRNA quantities correlated with high (>10ˆ6/mL) platelet concentrations in plasma (R2=0.68; p< 0.001), but not with erythrocyte ghosts (R2=0.02; p=0.62) or total EV concentration (R2=0.01; p=0.92). In corresponding SEC fractions (platelet concentration < 10ˆ6/mL) the correlation was less apparent.

Summary/Conclusion: EDTA plasma contains 10ˆ6‐10ˆ7 platelets/mL and 10ˆ6 erythrocytes ghosts/mL after a 2‐step centrifugation protocol, of which a proportion ends up in SEC fractions. This could result in an overestimation of EV‐miRNA content. Therefore, we recommend to measure and report concentrations of platelets and erythrocyte ghosts, and to remove them before freezing samples.

Funding: This study was funded by Health Holland (AQrate project).

Keywords: EVs; Plasma preparation; Platelets; Filtration

OS32.03.

## New Methods to Study the Composition and Structure of T cell trans‐Synaptic Particles

Pablo F. Céspedes‐Donoso
1; Ashwin K. Jainarayanan1; Michael L. Dustin1; Lola Fernandez‐Messina2; Francisco Sanchez‐Madrid2; Ben Peacock3; Roman Fischer4

1University of Oxford, Oxford, United Kingdom; 2Universidad Autonoma de Madrid, Madrid, Spain; 3NanoFCM Co., Ltd., Nottingham, United Kingdom; 4Target Discovery Institute, Centre for Medicines Discovery, Nuffield Department of Medicine, University of Oxford, Oxford, United Kingdom, Oxford, United Kingdom

Introduction: Here we present bead‐supported lipid bilayers as synthetic antigen‐presenting cells (sAPCs) to study the output of T cell synapses, which contains different extracellular particles, including trans‐synaptic vesicles (tSV) and supramolecular attack particles (SMAPs).

Methods: We developed bead supported lipid bilayers containing physiological densities of immune receptors and adhesion molecules to instigate the formation of T cell immunological synapses and capture the trans‐synaptic particles released by T cells. Using this methodology we quantitatively study the composition and biogenesis of trans‐synaptic vesicles (tSV) using TIRF and Airyscan microscopy, Multicolor, Nano and Spectral Flow Cytometry, and proteomics and small RNA sequencing.

Results: We discuss the various applications of sAPCs to characterise trans‐synaptic vesicle composition including flow cytometry, proteomics and sncRNA sequencing. Among others, we used sAPCs to compare the compositional hallmarks of trans‐synaptic vesicles and steadily released EVs. We also discuss the use of the quantitative flow cytometry data to reconstitute sAPCs as well as artificial vesicles. As a proof of concept, we show how the application of the quantitative information extracted using sAPCs helps to generate synthetic trans‐synaptic vesicles working as growth factors to establish human lymphoid organoids.

Summary/Conclusion: APCs represent a good model to dissect elements participating in the biogenesis of T cell trans‐synaptic particles. The application of different OMICs also enables the dissection of key trans‐cellular effectors, including juxtacrine signals recruited at the cell‐cell interface and sorted into trans‐synaptic vesicles as a result of their binding to cognate ligands.

Funding: P.F.C.D was supported by EMBO Long‐Term Fellowship (ALTF 1420‐2015, in conjunction with the European Commission (LTFCOFUND2013, GA‐2013‐609409) and Marie Sklodowska‐Curie Actions) and Oxford‐Bristol Myers Squibb Fellowship.

Keywords: Helper T cells, cytotoxic T lymphocytes, regulatory T cells, TSG101, ESCRT, CD81, CD63, CD40L, ADAM10, BST2, CAR T cells, CD4, BSLBs, microRNAs, target discovery platform, CRISPR/Cas9, Human immunology.

## Protocol to measure the concentration and size distribution of extracellular vesicles with microfluidic resistive pulse sensing

Mona Shahsavari
1; Rienk Nieuwland2; Ton van Leeuwen3; Edwin van der Pol4

1Department of Biomedical Engineering and Physics & Department of Clinical Chemistry, Amsterdam UMC, University of Amsterdam, Amsterdam, the Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 2Department of Clinical Chemistry; Vesicle Observation Center; Amsterdam University Medical Center, University of Amsterdam, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 31. Amsterdam UMC location University of Amsterdam, Biomedical Engineering and Physics, Meibergdreef 9, Amsterdam, the Netherlands 2. Amsterdam Cardiovascular Sciences, Atherosclerosis and Ischemic Syndromes, Amsterdam, the Netherlands 3. Cancer Center Amsterdam, Imaging and Biomarkers, Amsterdam, the Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 4Department of Clinical Chemistry, Vesicle Observation Center, Department of Biomedical Engineering and Physics, Amsterdam UMC, University of Amsterdam, Amsterdam, the Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Introduction: Microfluidic resistive pulse sensing (MRPS; nCS1, Spectradyne) is based on the Coulter Principle, the most accurate technology to size single particles in solution. However, MRPS is laborious because the pore and interior of the microfluidic chip need to be wet. To wet the chip, a wetting agent is added to reduce the surface tension of biological samples. Previously, we used bovine serum albumin (BSA), which leaves extracellular vesicles (EVs) intact but insufficiently decreases the surface tension, thereby hampering measurements. Here, a practical and reliable MRPS protocol was developed using non‐ionic surfactants, Poloxamer‐188, and Tween 20, as wetting agents added to an EV‐containing test sample.

Methods: A cell‐free outdated erythrocyte concentrate sample was used as the EV test sample. To find the percentage of labeled EVs out of all particles, EVs were labeled with CD235a or Lactadherin and measured with flow cytometry (A60‐Micro, Apogee; lower detection limit ∼145 nm). To study the effect of surfactants on EVs, the unstained sample was diluted in filtered Dulbecco's phosphate‐buffered saline (DPBS), or DPBS containing 0.1% BSA (w/v), 0.05% Poloxamer‐188 (v/v), 0.1% Triton X‐100 (v/v), or 1% Tween 20 (v/v), and measured with flow cytometry.

Results: Of all particles, 65% were CD235a+ or Lactadherin+ EVs. About 90% of these particles were susceptible to detergent lysis with Triton X‐100, confirming they are indeed EVs. The total concentration of unstained particles was 1.89E+10 in DPBS, 1.97E+10 in BSA, 1.94E+10 in Poloxamer‐188, and 2.07E+10 in Tween 20. Whereas the total concentration of EVs was unaffected by the presence of Poloxamer‐188 or Tween 20, the size distribution in Tween 20 differed from DPBS, and DPBS containing BSA or Poloxamer‐188. The concentration of EVs smaller than 235 nm and of EVs larger than 235 nm increased by 54% and decreased by 34% in Tween 20 compared to positive controls (DPBS, and DPBS containing BSA), respectively.

Summary/Conclusion: To investigate the effect of surfactants on EVs, not only the total concentration of particles should be taken into account, but also the size distribution should be considered because variations in size distribution do not necessarily affect the total particle concentration. Thus, Poloxamer‐188 can safely be used in MRPS to size particles, including EVS, in suspension.

Funding: M. Shahsavari acknowledges funding from the Dutch Research Council (NWO), grant P18‐26: Medical Photonics (MEDPHOT)

OS32.05.

## Removal and identification of protein corona members of extracellular vesicles using surface covering antimicrobial peptides

Imola Csilla Szigyártó
1; Priyanka Singh2; Mayra Queme Pena2; Anikó Gaál3; Lívia Fülöp4; Lilla Turiák5; Zoltán Varga3; Tamas Beke‐Somfai2

1Research Centre for Natural Science, Institute of Materials and Environmental Sciences, Budapest, Hungary; 2Research Centre for Natural Science, Budapest, Hungary; 3Biological Nanochemistry Research Group, Institute of Materials and Environmental Chemistry, Research Centre for Natural Sciences, Budapest, Hungary; 4Department of Medical Chemistry, University of Szeged, Szeged, Hungary; 5Research Centre for Natural Sciences, Institute of Organic Chemistry, Budapest, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs), these cell‐derived nanoparticles, in the last years have shown great interest and extreme potential in biomedical applications. Increasing number of studies suggest that a protein corona could adhere to the surface of EVs which can have a fundamental effect on EV function, on their targeting and thus on their therapeutical efficacy. However, removing and identifying these corona members is currently a challenging task to achieve.

Methods: Here we have employed three well‐characterized membrane active peptides: CM15, LL‐37 and FK‐16, which were recently reported to preferentially exert their membrane‐related activity via the surface‐covering carpet model. To test their removing capacity of EVs protein corona, red blood cell‐derived vesicles (REVs) were isolated. The interaction between peptides and EVs were followed by flow‐linear dichroism spectroscopy, which provided insight on the effective applicable concentration for EVs treatment. Samples were then subjected to subsequent size exclusion chromatography, microfluidic resistive pulse sensing measurements and proteomics analysis.

Results: Based on the results, antimicrobial peptides have reduced the protein content of the treated REV fractions, where TEM images clearly demonstrate a smooth vesicle surface lacking protein aggregates. Comparing the identified proteins for the control and peptide treated EVs, fifteen proteins were assigned as corona members, which were removed from surface by at least two antimicrobial peptides in separate experiments. These proteins include plasma membrane components or have cytosol, cytoplasm origin, which are likely to associate with the surface of EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: Our results envisage the applicability of antimicrobial peptides as a useful tool in specific surface engineering of nanoparticle from both synthetic and natural origin.

Funding: This work was funded by the Ministry of Innovation and Technology of Hungary from the National Research, Development and Innovation Fund, financed under the TKP2021‐EGA‐31 and the 2020‐1‐1‐2‐PIACI‐KFI_2020‐00021 funding schemes. Support from Eötvös Loránd Research Network, grant n.o. SA‐87/2021, is also acknowledged.

Keywords: red blood cell derived vesicles, protein corona, membrane active peptides, proteomic analysis
