## Chair: Carlos Salomon, MSc, DMedSc, PhD – The University of Queensland

OS33.01.

## Charge matters – identifying extracellular vesicle subpopulations in melanoma patients by free‐flow electrophoresis

Miriam Zentgraf
1; Zeldali Lourens1; Jan Van Deun1; Gerhard Weber2; Andreas Baur1

1Universitätsklinikum Erlangen, Erlangen, Germany; 2FFE Service GmbH, Feldkirchen, Germany

Introduction: The biomarker potential of extracellular vesicles stems from a dysregulated secretion in pathological conditions. While the identification of EV subpopulations based on size, density or surface proteins is well established, an underexposed characteristic of EVs is their surface charge. Free‐flow electrophoresis (FFE) is a gentle, matrix‐free and semi‐preparative method that isolates analytes according to their isoelectric point. Here, we report the use of FFE for the charge‐based separation of EV subpopulations in melanoma. This technology opens new perspectives for establishing EV‐based biomarkers.

Methods: EVs were isolated from cell culture by size‐exclusion chromatography (SEC) or density gradient (DG), resulting in preparations of variable purity. Plasma samples of control subjects and melanoma patients were pre‐processed by dual‐mode chromatography (DMC) to enrich EVs. The FFE instrument was primed with separation buffers spanning a pH range of 4.8‐7.9 and calibrated using pI marker dyes. Concentrated EV samples were introduced into the chamber, separated according to pI and collected into 96‐well plates. Engineered fluorescent EVs were used to optimize the separation conditions. Presence of EV‐associated proteins in different fractions was measured using a plate reader. Relevant fractions were pooled, concentrated by ultrafiltration and characterized by Western blotting and nanoparticle tracking analysis.

Results: By comparing the separation of SEC and DG EVs, we could demonstrate that a successful separation of EV populations by charge depends on their purity. SKMEL‐37 melanoma cell EVs were found to split into different populations, which had a variable relative expression of tetraspanins CD63, CD9 and CD81. Next, comparing DMC‐purified EVs extracted from plasma of control subjects and melanoma patients before and after excision of the primary tumor, we found that melanoma patients are characterized by an EV subpopulation with an increased negative charge.

Summary/Conclusion: Our results qualify DG/DMC‐FFE as a novel procedure for the charge‐based separation of EV populations. These previously unexplored subpopulations could function as promising biomarkers in melanoma.

Keywords: extracellular vesicles, surface charge, subpopulations, melanoma, dual‐mode chromatography, free‐flow electrophoresis

OS33.02.

## Definition of HDL and EV particles in prostate cancer monitoring

Liliia Paniushkina
1; Martin Wolf2; Laura Bianciardi3; Natasa Zarovni4; Dirk Strunk2; Irina Nazarenko5

1Paracelsus Medical Private University, Salzburg, Salzburg, Austria; 2Cell Therapy Institute, Spinal Cord Injury and Tissue Regeneration Center Salzburg (SCI ‐ TReCS), Paracelsus Medical University (PMU), Salzburg, Austria, Salzburg, Austria; 3Exosomics, Siena, Italy; 4HansaBioMed Life Sciences Ltd, Mäealuse 2/1, 12618 Tallinn, Estonia, Tallinn, Estonia; 5Institute for Infection Prevention and Hospital Epidemiology, Faculty of Medicine, University Medical Center Freiburg, Freiburg, Germany, Freiburg, Germany

Introduction: Isolation of purified EV populations from plasma remains a conceptual and methodological topic of discussion in the field. While some studies exclude lipoprotein‐enriched fractions from characterization, we compared proteins and oncogenic signatures in low and high HDL fractions from different EV subtypes in plasma from prostate cancer (PC) and castration‐resistant prostate cancer (CRPC) patients. Our results demonstrate the specific formation of HDL‐EV complexes in progressing prostate cancer.

Methods: ACD plasma samples from patients with BPH, PC and CRPC were analysed regarding platelet and haemolysed RBC. Four size‐based EV subtypes were isolated from contamination‐free samples using differential centrifugation (DC) followed by iodixanol density gradient purification. Each of the fractions was characterized by Nanoparticle Tracking Analysis (NTA), Tunable Resistive Pulse Sensing (TRPS), Interferometric light microscopy (ILM) followed by ELISA, MACSPlex and Transmission electron microscopy (TEM). The CD63, CD9 and CD81 were used as EV markers, ApoB and ApoA1 ‐ for lipoproteins, CD3, CD41 and PSMA as a tissue‐specific biomarker. HDL and EV‐contained fractions were subjected to Next Generation Sequencing (NGS). Differentially expressed genes were characterized using the oncogenic signature pathways library.

Results: High‐ and low‐density fractions from PC and CRPC plasma have different EVs and lipoproteins levels. CRPC plasma‐isolated EV samples contain more CD41 and ApoA1 proteins than PC. The fraction at 1,10 g/ml density was highly enriched on oncogenic signatures (OS) in the CRPC case only. The OS present in the EV5 sample from CRPC plasma is involved in immune system suppression, activation of scavenging by Class A receptors, increase of post‐translational protein modification and activation of vesicle‐mediated transport.

Summary/Conclusion: The characterisation of high‐density particles from plasma samples with PC and CRPC diagnoses is informative. Specific formation of HDL‐EV complexes might signify prostate cancer progression.

Funding: European Union's Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Sklodowska‐Curie grant agreement No 722148.

Keywords: prostate cancer, ACD plasma, oncogenic signatures, HDL

OS33.03.

## Diagnostic performance evaluation of small RNA biomarkers in urinary EVs from prostate cancer patients

Elena S. Martens‐Uzunova
1; Natasja Dits1; Jozien Heleman1; Martin E. E. van Royen2; Irene Bijnsdorp3; Gerald Verhaegh4; Connie Jimenez5; Jack Schalken4; Guido W. W. Jenster1

1Department of Urology, Erasmus MC Cancer Institute, Erasmus University Medical Center, Rotterdam, Netherlands; 2Department of Pathology, Erasmus MC, Rotterdam, The Netherlands, Rotterdam, Netherlands; 3Department of Urology, Amsterdam UMC, Location VUMC, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 4Department of Urology, Radboud UMC, Nijmegen, Netherlands; 5Department of Medical Oncology, OncoProteomics Laboratory, Cancer Center Amsterdam, Amsterdam UMC, Location VUMC, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Introduction: Early identification of clinically significant disease remains a major challenge in the treatment of prostate cancer (PCa) patients. A warranted solution is the development of a minimally‐invasive test that can detect aggressive PCa and prevent repeated biopsies. Here, we explored the potential of a group of small RNA (sRNA) biomarkers (IP evaluation pending) previously identified in PCa tissue for their presence in urinary extracellular vesicles (uEVs) and their performance as minimally‐invasive, liquid biopsy biomarkers.

Methods: Spot urines were collected at 3 Dutch medical centers after digital rectal examination and prior to diagnostic biopsy. Informed consent was obtained from all patients. Urines were precleared by 20 min centrifugation at 500 G within 4 h after collection. Creatinine and urinary PSA (uPSA) were determined by standard clinical laboratory tests. Total uEV RNA was isolated from 2x4 ml supernatant aliquots with Norgen urine exosome RNA isolation kit. uEV quantity, CD9, and CD63 were determined by (immuno)EVQuant and TR‐FIA. Duplex qPCR was done with TaqMan advanced kit and custom probes. Differences between groups were assessed by Kruskal‐Wallis test with Dunn's test for multiple comparisons. Biomarker performance was assessed by receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve analysis.

Results: Evaluation of sRNA performance was done in 3 assessment rounds. 2 out of 11 tested sRNAs (sRNAx2) were selected based on their expression and ability to detect aggressive PCa (Gleason score >7) for final evaluation in a cohort of 107 PCa negative and 221 PCa positive patients (Gleason score: 6, n=64; 7, n=98; >7, n=59). There was no significant difference in creatinine levels among the groups. However, uPSA levels were significantly lower in patients with aggressive PCa, indicating lower proportion of prostate fluid and prostate derived EVs in the urine of patients from this group. Therefore sRNAx2 values were normalized to uPSA. Normalized sRNAx2 values were significantly higher for patients with aggressive PCa compared to indolent PCa (Gleason score 6, adjusted p = 0.002) or no PCa (adjusted p = 0.011). ROC curve analysis of the diagnostic ability of sRNAx2/uPSA ratio returned an AUC= 0,682 (p = 0.001) and an AUC = 0,655 (p = 0.0005) for the indolent PCa and for the no PCa groups, respectively.

Summary/Conclusion: Urinary EV sRNAx2/uPSA ratio can classify patients with high grade PCa and has a potential as an early liquid biopsy biomarker for aggressive PCa.

Funding: The IMMPROVE consortium (Innovative Measurements and Markers for PROstate cancer diagnosis and prognosis using extracellular VEsicles) is supported by an Alpe d'HuZes & Dutch Cancer Society grant (KWF grant number #EMCR2015‐8022).

Keywords: prostate cancer, diagnosis, urinary extracellular vesicles, small RNA

OS33.04.

## Extracellular vesicles released from leptin‐treated breast cancer cells rewire cell metabolism: a proteomic approach

Luca Gelsomino
1; Amanda Caruso2; Francesca Giordano2; Matteo Brindisi2; Giovanna Morello3; Felice Maria Accattatis2; Ines Barone2; Salvatore Panza2; Daniela Bonofiglio2; Sebastiano Andò2; Cinzia Giordano2; Stefania Catalano2

1University of Calabria, arcavacata di Rende, Italy; 2University of Calabria, Arcavacata di Rende, Italy; 3National Research Council, Catania, Italy

Introduction: The obesity hormone leptin acting in autocrine, paracrine and endocrine manner deeply influences breast cancer (BC) biology thus recognized as a crucial mediator in cell‐to‐cell communication. Recently, we demonstrated that leptin increases extracellular vesicle (EV) biogenesis and release in BC cells. Here, we focused on the identification of a specific leptin‐induced EV proteomic signature in BC cells in attempt to find molecular effectors associated with BC progression.

Methods: EVs from conditioned media of MCF‐7 BC cells have been isolated by ultracentrifugation method and, accordingly to MISEV guidelines, characterized by transmission electron microscopy, nanoparticle tracking analysis and immunoblot. EV proteomic profile was obtained by liquid chromatography‐tandem mass spectrometry analysis (LC‐MS/MS), and analyzed by MaxQuant Software. Gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) was used to explore gene ontology (GO) categories enriched in leptin‐derived EVs. ATP assay, MitoTracker Deep Red/Orange and Seahorse XFe96 analysis were performed to assess energy production and mitochondrial metabolism.

Results: LC‐MS/MS of EV cargo allowed the identification of 38 differentially expressed proteins (FDR< 0.2) in EV from leptin treated MCF‐7 cells considering |1.5| fold as a cut‐off. GSEA revealed an enrichment in GO involved in energy production and mitochondrial metabolism in leptin‐derived EVs compared to the untreated one. Functional studies revealed that leptin‐derived EVs significantly increased ATP production, mitochondrial activity and oxidative phosphorylation in BC cells.

Summary/Conclusion: Our data indicate that leptin through the release of EV‐enriched mitochondrial proteins may rewire metabolism of BC cells. Characterization of tumor‐derived EV protein cargo in obesity‐associated milieu, as in the presence of elevated leptin levels, might allow to identify unique features and specific biological processes to be targeted in clinical management of disease.

Funding: Department of Pharmacy, Health and Nutritional Sciences of University of Calabria (Department of Excellence, Italian Law 232/2016) MIUR; AIRC IG #21414; PRIN 2017 #2017EKMFTN_001 to S. C.; PRIN 2017 #2017WNKSLR_005 to I.B; SILA PONa3_00341.

Keywords: leptin, breast cancer, cell metabolism

OS33.05.

## Heparin treatment prevents tumour‐derived EV‐induced epithelial‐to‐mesenchymal transition: A novel bioelectronic platform for screening anti‐cancer drugs

Walther Traberg‐Christensen; Roisin Owens

University of Cambridge, Cambridge, United Kingdom

Introduction: Tumor cell‐derived EVs (TEVs) have recently been reported to play an active role in tumorigenesis and metastasis owing to their ability to transmit oncogenes. One hypothesis is that TEVs contribute to metastasis by inducing the epithelial‐to‐mesenchymal transition (EMT), characterized by the loss of barrier function in barrier tissue‐forming cells. Developing drugs that directly target TEVs and block TEV‐induced EMT is crucial for improving clinical outcomes in cancer.

Methods: Current methods for measuring EV function lack the ability to obtain quantitative data in a non‐disruptive, label‐free, and continuous manner. We have shown that organic electronic devices based on the conducting polymer PEDOT:PSS can be interfaced with biological systems of varying complexity allowing for quantitative real‐time monitoring of biological interactions with excellent sensitivity. The optical transparency of organic electrochemical transistors (OECTs) based on PEDOT:PSS allows for multiplex analysis with both optical and electrical readouts. The malignant transformation of healthy breast epithelial cells (MCF‐10A) after exposure to EVs derived from triple‐negative metastatic breast cancer cells (MDA‐MB‐231) was monitored using OECTs and orthogonal validation by immunoblotting, immunofluorescence imaging, DNA methylation, and ELISA was performed to validate the electrical measurements.

Results: We show that CD63‐positive TEVs can induce an EMT‐like process in MCF‐10A cells as demonstrated by changes to cell morphology, loss of apico‐basal polarity, reorganization of filamentus actin, and gain of mesenchymal proteins, such as vimentin and N‐cadherin. The decrease in barrier integrity of the cell monolayer was monitored electrically using OECTs, gaining of truly quantitative insight into TEV‐induced EMT with higher temporal resolution than conventional, orthogonal methods. Having established this, we demonstrated that heparin ‐ a common anticoagulant drug ‐ successfully abrogates this malignant transformation and prevented TEV‐induced EMT.

Summary/Conclusion: Importantly, our OECT platform allowed us to monitor the transformational state of the cells in real‐time, providing unprecedented temporal resolution of EMT and revealing that persistent heparin treatment is necessary to prevent TEV‐induced EMT. The non‐disruptive and continuous measurements enabled by this bioelectronic platform allow researchers to test the efficacy of anti‐cancer, and especially anti‐metastatic drugs, faster and using fewer resources than conventional methods, opening up the possibility for high‐throughput drug screening.

Keywords: Cancer metastasis, EMT, heparin, anti‐cancer drug, drug screening, screening platform

## OS36: Late‐Breaking Session: EVs and Cancer II

## Chair: Nicholas Peake – Sheffield Hallam University

OS36.01.

## Extracellular vesicle‐based delivery of therapeutic siRNAs alleviates skeletal muscle loss and prolongs survival of cancer cachectic mice

Boya Peng
1; Yuqi Yang1; Migara Kavishka K. Jayasinghe1; Rachel Tan1; Marco Pirisinu2; Thach Tuan Pham1; Kun Liang3; Shyh‐Chang Ng3; Minh T.N. T.N Le1

1Department of Pharmacology, Yong Loo Lin School of Medicine, National University of Singapore, Singapore, Singapore, Singapore; 2Department of Biomedical Sciences, College of Veterinary Medicine and Life Sciences, City University of Hong Kong, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, Hong Kong; 3State Key Laboratory of Stem Cell and Reproductive Biology, Institute of Zoology, Chinese Academy of Sciences, Beijing, China (People's Republic)

Introduction: RNA drug delivery to the muscle is a promising strategy for the treatment of muscle degenerative diseases but it is challenging because myocytes are hard to transfect. Here, we utilize red blood cell extracellular vesicles (RBCEVs) to deliver siRNAs to skeletal muscles for cancer cachexia treatment based on our previous observation that small RNAs can be loaded efficiently into RBCEVs. Cancer cachexia is the loss of skeletal muscle commonly seen in cancer patients. It not only has an adverse impact on quality of life, but is also associated with poor responses to chemotherapy and poor survival.

Methods: RBCEVs were purified from RBCs of healthy donors by ultracentrifugation and characterized by nanoparticle tracking analysis, western blotting and nanoanalyzer flow cytometry. We studied the uptake of RBCEVs by myotubes both in vitro and in vivo using immunostaining. siRNAs were designed to target Myostatin (Mstn), a negative regulator of skeletal muscle growth and differentiation, and Malonyl‐CoA decarboxylase (Mlycd), an enzyme driving the malfunctioned metabolism of cancer cachexia. We assessed the therapeutic effects of siRNAs against Mstn and Mlycd when delivered using RBCEVs via intramuscular administration in a mouse model of lung cancer with cachexia.

Results: RBCEVs were robustly taken up by C2C12 myotubes in culture and by primary myotubes in the quadriceps upon intramuscular injection. The EV uptake was mediated by endocytosis. Delivery of Mstn siRNA and Mlycd siRNA using RBCEVs notably inhibited endogenous Mstn and Mlycd, and increased skeletal muscle growth without considerable side effects in wild‐type mice. In cancer cachectic mouse model, we observed significant alleviation of skeletal muscle loss and prolonged survival rate after multiple intramuscular administrations of Mstn siRNA‐loaded and Mlycd siRNA‐loaded RBCEVs. Furthermore, blood chemistry tests of the mice demonstrated that the intramuscular delivery of siRNAs using RBCEVs was non‐toxic and non‐inflammatory.

Summary/Conclusion: In this study, we have characterized the uptake of RBCEVs by myotubes and demonstrated potential therapeutic properties of RBCEV‐delivered Mstn siRNA and Mlycd siRNA in cancer cachexia treatment. Hence, this is a safe and efficient RNA drug delivery strategy for muscle degenerative diseases.

OS36.02.

## Influence of age on fibroblast‐derived extracellular vesicles and their role in melanoma progression

Laura Hueser
1; Yash Chhabra1; Olesia Gololobova2; Mitchell Fane1; Kenneth W. Witwer2; Ashani Weeraratna1

1Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, USA; 2Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Baltimore, USA

Introduction: Age is an important prognostic factor in cutaneous melanoma, commonly arising in the elderly. Melanoma progression is the concerted outcome of cellular and molecular changes occurring in tumor cells and within the milieu of the stromal cells, collectively referred to as the tumor microenvironment (TME). Also, TME‐secreted growth factors, cytokines, and extracellular matrix (ECM) are vital to tumor progression and therapy resistance. Aging causes remodeling of the TME making it conducive to melanoma progression. Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have been shown to transport lipids, mRNAs, and functional proteins across several cell types. Considering their important role in tumor progression in different cancer types, investigating how an aged tissue microenvironment can alter EV attributes and cargo remains to be evaluated.

Methods: Conditioned media of age‐stratified dermal fibroblasts (dFs), the predominant stromal cell population of the melanoma TME, was concentrated and subsequently EVs were isolated using size separation via Izon (consistent with MISEV2018). Next, size and concentration of EVs were analyzed via NTA and mass spectrometry was performed to investigate how systemic aging changes the EVs released by dFs. We also sought to evaluate the influence of these EVs on melanoma progression.

Results: Our study shows that the aged secretome significantly promotes melanoma cell invasion and that this effect can be significantly reduced by EV depletion. We found that the tetraspanin CD9 was decreased in aged dFs and their released EVs versus young dFs. Reduction in CD9 expression in dFs was sufficient to significantly reduce the proliferation of melanoma cells while significantly increasing melanoma cell invasion.

Summary/Conclusion: Thus, we can conclude that with age, EVs released by dFs experience cargo changes and promote tumor progression. EVs are thus another mechanistic avenue to age‐associated melanoma progression and therapy resistance.

Funding: Deutsche Forschung Gesellschaft (DFG) Post Doc Fellowship

Keywords: Melanoma, tumor microenvironment, age

OS36.03.

## RNA‐Seq and Mass Spec profiles of cancer‐derived EVs overlap in distinct EV fractions
Tatyana Vagner1; Elizabeth Hutchins2; Wenyan Zhao3; Yari Ciani4; Federico Vannuccini4; Andrew R. Chin1; Javier Mariscal1; Minhyung Kim1; Sungyong You1; Agnes Kittel5; Krizia Sagini6; Marco De Simone7; Edit I. Buzás8; Wei Yang1; Clotilde Théry9; Francesca Demichelis4; Paul Boutros10; Kendall Jensen2; Dolores Di Vizio
1

1Department of Surgery, Division of Cancer Biology and Therapeutics, Cedars‐Sinai Medical Center, Los Angeles, CA, USA, Los Angeles, USA; 2Neurogenomics Division, Translational Genomics Research Institute, Phoenix, USA; 3Department of Human Genetics, Jonsson Comprehensive Cancer Center, Institute for Precision Health, University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, USA; 4Department of Cellular, Computational and Integrative Biology (CIBIO), University of Trento, 38123 Trento, Italy, Trento, Italy; 5Institute of Experimental Medicine, Budapest, Hungary; 6Institute for Cancer Research, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway; 7Department of Radiation Oncology, Cedars‐Sinai Medical Center, Los Angeles, USA; 8Semmelweis University Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Budapest, Hungary; 9Institut Curie, PSL Research University, INSERM U932, Paris, France; 10Department of Human Genetics, Jonsson Comprehensive Cancer Center, Institute for Precision Health, Broad Stem Cell Research Center, Department of Urology, University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, USA

Introduction: To identify general and EV population‐specific proteins and transcripts, we performed quantitative proteomic and transcriptomic analyses of 3 EV fractions obtained from prostate cancer, glioma, and breast cancer cell lines.

Methods: Differential centrifugation at low, medium, and high speed (2.8K, 10K, 100K) and density gradient, TRPS, TEM, LC‐MS/MS, RNA‐Seq, Flow Cytometry, SP‐IRIS.

Results: Quantitative proteomic analysis of 3 EV fractions showed that proteins considered to be enriched in exosomes are present in all EVs and can be used as general EV markers. The most distinct protein and RNA expression patterns were identified in the 2.8K and 100K fractions, while the 10K EVs represented a mixture of the two. The correlation between the proteins and protein‐coding mRNA transcripts was higher in 2.8K vs 10K, and higher in 10K vs 100K, with the 2.8K and 10K EVs exhibiting the mRNA profiles more similar to each other than to the 100K EVs. Additionally, the protein cargo correlations between the 2.8K and 10K (0.64) and between the 10K and 100K (0.84) were lower than the mRNA correlations between these fractions (0.94 and 0.91, respectively), potentially indicating that the identity of the EV populations is defined by their protein cargo more than by their mRNA cargo. We also noticed that the mRNA transcripts in 2.8K and 10K are more similar to each other than the mRNA transcripts in 10K and 100K, while the proteins in 10K and 100K are more similar to each other than the proteins in 2.8K and 10K, indicating that 2.8K and 10K are more similar to each other at the mRNA level, whereas 10K and 100K are more similar at the protein level. Pathway analysis demonstrated that large EVs (2.8K and 10K) are enriched in pathways involved in essential metabolic activities including mitochondrial functions. The enrichment of mitochondria transcripts was also supported by single vesicles RNA sequencing of the 10K EVs. Finally, we identified several internal and surface proteins as novel markers of EV populations.

Summary/Conclusion: Comprehensive proteomic and transcriptomic analyses identify specific protein and mRNA profiles in EVs suggesting that cancer pathways are conserved at the protein and RNA level in EVs.

Funding: Support: National Institutes of Health R01CA218526 (DDV); DoD PC150836 (DDV)

Keywords: EV heterogeneity, mass spectrometry, RNA‐seq, proteomics, transcriptomics, cancer

OS36.05.

## The role of small extracellular vesicles from head and neck cancer patients in tumor angiogenesis

Luisa Tengler
1; Marie‐Nicole Theodoraki2; Julia Schütz3; Frederic Jungbauer3; Anne Lammert3; Nicole Rotter3; Sonja Ludwig3

1Medical Faculty Mannheim of Heidelberg University, Germany, Mannheim, Germany; 2Department of Otorhinolaryngology, Head and Neck Surgery, Ulm University Medical Center, Ulm, Germany, Ulm, Germany; 3Department of Otorhinolaryngology, Head and Neck Surgery, University Hospital Mannheim, Mannheim, Germany, Mannheim, Germany

Introduction: Angiogenesis is a key regulatory mechanism in the carcinogenesis of head and neck cancer (HNC). While small extracellular vesicles (sEVs) from HNC cell lines (TEX) reprogram endothelial cells (ECs) and promote angiogenesis, the role of patient plasma sEVs still remains unclear.

Methods: sEVs were isolated from plasma of 24 HNC patients (HNC), 10 recovered patients (NED) and 9 healthy donors (HD) by size‐exclusion chromatography. The vesicles were characterized by transmission electron microscopy (TEM), nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) and Western blots (WBs), respectively. The interaction of sEVs with human umbilical vein ECs (HUVECs) was visualized by confocal microscopy (CM). The angiogenesis‐related protein profiles of sEVs were examined via antibody arrays. Functional assays were performed to analyze the effect of sEVs on the proliferation, migration and tubulogenesis of HUVECs.

Results: Plasma sEVs showed typical shape in TEM, size in NTA and protein markers in WBs and were effectively internalized by HUVECs. Antibody arrays confirmed an enrichment of anti‐angiogenic proteins in all sEVs, while the pro‐angiogenic protein MMP‐9 was significantly overexpressed in sEVs from HNC compared to ND sEVs. In functional assays, sEVs from HNC patients inhibited proliferation, migration and tubulogenesis of HUVECs, however, to a significantly lesser extent than HD and NED sEVs.

Summary/Conclusion: Angiogenesis is regulated by a balance between inhibitors and stimulators. In contrast to TEX, HNC sEVs show rather inhibiting effects on ECs. Since plasma contains a mixture of sEVs from different origins, HNC plasma sEVs might shift this balance in favor of a less anti‐angiogenic side in comparison to NED and HD sEVs.

Funding: German Research Foundation (DFG) and the Foundation for Cancer and Scarlet Fever Research of the University Heidelberg

Keywords: sEV, angiogenesis, head and neck cancer, HUVEC

## OS34: Late‐Breaking Session: EVs and Viruses

## OS34 Astrocytes & astrocyte derived extracellular vesicles in HIV associated neurological disorders: HIF‐1α‐BACE1AS axis

Susmita Sil
1; Divya Chemparthy2; Shannon Callen2; Shilpa Buch3

1Department of Pharmacology & Experimental Neuroscience, Omaha, USA; 2UNMC, Omaha, USA; 3University of Nebraska Medical Center, Omaha, USA

Introduction: Although the advent of antiretoivrals has dramatically increased the lifespan of HIV+ individuals, paradoxically, long‐term dependence on these toxic medications, is also associated with an increased risk of Alzheimer's‐like pathology – an emerging comorbidity of HIV‐Associated Neurocognitive Disorders (HAND). Based on our previous findings that astrocytes play a major role in HIV Tat‐mediated amyloidosis, and, since amyloids are released in extracellular vesicles (EVs), we sought to assess whether HIV‐1‐Tat stimulated astrocyte derived EVs (ADEVs): a) could mediate synaptodendritic injury in vitro in hippocampal neurons and, b) when injected stereotactically into the brains of naïve mice could mediate cognitive impairment. Our previous studies have demonstrated the role of HIF‐1α as an upstream regulator of HIV1‐Tat mediated astrocytuc amyloidosis. We thus hypothesized that blocking HIF‐1α could likely mitigate neuronal injury.

Methods: Tat‐ADEV uptake by the neurons was assessed by mcherry plasmid tagged EVs, and synaptic injury by confocal microscopy & analysis in Neurolucida software. Behavioral assays included Y‐maze study, locomotor, anxiety & fear conditioning.

Results: Rat hippocampal neurons exposed to Tat‐ADEVs carrying amyloids led to reduction of dendritic arborization, spines, ratio of spine sub‐types, synaptic proteins & post‐synaptic currents. Silencing of astrocytic HIF‐1α not only reduced the numbers of ADEV release and their amyloid cargos, but also ameliorated Tat‐ADEV induced neuronal synaptodendritic injury. Interestingly injection of Tat‐ADEVs carrying amyloids in the hippocampus of naïve mice brains, resulted in reduction of excitatory synapse densities, increased chromatolysis in neurons, increased amyloid deposition, and enhanced glial cell activation and neuroinflammation. Concomitantly these animals showed impairment of long‐term memory, locomotor activity as well as anxiety like behavior. This impairment(s) was abrogated in mice injected with HIF‐1α silenced ADEVs.

Summary/Conclusion: This study underscores the role of amyloid carrying ADEVs in mediating synaptodegeneration leading to cognitive impairments associated with HAND, and highlights the protective role of HIF‐1α in this process.

Funding: This work was supported by grants R21AG069541 and John Hopkins University, R25MH080661 (S. Sil) from National Institute of Health.

Keywords: HIF‐1α, HAND, ADEVs, synaptodegeneration, memory

## OS34 HIV‐1 Nef changes the protein content of T cells extracellular vesicles promoting the reduction of the antiviral proteins IFITMs

Mara E. da Silva‐Januário
1; Cristina da Costa2; Ana Karina Oliveira3; Lucas tavares2; Yunan Januário2; Andreia de Carvalho2; Michael Miller4; Clarice Arns4; Adriana Leme3; Luis L. da Silva2

1Ribeirão Preto Medical School ‐ University of São Paulo, Brazil, Ribeirão Preto, Brazil; 2Virology Research Center ‐ Ribeirão Preto School of Medicine ‐ University of Sao Paulo, Ribeirão Preto, Brazil; 3CNPEM, Campinas, Brazil; 4UNICAMP, Campinas, Brazil

Introduction: EVs carry a repertoire of molecules involved in intercellular communication and function as vectors in the transference of proteins involved in defense against virus infections. Conversely, viruses counteract EVs with antiviral activity by modifying their content to favor infection. Nef is a multifunctional accessory protein of HIV that contributes to higher viremia and disease progression. Nef achieves these goals by modifying the levels of several antiviral proteins in different compartments of the host cell, such as the plasma membrane, Golgi complex, and EVs.

Methods: We performed quantitative proteomics of EVs isolated from T cells to characterize the global changes in EVs protein content and identify novel targets of Nef. The EVs were separated by differential ultracentrifugation, washed, and characterized by NTA, SEM, and western blot. EVs content delivery was analyzed by western blot and EVs membrane dye associated with confocal microscopy. HIV challenge in acceptor cells was monitored by luciferase assays.

Results: Proteomic data showed an evident variation in the levels of specific proteins due to Nef expression. The antiviral protein IFITM2 presented reduced levels. Further analysis showed that Nef reduced the IFITM1, IFITM2, and IFITM3 in T cells EVs. Besides, Nef altered IFITM3 subcellular localization in T cells and Hela cells, excluding IFITMs from plasma membrane and lipid rafts domains. Previous works showed that IFITM3 transference by EVs confers protection against Dengue and Zika infection. Therefore, we investigated if IFITMs transference via EVs to acceptor cells would confer antiviral activity to HIV infection. There was an efficient delivery of IFITM3 via EVs to acceptor cells. Our preliminary data showed that HIV infectivity was reduced in acceptor cells of IFITM3‐containing EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: The described findings point Nef as a global modulator of EVs protein content. Our preliminary results suggest that IFITM3 transference by EVs may confer protection against HIV infection. We propose that Nef is an antagonist of antiviral IFITMs transference mediated by EVs.

Funding: CNPq and FAPESP.

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, HIV, Nef, IFITMs, and antiviral activity.

## OS34 Milk extracellular vesicles carry ACE2 and serve as natural decoys to inhibit pseudotyped SARS‐CoV‐2 virus

Martijn van Herwijnen
1; Susanne G. van der Grein2; Lonneke Broeks3; Marije Kleinjan1; Berend‐Jan Bosch2; Frank J.M. Van Kuppeveld2; Marca H.M Wauben4

1Utrecht University, Utrecht, Netherlands; 2Virology Section, Division Infectious Diseases & Immunology, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, Netherlands; 3Division of Cell Biology, Metabolism & Cancer, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, Netherlands; 4Division of Cell Biology, Metabolism & Cancer, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands

Introduction: Human milk is a rich source of immunomodulatory and anti‐viral components that reduce the risk for enteric and respiratory virus infections in breastfed infants. For instance, extracellular vesicles (EVs) from milk have been shown to reduce rotavirus and respiratory syncytial virus infection. Yet, despite considerable research on severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS‐CoV‐2), and indications that breastfeeding protects infants from COVID‐19, the direct role of milk‐derived EVs on corona virus infection has not been elucidated.

Methods: In this study, we used mature milk that was donated by healthy mothers prior to the emergence of SARS‐CoV‐2 and purified EVs (and a procedural EV‐depleted control) via differential centrifugation, density gradient separation and SEC. Proteomic analysis, as well as immunoblotting was used to identify SARS‐CoV‐2 binding proteins on milk EVs. Functional activity of milk EVs was tested in vitro by incubating EVs with vesicular stomatitis virus (VSV) expressing firefly luciferase and pseudotyped with the SARS‐CoV‐2 spike protein, after which these were added to Vero E6 cells. Luciferase activity was determined after overnight incubation and served as a measure for viral entry. Additionally, milk EVs were treated with blocking antibodies against ACE2 prior to virus incubation to test the involvement of ACE2 in their function.

Results: Pre‐incubation of virus and milk EVs prior to infection reduced viral entry significantly in a dose‐dependent matter, while a procedural EV‐depleted milk control did not. Using proteomic analysis together with immunoblotting, we show that milk EVs express ACE2. We therefore investigated whether the inhibition of viral entry was caused by binding of the SARS‐CoV‐2 spike protein to EV‐associated ACE2. Indeed, blocking ACE2 on the milk EVs prior to virus incubation led to a reduction in viral entry inhibition.

Summary/Conclusion: By analysing pre‐pandemic expressed milk, we show that milk EVs are naturally endowed with the capacity to neutralize viral entry of SARS‐CoV‐2 and acts as decoys potentially via ACE2.

Funding: This work was supported by the European Union's Horizon 2020 Framework Programme under the grant FETOPEN‐801367 evFOUNDRY

Keywords: human milk SEC density gradient corona virus SARS‐CoV‐2 COVID‐19 ACE2

## OS34 Proteomics characterization of circulating extracellular vesicles in COVID‐19 patients with different clinical manifestations
Maria Jesus Pinazo1; Núria Cortés‐Serra2; Alberto Ayllon‐Hermida2; Melisa Gualdrón‐López2; Patricia Resa‐Infante3; Joan Josep Bech‐Serra4; Sergio Montaner‐Tarbes5; Iris Aparici Herraiz2; Marc Nicolau‐Fernandez6; Itziar Erkizia3; Bernat Cucurull4; Lucia Gutierrez‐Chamorro3; Ester Ballana‐Guix3; Silvia Marfil3; Edwards Pradenas‐Saavedra3; Carlos Ávila‐Nieto3; Julia Bauzá‐Martinez7; Franziska I Völlmy8; Magdalena Muelas1; Ruth Sotil1; Núria Izquierdo‐Useros3; Lorenzo Fraile9; Maria Montoya10; Julia Vergara11; Joaquim Segalés11; Jorge Carrillo3; Julia Blanco3; Carmen Fernandez Becerra2; Carolina De la Torre4; Javier Martinez‐Picado12; Hernando A. del Portillo
13

1ISGlobal, Barcelona, Spain; 2ISGlobal IGTP, Barcelona, Spain; 3IrsiCaixa, Badalona, Spain; 4Institute Josep Carreras, Badalona, Spain; 5Innovex Therapeutics S.L., Badalona, Spain; 6ISGlobal IGTP, Badalona, Spain; 7Biomolecular Mass Spectrometry and Proteomics, Bijvoet Center for Biomolecular Research and Utrecht Institute for Pharmaceutical Sciences, Utrecht University, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands; 8Biomolecular Mass Spectrometry and Proteomics, Bijvoet Center for Biomolecular Research and Utrecht Institute for Pharmaceutical Sciences, Utrecht University, Utrecht, The Netherlands, utrecht, Netherlands; 9Universitat de Lleida, Lleida, Spain; 10CSIC‐CiB, madrid, Spain; 11IRTA, Barcelona, Spain; 12IrsiCaixa ICREA, Badalona, Spain; 13ISGlobal IGTP ICREA, Barcelona, Spain

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) released by viral‐infected cells can incorporate viral peptides. In this study, we hypothesised that the molecular characterization of EVs obtained from COVID‐19 patients will reveal novel immunogenic viral peptides and molecular signatures of disease severity.

Methods: Studies were ethically approved. Blood samples from COVID‐19 patients with severe (G1, N=19) mild/asymptomatic (G2, N=26) disease and controls (G3, N=12) were collected during active infection (T0) in early 2020 and 8 months’ (T8) post‐infection. Total and neutralizing antibody levels were determined. EVs from plasma were isolated by immunocapture (CD9), ganglioside‐capture (Siglec‐1) and size‐exclusion chromatography (SEC). In addition, we isolated EVs from SARS‐CoV2 infected hamsters. EVs were characterized by Western blot, BBA‐assay, NTA, and analysed by label‐free proteomics.

Results: Clinical data showed increased inflammation markers in G1. Neutralising antibodies in G1 non‐vaccinated individuals increased at T8, indicating sustained exposure to SARS‐CoV2 antigens. Proteomic analysis of EVs from G1 and G2 patients, failed to detect viral peptides. These results were validated by Western blot against Spike‐RBD. Moreover, we also failed to detect viral peptides in plasma‐derived EVs from hamsters with active (PCR+) SARS‐Cov2 infection. CD9‐enriched EVs showed significant upregulation of 140 proteins in G1, including proteins involved in the response to viral infection. Siglec‐1‐captured EVs showed significant enrichment of EV markers and upregulation of 26 proteins in G1, including 13 of the most upregulated in CD9+EVs in G1. SEC‐enriched EVs showed no significant differences in proteins in any group. Control individuals from G3 showed dramatic changes in the protein's abundance when comparing T0 vs T8, likely due to vaccination.

Summary/Conclusion: Proteomic profiling of EVs isolated by three different methods failed in identifying viral peptides associated with circulating EVs from COVID‐19 patients. Human EV cargo identified proteins with prognostic potential in severe disease.

Funding: SGlobal, IrsiCaixa, IJC, EPIC‐XS

Keywords: Plasma circulating EVs, Covid‐19 patients, different clinical syndromes, neutralizing antibodies, proteomics

## OS34 Red blood cell‐derived extracellular vesicles display endogenous anti‐viral effects and serve as effective oligonucleotide carriers for SARS‐CoV‐2 inhibition

Migara Kavishka K. Jayasinghe
1; Chang Gao1; Gracemary Yap1; Brendon Yeo1; Wen Xiu Loh1; Thach Tuan Pham1; Dong Van Hoang1; Jang Hann Chu2; Minh T.N. T.N Le1

1Department of Pharmacology, Yong Loo Lin School of Medicine, National University of Singapore, Singapore, Singapore, Singapore; 2Department of Microbiology and Immunology, Yong Loo Lin School of Medicine, National University of Singapore, Singapore, Singapore, Singapore

Introduction: The severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS‐CoV‐2) has caused the largest pandemic in recent history. Despite the success of vaccines, there still remains no widely accessible treatment that can efficiently suppress COVID‐19 infection. Several recent studies have suggested the application of extracellular vesicles (EVs) expressing ACE2 as a potential treatment for SARS‐CoV‐2. Here, we investigated the endogenous anti‐viral effects displayed by ACE2‐negative red blood cell‐derived EVs (RBCEVs) against SARS‐CoV‐2. Moreover, the use of RBCEVs to deliver anti‐sense oligonucleotides (ASOs) for SARS‐CoV‐2 treatment is explored.

Methods: RBCEVs were isolated using established protocols using a sequential workflow consisting of differential centrifugation, filtration, density centrifugation and size exclusion chromatography. The resulting EVs were assessed on their ability to suppress pseudotyped and authentic SARS‐CoV‐2 infection using plaque reduction neutralization tests, qPCR and immunofluorescent imaging. Subsequently, EVs were loaded with ASOs targeting conserved regions of key SARS‐CoV‐2 genes and assayed for the ability to inhibit authentic SARS‐CoV‐2 infection.

Results: RBCEVs were shown to be capable of inhibiting pseudotyped and authentic SARS‐CoV‐2 infection in a dose dependent manner. The neutralization effects were shown to be consistent across major variants of concern and mediated via inhibiting viral entry into cells. RBCEV‐mediated delivery of ASOs targeting SARS‐CoV‐2 genes helicase, 3CLPro, PLpro and RdRp effectively inhibited authentic SARS‐CoV‐2 infection and replication. ASO‐loaded RBCEVs administered via intratracheal delivery to K18‐ACE2 mice accumulated in lung epithelial cells and suppressed SARS‐CoV‐2 infection.

Summary/Conclusion: We leveraged the endogenous anti‐viral properties of RBCEVs together with exogenous loading of anti‐sense oligonucleotides (ASOs), to develop a potent EV‐based anti‐viral therapeutic capable of efficiently suppressing SARS‐CoV‐2 infection and replication.

Funding: This work was funded by the Institute for Digital Medicine (WisDM) and the Singapore Ministry of Education.

Keywords: SARS‐CoV‐2, COVID‐19, extracellular vesicles, RBCEVs, oligonucleotide therapy, anti‐viral therapy

## A GWC screen to unravel new molecular mechanisms involved in EV uptake

Víctor Toribio
1; Albert Lu2; Miguel Palma‐Cobo1; Carlos E. Bastús2; María Yáñez‐Mó3

1Universidad Autónoma de Madrid (UAM), Madrid, Spain; 2Universidad de Barcelona, Facultad de Medicina, IDIBAPS., Barcelona, Spain; 3Universidad Autónoma de Madrid (UAM), Madrid, Spain, Madrid, Spain

Introduction: The outstanding potential of Extracellular Vesicles (EVs) in medicine deserves a detailed study of the molecular aspects regulating their incorporation into target cells. We have performed a Genome Wide CRISPR screen by flow cytometry sorting, to identify possible molecular candidates that may regulate EV uptake.

Methods: We have used a GWC cell library in K562 cells, which includes 10 sgRNAs for each gene of the whole human genome, plus negative controls, summing up a total of 250,000 individual sgRNAs. Total EVs were isolated from SKMEL147 melanoma cell line by ultracentrifugation at 100000g. EVs were characterized by NTA, Electron Microscopy, Western blot and confocal microscopy. EVs were stained with Alexa633‐C5‐Maleimide, a fluorescent compound able to covalently bind to sulphur residues of proteins and glycans, and the unbound compound was removed by passing the sample through a ExoSpinColumns (3000MW). For each assay 3.6x1012 of stained EVs were used. 500x106 cells were used per assay in order to have a 2000x coverage. After two hours of incubation with cells, the cell culture was washed and analysed with a flow cytometer for cellular sorting. 5% high and low fluorescence populations were sorted. Total genomic DNA was obtained, and sgRNAs sequences amplified, adding Illumina adaptors as overhangs, to perform NGS.

Results: The results point out to the COMMD and the WASH complex as possible candidates among others.

Summary/Conclusion: Quantitative EV uptake assays previously developed in the laboratory, based on Renilla Luciferase, will be used to validate the hits found in the Genome Wide CRISPR screen.

Funding: This project was funded by a GEIVEX mobility fellowship.

Keywords: Genome‐Wide CRISPR, EV uptake, flow cytometry

OS35.02.

## At which size does the membrane‐to‐lumen protein partition switch?

Paolo Bergese
1; Giorgia Guerra2; Krizia Sagini3; Tatyana Vagner2; Dolores Di Vizio2

1Università degli studi di Brescia, Brescia, Italy; 2Department of Surgery, Division of Cancer Biology and Therapeutics, Cedars‐Sinai Medical Center, Los Angeles, CA, USA, Los Angeles, USA; 3Institute for Cancer Research, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway

Introduction: Nanomaterials are characterized by amazing surface‐to‐volume ratio. Extracellular Vesicles (EVs) – which have been recently recognized as the universal agent of intercellular communication, being involved in many physiological and pathological processes and in interkingdom biochemical communication – are nanoparticles, but this key aspect has never been rationally addressed. We will present and discuss the first attempt to quantify the membrane‐to‐lumen (viz. the surface‐to‐bulk) partition of proteins in EVs.

Methods: A semi‐quantitative model, based on the available cellular compositional and microstructural data, was formulated. Large EVs were isolated from culture medium of human prostate cancer cells (PC3 cell line). EV separation and characterization was compliant with MISEV2018.

Results: The model allows for estimation of the partition between membrane (surface) associated and lumen (bulk) proteins, as well of the overall protein content of an EV, as a function of the EV size. Calculations identify a switch diameter at 180 nm, below which EVs carry more membrane than luminal proteins. At larger diameters the partition is reversed, surpassing 80% of luminal proteins for EVs with diameter larger 800 nm. The model has been successfully tested to analyze and describe a real preparation composed of subpopulations of small EVs and Large Oncosomes from human prostate cancer cells. Further latest results on the model validation will be also presented.

Summary/Conclusion: Overall, the model may improve experimental design and data analysis, including: (i) new ways of dosing EVs in vitro and in vivo, (ii) interpretation of EV biological effects and (iii) drawing protocols for EV engineering.

Funding: We acknowledge the grant H2020 FETPROACT 952183 “BOW‐ Biogenic Organotropic Wetsuits” and the grant NIH/NCI R01 CA234557.

Keywords: Extracellular Vesicles, Large Oncosome, Protein, Membrane, Nanoparticles, Bio‐nano Interfaces

OS35.03.

## Extracellular vesicle internalization by prostate cancer cells is cell cycle dependent and source‐independent

Anca D. Dobrian; Allison M. Mathiesen; Noel Miller; MIchael Brown

Eastern Virginia Medical School, Norfolk, USA

Introduction: Endothelial cells (EC) represent a large proportion of the tumor microenvironment and their EVs were shown to support malignant cell growth. Also, EC EVs represent an approach to target cancer cells for delivery of therapeutics. Therefore, understanding the uptake mechanisms and cellular processing of EVs is of prime importance. In this study we examined the internalization of EVs produced by microvascular ECs in absence or presence of inflammation by metastatic prostate cancer cells.

Methods: Primary microvascular EC were cultured with or without proinflammatory cytokines. EV were isolated from media by sequential centrifugation (500 x g, 10,000 x g and 2x at 100,000 x g). EV were characterized by EM, protein markers, and NTA. EV were labeled with lipophilic dyes and incubated with PC3ML cells for 4, 12 and 24 hours. To determine mechanisms of uptake we used dynasore, genistein, fillipin and nystatin. EV internalization, cell cycle and lysosomal co‐localization were assessed by imaging flow cytometry.

Results: EV internalization was cell cycle dependent, energy dependent and independent of EV type. The proportion of PC3ML that internalized EV was independent of time and concentration of EV. The average percentage of cells that internalized EVs was 59.4% of which 55% were in G0, followed by S phase(∼35%) and G2/M (∼10%). Internalization in G0 and S phases was ∼80% inhibited by both dynasore and genistein suggesting clathrin and caveolin‐mediate endocytosis. Internalization at G2/M was not significantly affected by any of the inhibitors. Further, only 10% of the EVs were found in the lysosomal compartment in all experimental conditions.

Summary/Conclusion: Proliferative cells have minimal EV internalization making their targeting by EV limited. Modulation of endocytosis has profound impact on EV internalization but not on lysosomal localization. Internalization mechanisms and kinetics are independent of the inflammatory environment of the EV producing cells.

Funding: American Heart Association

OS35.04.

## PDZ proteins interact with tetraspanins & syndecans and regulate the production, composition and uptake of extracellular vesicles
Monica Castro‐Cruz1; Anouk Oris2; George Daaboul3; Sofie Meeussen2; Lukas Hyka2; Raphael Leblanc4; Guido David5; Pascale Zimmermann
6

1KU leuven, Leuven, Belgium; 2KU Leuven, Leuven, Belgium; 3NanoView Biosciences, Boston, USA; 4CRCM, Marseille, France; 5Centre de Recherche en Cancérologie de Marseille, Marseille, France; 6KU Leuven, MARSEILLE 09, France

Introduction: PDZ proteins co‐evolved with multi‐cellularity and control multiple aspects of cell signaling and polarity. We previously established that, together with syndecan heparan sulfate proteoglycan, the PDZ protein syntenin is a rate‐limiting factor for the exchanges of small extracellular vesicles (sEV, specifically exosomes), stimulating both exosome biogenesis and uptake. Here, we evaluate whether additional proteins of the PDZ family might function in sEV biology.

Methods: We mapped interactions by yeast‐two‐hybrid; silenced the expression of 10 PDZ proteins by siRNA treatment of MCF‐7 and HEK293T cells; purified sEV by differential centrifugation, size‐exclusion chromatography and single particle interferometric reflectance imaging; characterized protein composition by western blot and immunocapture; studied subcellular localization and sEV uptake by immunocytochemistry and fluorescence microscopy; depleted membrane heparan sulfate by heparitinase treatment.

Results: We reveal that PDZ proteins establish multiple direct interactions with CD9, CD63, CD81 and with syndecan‐1/2/3/4. We illustrate that syndecans are selective markers of sEVs. We establish that PDZ proteins differentially control the number of sEVs and the exosomal accumulations of tetraspanins and syndecans. Intracellularly, PDZ proteins impact on tetraspanin/syndecan endosomal budding, subcellular localizations and steady‐state levels. Finally, we show that PDZ proteins differentially control the uptake of CD63 positive extracellular vesicles, most probably by regulating the cell surface levels of heparan sulfate.

Summary/Conclusion: Our study establishes that the extensive networking of syndecans, tetraspanins and PDZ proteins contributes to exosome heterogeneity and turnover, highlighting an important piece of the molecular framework governing intracellular trafficking and intercellular communication.

Funding: This work was supported by CONACYT, ANR‐18‐CE13‐0017 (Project SynTEV), FWO (G0C5718N & LK fellowship) and the internal funds of the KU Leuven (C14/20/105).

Keywords: tetraspanins, syndecans, PDZ proteins, EV production, EV composition, EV uptake

OS35.05.

## Single EV analysis for investigating EV biogenesis

Chungmin Han; Jaesung Park

POSTECH, Pohang, Republic of Korea

Introduction: The most widely accepted EV classification have suggested that EVs can be classified into subpopulations according to their biogenesis pathway, for example, exosomes, microvesicles, etc. However, due to the lack of appropriate EV analysis, the relationship between EV subpopulations and their biogenesis have not yet been clearly elucidated. Therefore, in this study, we utilized a single EV analysis techniques to investigate this relationship.

Methods: EVs were isolated from cultured HEK293 cells using differential centrifugation, and characterized according to the MISEV2018 guideline. EVs were analyzed using our TIRF‐based single EV analysis technique. Briefly, EVs were immobilized on a passivated glass surface and labeled with antibodies. EV signals from four different fluorescence channels were simultaneously acquired using Quad‐View optics system. Signals located within 3‐pixels were considered co‐localized. To compare tetraspanin expression profiles of EVs and virus‐like particles (VLPs), two different VLPs (HIV and CoV) were prepared according to the methods from highly cited literatures.

Results: Single EV tetraspanin (CD9, CD63 and CD81) expression analysis of WT HEK293 showed distinct correlations between the tetraspanins. CD9 and CD81 were mostly co‐expressed in the same vesicles, while half of CD63 were expressed alone. Following single EV analysis of HEK293 cell lines that express organelle markers showed CD9 and CD81 were more correlated with plasma membrane and early endosomes, while CD63 were more correlated with late endosomes and lysosomes. These results indicated that EVs expressing CD9 and CD81 might be originated from plasma membrane and EVs expressing CD63 might be originated from endosomal organelles. To test this hypothesis, HIV and CoV VLPs, whose biogenesis were well studied, were applied to the single vesicle analysis. The single HIV VLP analysis showed high correlation with the all three tetraspanins, while single CoV VLP did not show any correlations with the tetraspanins. Considering that the biogenesis of HIV in HEK293 is more correlated with ESCRT‐dependent and endosome related exosome biogenesis, EVs expressing all three tetraspanin at the same time might be the exosome populations.

Summary/Conclusion: We identified distinct EV subpopulations from the results of single EV tetraspanin analysis of HEK293 EVs. Following analysis using HEK293 cells expressing organelle specific fluorescence markers indicated that these subpopulations might have different subcellular origination. Single EV analysis VLPs provided an evidence that support our hypothesis: EVs expressing all three tetraspanins (CD9, CD63 and CD81) might be exosomes.

Funding: National Research Foundation of Korea (NRF) (2021R1I1A1A01060075)
Korea Medical Device Development Fund grant (202011B12).

Keywords: exosomes, microvesicles, viruses, biogenesis, single‐vesicle analysis

## PT12: Late‐Breaking Poster Session 1: Therapeutics

## Extracellular vesicles from clinically expired platelet concentrates as a targeted anti‐cancer drug delivery system
Ana Meliciano1; Daniela Salvador2; Ana Filipa D. Louro2; Pedro Mendonça3; Ana Paula Sousa3; Margarida Serra2

1iBET‐ITQB/NOVA, Oeiras, Portugal; 2iBET‐ITQB/NOVA, Lisboa, Portugal; 3IPST, Lisboa, Portugal

Introduction: Platelet concentrates (PC) have been widely used to treat hematological diseases, but due to their short shelf‐life, several pools of PC are periodically disposed. To value this product beyond its expiration date, alternative applications in regenerative medicine have been explored, particularly their use as a source of growth factors for (stem) ‐cell culture. It is known that large amounts of platelet‐derived extracellular vesicles (pEV) are released during PC storage but few studies have explored its applicability. In fact, an attractive interaction between tumor cells and platelets membrane integrins and receptors (e.g., CD62p, GPIIb/IIIa) has already been described, and thus, in this study, we evaluated the feasibility of using pEV as targeted drug‐delivery systems for cancer therapy.

Methods: pEV isolated by density gradient were loaded with the anticancer agent paclitaxel (PTX‐pEV) by direct incubation. pEV were characterized by nanoparticle tracking analysis, western blotting, and transmission electron microscopy. The bioactivity of PTX‐pEV was assessed on human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVEC) and metastatic breast cancer cells MDA‐MB‐231, by observing its effect on angiogenesis, migration, and invasion capability.

Results: pEV were successfully loaded with PTX and entrapment efficiencies of 10% were achieved. The biological efficacy of the engineered pEV was also validated as bioactivity assays showed a greater inhibition in wound closure ability of HUVEC (21%) treated with PTX‐pEV compared to free drug. Promising results were observed in treated MDA‐MB‐231, with a decrease in cell migration (44%) and invasion (22%) abilities.

Summary/Conclusion: These data unveiled for the first time, a novel valorization of PC with no therapeutic value, using pEV as PTX‐delivery systems. PTX‐pEV can potentially provide clinical advantages over free drug or synthetic nanocarriers, regarding both efficacy of delivery and reduction of off‐target effects.

PT12.02.

## Target‐specific delivery of small RNA cargos facilitated by BioDrone® platform technology
Sung‐Soo Park1; Yoonjin Baeg2; Eun Hyang Jang2; Dong Woo Han2; Jinhee Park2; Miyeon Choi2; Minchul Jung2; Kyeongeun Park2; Jeong Eun Park1; Hye Yeong Nam2; Seung Wook Oh1

1BioDrone® Research Institute, MDimune Inc., Seoul, Republic of Korea; 2BioDrone Research Institute, MDimune Inc., Seoul, Republic of Korea

Introduction: At MDimune, we are developing an innovative drug delivery platform technology, BioDrone®, using cell‐derived vesicles (CDVs) produced from diverse human cell sources by using our proprietary extrusion process. We have previously demonstrated that CDVs, while sharing many physical and biochemical properties with exosomes, can serve as effective biomolecular carriers with highly efficient drug loading capabilities and excellent cellular uptake and tissue penetration properties.

Methods: Small RNAs were loaded onto CDVs as efficiently as exosomes using the cholesterol conjugation method previously published. On the other hand, mRNAs were complexed with CDVs using charge interaction mediated by PEI. Finally, the surface of CDVs was modified by either engineering source cells to express the ligands specific to tumors or chemically conjugating them on the purified CDV membrane.

Results: The CDVs decorated with tumor‐specific ligands enhance the cellular uptake of CDVs to target tumor cells. We also examine how such engineered CDVs promote the delivery of RNA cargos and eventually lead to improved cellular response. Finally, such enhancement in gene modulation mediated by CDVs is compared to exosomes and other conventional gene delivery methods.

Summary/Conclusion: Along with the manufacturing‐scale extrusion process we established recently, the demonstration of targeted delivery of various gene cargos will propel the therapeutic applications of BioDrone® platform technology.

PT12.03.

## A CD73 enzyme activity assay for stability and dose‐finding of therapeutically‐active EVs and CDVs
Daniela Auer1; Tanja Schally1; Claudia Arzt2; Han Fang2; Eva Grasmann2; Carina Kals1; Elisabeth Bayer1; Eva Rohde3; Hui‐Chong Lau4; Seung Wook Oh4; Mario Gimona1

1Paracelsus Medizinische Privatuniversität Salzburg ‐ Privatstiftung, Salzburg, Austria; 2EVTT at Paracelsus Medizinische Privatuniversität Salzburg ‐ Privatstiftung, Salzburg, Austria; 3Transfusion Medicine at SALK / Paracelsus Medizinische Privatuniversität Salzburg ‐ Privatstiftung, Salzburg, Austria; 4BioDrone® Research Institute, MDimune Inc., Seoul, Republic of Korea

Introduction: The translation of nanovesicle‐based therapeutics into clinical application requires the definition and quantitative, reproducible analysis of criteria of bioactivity and stability. For naive un‐manipulated extracellular vesicles (EVs) or cell‐derived vesicles (CDVs) generated by serial extrusion of cells the definition of the active substance(s) remains difficult and the intrinsic heterogeneity of nanovesicle preparations prevents dose determination that is solely based on particle number or total protein mass. The GPI‐anchored 5’‐ectonucleotidase CD73 is an established identity marker for multipotent (mesenchymal) stromal cells (MSCs) and is abundantly present on umbilical cord MSC‐derived EVs and CDVs. The exposed position at the vesicle membrane and the delicate nature of the enzymatic activity position CD73 as a suitable surrogate molecule to establish a biochemical assay that meets the above criteria and that may assist in the control of storage stability and dose definition.

Methods: CD73 hydrolyzes AMP to adenosine and inorganic phosphate. Thus, the activity of the enzyme can be determined by measuring any of the three parameters. We measured CD73 activity in various research grade and GMP‐compliant human MSC‐EV and MSC‐CDV preparations via the consumption of AMP, essentially employing the commercial AMP‐Glo™‐Assay Kit (Promega) with some modifications. The concentration of non‐hydrolyzed AMP is proportional to the detected light generated by the luciferase reaction.

Results: Reproducibility and stability of the assay system was confirmed over a range of buffers (including phosphate‐containing systems) and the sensitivity was suitable for both naturally secreted EVs and extruded CDVs. We reproducibly obtained values of 2‐4 x 10‐8 mM Adenosine production / particle. Enzymatic activity was abolished by heat inactivation while vesicle aggregation or loss of particles was not observed by this treatment. Moreover, the detected enzymatic activity in correlation to the particle number was independent of the total protein concentration of the preparations.

Summary/Conclusion: The biological activity of a GPI‐anchored enzyme is sensitive to denaturing conditions and could indicate the deterioration of a given CD73+ nanovesicular preparation during the storage process. Excipients and co‐purifying protein components in the preparations do not seem to affect the activity and thus allows this assay to be used for a wide range of buffers and purification strategies.

Funding: Supported by the Projects P1812596 “EV‐TT” and 20102‐F1900731‐KZP “EV‐TT‐Bpro”.

Keywords: Biological Biochemical Activity Assay
Extracellular Vesicles
Cell Derived Vesicles
EV Mimetics
Stability
Dose Finding

PT12.04.

## Extracellular vesicles from adipose tissue‐derived stem cells alleviate osteoporosis through osteoprotegerin and miR‐21‐5p
Jeongmi Lee1; Kyoung Soo Lee2; Eunae Kim1; Ji Suk Choi2; Yong Woo Cho3; Dong‐Gyu Jo1

1Sungkyunkwan University, Suwon‐si, Republic of Korea; 2Exostemtech, Inc., Ansan‐si, Republic of Korea; 3ExoStemTech Inc., Seongdong‐gu, Republic of Korea

Introduction: Antiresorptive and anabolic agents are predominantly used as osteoporosis medications. However, the limited efficacy and undesirable side effects of these drugs are obstacles to osteoporosis treatment. Because stem cell‐derived extracellular vesicles are growing attention as novel cell‐free therapeutics that have advantages over parental stem cells, the therapeutic effects of extracellular vesicles from adipose‐derived stem cells (ASC‐EVs) on osteoporosis pathogenesis were investigated.

Methods: ASC‐EVs were isolated by a multifiltration system based on the tangential flow filtration system and characterized using transmission electron microscopy, dynamic light scattering, zeta potential, flow cytometry, cytokine arrays, and enzyme‐linked immunosorbent assay. Eight‐week‐old female ovariectomized mice were used for the in vivo experimental model of osteoporosis. Small RNA sequencing data were analyzed to identify miRNA candidates related to anti‐osteoporosis effects.

Results: Extracellular vesicles are rich in growth factors and cytokines related to bone metabolism and mesenchymal stem cell (MSC) migration. In particular, osteoprotegerin (OPG), a natural inhibitor of receptor activator of nuclear factor‐κB ligand, was highly expressed in ASC‐EVs. The intravenous administration of ASC‐EVs attenuated bone loss in osteoporosis mice. ASC‐EVs significantly inhibited osteoclast differentiation of macrophages and promoted the migration of bone marrow‐derived MSCs. However, OPG‐depleted ASC‐EVs did not show anti‐osteoclastogenesis effects, demonstrating that OPG is critical for the therapeutic effects of ASC‐EVs. Additionally, miR‐21‐5p in ASC‐EVs inhibited osteoclast differentiation through Acvr2a down‐regulation.

Summary/Conclusion: OPG and miR‐21‐5p in ASC‐EVs inhibit osteoclast differentiation and reduce gene expression related to bone resorption, suggesting that ASC‐EVs are highly promising as cell‐free therapeutic agents for osteoporosis treatment.

Funding: This work was supported by the National Research Foundation (NRF) under Grant NRF‐2018M3A9H1023767, NRF‐2019R1A5A2027340 ); Ministry of Food and Drug Safety under Grant (18172MFDS173); and the startup commercialization by MMS (B0080831000460, ASC‐derived EV). This work was also supported by a grant from the Ministry of Oceans and Fisheries’ R&D project, Korea (1525011845); and Korea Basic Science Institute (National Research Facilities and Equipment Center) grant funded by the Ministry of Education (2020R1A6C101A191).

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, Human adipose‐derived stem cells, Osteoprotegerin, Osteoporosis, miR‐21‐5p

PT12.05.

## Therapeutic effects of extracellular vesicles from human adipose‐derived stem cells in systemic sclerosis
Eunae Kim; Hark Kyun Kim; Jae Hoon Sul; Dong‐Gyu Jo

Sungkyunkwan University, Suwon‐si, Republic of Korea

Introduction: Systemic sclerosis (SSc) is an autoimmune disease characterized by inflammatory reactions and fibrosis. SSc causes severe damage and dysfunction of multiple organs, but there is no complete cure method. Myofibroblasts are considered as one of the therapeutic targets to prevent and reverse the pathogenesis of fibrosis in SSc. Although the mechanisms that differentiate into myofibroblasts are diverse, transforming growth factor‐beta (TGF‐β) is known as a key mediator in the fibrosis of SSc. Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are 30‐200 nm nano‐sized vesicles that cells secrete into the extracellular space.

Methods: EVs were isolated from conditioned media of hADSCs with tangential flow filtration (TFF) and characterized with nanoparticle tracking analysis(NTA), dynamic light scattering(DLS), and western blotting. Subcutaneous injection of bleomycin (100μl from a stock of 0.5mg/ml) to 6 weeks old male DBA/2 mice was continued every other day for 6 weeks. At 5 weeks after the first injection of bleomycin, ADSC‐EVs were administered subcutaneously three times on week 5 and week 6. Primary human dermal fibroblasts (HDFs) were treated for 24 hours with TGF‐β1 or ADSC‐EVs for in vitro studies.

Results: The therapeutic effects of EVs on in vivo SSc model were evaluated with dermal thickness and the number of alpha‐smooth muscle actin (α‐SMA) expressing cells through hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) staining and immunohistochemistry (IHC). ADSC‐EVs decreased dermal thickness and α‐SMA expressing cell number and the mRNA level of fibrotic genes such as α‐SMA, CTGF, Col1A1, COMP, LOX. In TGF‐β‐induced dermal fibroblasts, ADSC‐EVs also downregulated the mRNA and protein levels of α‐SMA, CTGF. Furthermore, ADSC‐EVs can inhibit the phosphorylation of SMAD2 and the nuclear localization of phosphor‐SMAD2. These results indicate that ADSC‐EVs block the TGF‐β/SMAD signaling pathway and ameliorate the fibrotic symptoms.

Summary/Conclusion: ADSC‐EVs have therapeutic effects on a bleomycin‐induced mouse model and TGF‐β induced human dermal fibroblasts in vitro. ADSC‐EVs downregulated the expression of fibrotic genes and decreased the accumulation of extracellular matrix. These results indicate that ADSC‐EVs can be a therapeutic approach for preventing and reversing SSc.

PT12.06.

## Cell reprogramming via extracellular vesicles derived from adipose‐derived stem cells during white/beige adipogenic differentiation
Jihoon Han1; Hark Kyun Kim2; Youn Jae Jung3; Yong Woo Cho4; Dong‐Gyu Jo2

1Sungkyunkwan University, Suwon, Republic of Korea; 2Sungkyunkwan University, Suwon‐si, Republic of Korea; 3ExoStemTech Inc., Ansan, Republic of Korea; 4ExoStemTech Inc., Seongdong‐gu, Republic of Korea

Introduction: Mesenchymal stem cells (MSCs) derived from human tissues such as bone marrow, adipose tissue, and placental tissue are widely used as therapeutic agents for tissue repair and regeneration. However, stem cells remain relatively rare after transplantation and differentiation is inefficient. On the other hand, stem cell‐derived extracellular vesicles (EVs) offer alternative approaches to stem cell‐based therapy for regenerative medicine. In this study, stem cell EVs derived during differentiation were used as cell‐free therapeutic systems to induce tissue‐specific differentiation.

Methods: EVs were isolated from human adipose‐derived stem cells (HASCs) during white and beige adipogenic differentiation (D‐EVs and BD‐EVs, respectively) via a multifiltration system based on the tangential flow filtration. Purified EVs were characterized by nanoparticle tracking analysis, dynamic light scattering, electrophoretic light scattering, and cryo‐TEM.

Results: Both D‐EVs and BD‐EVs successfully differentiated HASCs into white and beige adipocytes, respectively. D‐EVs were transplanted with collagen/methylcellulose hydrogels on the backs of BALB/c mice, and they produced numerous lipid droplets in injected sites. Treatments of BD‐EVs attenuated diet‐induced obesity through browning of adipose tissue in mice. Furthermore, high‐fat diet‐induced hepatic steatosis and glucose tolerance were improved by BD‐EVs treatment. The miRNA expression profile of EVs indicates that miRNAs contributing to brown fat cell differentiation are enriched in BD‐EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: These results reveal that secreted EVs during stem cell differentiation into white adipocytes or beige adipocytes can induce cellular differentiation, suggesting a potential for cell‐free therapeutic systems by inducing tissue‐specific differentiation in defective tissues.

Funding: This study was supported by a grant from the National R&D Program for Cancer Control, Ministry of Health and Welfare, Republic of Korea (1420390). This study was also supported by the National Research Foundation (NRF) funded by the Korean Government (NRF‐2019R1A2C3011422, NRF‐2019R1A5A2027340, NRF‐2018M3A9H1023767, and NRF‐2017R1A5A1070259), and the Industrial Core Technology Development Program (10078392) funded by the Ministry of Trade, Industry and Energy (MOTIE, Korea).

PT12.07.

## Antiinflammatory properties of ROS‐responsive tolerogenic dendritic cell‐derived exosomes in rheumatoid arthritis
Leon Felipe Palomera Sandoval1; Jae Hoon Sul2; Eun sook Lee3; Jae Hyung Park3; Dong‐Gyu Jo2

1SKKU, suwon, Republic of Korea; 2Sungkyunkwan University, Suwon‐si, Republic of Korea; 3skku, suwon, Republic of Korea

Introduction: Despite the emergence of tolerogenic dendritic cell‐derived exosomes (TolDex) as a novel treatment in models of rheumatoid arthritis (RA), their use is limited by the poor bioavailability after administration. Our group achieved lesion‐specific immunoregulation through reactive oxygen species‐responsive TolDex in RA by hydrophobic insertion of a polyethylene glycol (PEG)‐embedded thioketal (TK) linker on the surface of the TolDex. ROS overproduction causes enhanced cellular uptake of ROS‐responsive TolDEX (TKDex) through PEG detachment. This study shows the potential therapeutic application of TKDex in RA.

Methods: After Synthesis of thioketal (TK) linker TolDex were isolated from DC 2.4 cells cultured in conditioned media supplemented with exosome‐free FBS. Media were collected, centrifuged and then filtered using a 0.22‐μm filter, followed by a TFF system at 30 rpm with a TFF capsule of 300 kDa. Evs were mixed with the TK linker to form TKDEX and characterized using NMR, NTA, TEM and western blot. In vivo studies including biodistribution, ex‐vivo fluorescence and immune regulatory effects were carried out in collagen‐induced arthritis (CIA) 8‐week‐old male DBA1/J mice.

Results: CD40 downregulation as well as reduced secretion of pro‐inflammatory cytokines such as tumor necrosis factor α (TNF‐α) and interleukin 6 (IL‐6), were found in mature dendritic cells (mDCs) after TolDex and TKDex treatment. Following intravenous administration, PEG increased bioavailability of TKDex in the collagen induced arthritis (CIA) model, and enhanced joint accumulation. Regulatory T cells (Treg) expressing CD4/CD25/FOXP3 were induced as well as transforming growth factor ß elevation and IL‐6 reduction by TKDex.

Summary/Conclusion: An enhanced efficiency ROS‐sensitive TolDex was developed after investigation of the putative mechanism of action of TolDex. Interestingly, our results showed that not only a reduction in the pro‐inflammatory signals were achieved, but an anti‐inflammatory switch in the cell population occurred after CD40 downregulation. The RA microenviroment in the lesions was altered by citokyne regulation and Treg induction. This study might set a foundation for future works on stimuli‐ responsive exosomes for the treatment of disease.

Funding: Basic Science Research Programs (Grants number: 2019R1A5A2027340, 2020R1I1A1A01069178 and 2018R1A2B3006080) of the National Research Foundation (NRF) and the Korea Basic Science Institute (National Research Facilities and Equipment Center, 2020R1A6C101A191) grant from Ministry of Education, the Republic of Korea.
