PT12.08.

## Plant extracellular vesicles as a drug delivery system for humans
Joshua Farley1; Laura Pellegrini2; Madeline Lancaster2; Stephen Muench3; Vincent Postis4; Carine De Marcos Lousa1

1School of Health, Leeds Beckett University, Leeds, United Kingdom; 2MRC Laboratory of Molecular Medicine, University of Cambridge, Cambridge, United Kingdom; 3Faculty of Biological Science, University of Leeds, Leeds, United Kingdom; 4School of Life Sciences, University of Dundee, Dundee, United Kingdom

Introduction: Ever‐growing interest has focused on human EVs (hEVs) as a drug delivery system. Plant‐derived EVs (pEVs) are an appealing alternative to hEVs due to a low cost associated with their isolation, low immunogenicity, and high engineering potential with biopharming. Here, we investigate the potential of pEVs as a drug delivery system in human cells.

Methods: EVs were isolated from apoplastic fluids from Nicotiana Benthamiana (Nb) leaves using vacuum infiltration followed by differential ultracentrifugation. EVs were characterised by Nanoparticle tracking analysis (NS300‐ Malvern) and negative stain electron microscopy (NS‐EM). The nature of EVs was confirmed via western blot with the pEV‐specific markers PEN1 and TET8. Uptake of fluorescently stained pEVs by human cells (SH‐SY5Y) was analysed through flow cytometry with free stain as controls. Toxicity of pEVs on human cells was analysed through Ready Probes™ live/dead staining.

Results: Using NTA and NS‐EM, we confirmed that nanoparticles with expected vesicular size and morphology can be isolated from Nb leaf apoplastic fluids (plant extracellular medium). We then confirmed the presence of 2 distinct populations of EVs through western blotting: a SYP121/PEN1 population from the P40 fraction and a TET8 (CD63 orthologue) positive population from the P100‐40 fraction. We demonstrated that fluorescent total pEVs (P100) are taken up by human neuroblastoma cells and human brain organoids. Additionally, we have confirmed that there is no cytotoxic effect of pEV uptake in SH‐SY5Y cell culture.

Summary/Conclusion: We can purify pEVs from plant extracellular fluids successfully and show pEVs are taken up by human cells without cytotoxicity, shedding a positive light on the use of pEVs as a drug delivery system. Further characterisation forthcoming includes density gradients, proteomics, and lipidomics. Delivery of therapeutic cargo from siRNA loaded pEVs to human cells is ongoing.

PT12.09.

## Therapeutic effect of Engineered small extracellular vesicles displaying ACE2 variants on the surface against SARS‐CoV‐2 infection
JUNSIK KIM1; Hark Kyun Kim2; Dong‐Gyu Jo2

1SKKU, Suwon, Republic of Korea; 2Sungkyunkwan University, Suwon‐si, Republic of Korea

Introduction: Severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS‐CoV‐2) entry is mediated by the interaction of the viral spike (S) protein with angiotensin‐converting enzyme 2 (ACE2) on the host cell surface. Although a clinical trial testing soluble ACE2 (sACE2) for COVID‐19 is currently ongoing, our understanding of the delivery of sACE2 via small extracellular vesicles (sEVs) is still rudimentary. With excellent biocompatibility allowing for the effective delivery of molecular cargos, sEVs are broadly studied as nanoscale protein carriers. In order to exploit the potential of sEVs, we design truncated CD9 scaffolds to display sACE2 on the sEV surface as a decoy receptor for the S protein of SARS‐CoV‐2. Moreover, to enhance the sACE2‐S binding interaction, we employ sACE2 variants. sACE2‐loaded sEVs exhibit typical sEV characteristics and bind to the S protein. Furthermore, engineered sEVs inhibit the entry of wild‐type (WT), the globally dominant D614G variant, Beta (K417N‐E484K‐N501Y) variant, and Delta (L452R‐T478K‐D614G) variant SARS‐CoV‐2 pseudovirus, and protect against authentic SARS‐CoV‐2 and Delta variant infection. Of note, sACE2 variants harboring sEV show superior antiviral efficacy than WT sACE2 loaded sEV. The current findings provide opportunities for the development of new EV‐based antiviral therapeutics.

Methods: Plasmid construction Isolation and characterization of sEVs Cell culture and generation of stably transfected cell lines sEVs neutralization assay and authentic wildtype SARS‐CoV‐2 infection sEVs neutralization assay and authentic Delta variant SARS‐CoV‐2 infection ACE2/SARS‐CoV‐2 Spike S1 inhibitor screening assay Luciferase assay

Results: Characterization of engineered sACE2 sEVs. Binding properties of engineered sEVs Protection against WT and mutant S‐containing pseudovirus. Protection against authentic Delta variant SARS‐CoV‐2 infection.

Summary/Conclusion: EV engineering allows for displaying native proteins on the EV surface, with various potential therapeutic applications. Herein, we showed that CD9ΔTM4 scaffolds successfully presented sACE2 and its variants on the sEVs surface. These sEVs inhibited binding between S and ACE2 in a dose‐dependent manner by occupying available S protein as decoys. The infectivity of WT, D614G, Beta variant, and Delta variant mutant S‐containing pseudovirus was compromised by sACE2.v1 and sACE2.v2 sEVs. Furthermore, both sEVs potently blocked authentic SARS‐CoV‐2 (BetaCoV/Korea/KCDC03/2020 virus) and Delta variant infection in Vero E6 cells. Our observation that sACE2‐expressing sEVs markedly protected against S‐containing pseudovirus and authentic SARC‐CoV‐2 infection further support the potential of engineered sEVs as an efficient platform for the delivery of therapeutics.

## Using Nanobody displaying EVs to target the Transferrin Receptor
Philip C. Hewlett1; Arianna Coluzzi1; Heena Sharma2

1Evox Therapuetics, Oxford, United Kingdom; 2Evox Therapeutics Limited, Oxford Science Park, Oxford, UK., Oxford, United Kingdom

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EV) are naturally occurring particles that have inherent tropism for specific cells or organs. Observations from bio‐distribution experiments show (CAP)‐derived EVs are taken up predominantly by the liver and spleen. Antibodies against the transferrin receptor (TFR) have been widely shown to carry drugs to the CNS across the BBB. The aim of this study was to assess whether TFR targeting moieties can be used to redirect CAP‐derived EVs to cells of the CNS.

Methods: To this end, TFR‐targeting nanobodies were fused at the N‐terminal of the EV membrane protein Lamp2b, whereas the C‐terminal was fused to the reporter protein eGFP. Following transient transfection of CAP cells, NanoFCM and eGFP quantification were utilised to determine a ligand density of 500 molecules per EV in our engineered population of vesicles; with functional display targeting moieties confirmed with ELISA. Next, the ability of EV‐expressed nanobodies against TFR to facilitate uptake in TFR expressing cell lines was assessed through high content imaging.

Results: Both cell and cell‐free methods showed a dose‐dependent increase in receptor engagement with targeted over control EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: In the next steps, we will explore different ligand densities, scaffolds, and cell sources to see if binding and cellular uptake can be further improved.

Keywords: EVs, Nanobodies, TFR, High Content Microscopy, NanoFCM, ELISA

PT12.12.

## Hybrid extracellular vesicles for RNA delivery
Xiaoqin Wang1; Kristina Friis2; Andreia M. Silva3; Niek Dekker3

1Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Gothenburg, Sweden, Gothenburg, Sweden; 2Advanced Drug Delivery, Pharmaceutical Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Gothenburg, Sweden, Gothenburg, Sweden; 3Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D AstraZeneca, Gothenburg, Sweden, Molndal, Sweden

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are naturally secreted nanovesicles carrying bioactive molecules and show to mediate cell‐to‐cell communication. EVs possess intrinsic features, such as low immunogenicity, innate tropism and capacity to cross physiological barriers, which emerge EV as an attractive drug delivery vehicle. Different approaches have been investigated to engineer EV for loading of diverse cargoes. Nevertheless, challenges such as low loading efficiency still remain. Here, we develop a novel approach to load and deliver RNA via hybrid EVs.

Methods: EVs purified from Expi293F cells were fused with lipid nanoparticles (LNP) carrying Cre mRNA. The resulting mixed particles are referred to as hybrid EV (HEV). Various parameters were evaluated to optimize the formulation of HEVs. The size profile, protein composition and Cre mRNA encapsulation efficiency were analyzed by nanoparticle tracking analysis, Western blot, immuno‐electronic microscopy and Qubit RNA HS assay. The functional mRNA delivery by HEV and by LNPs were tested in a Cre reporter assay. A cell viability assay was used to determine the cell tolerability.

Results: Total particle concentration and EV‐LNP ratio are key parameters influencing the encapsulation efficiency, yield and properties of HEVs. HEVs displayed increased size and carried components of EV and LNP. Cre mRNA was successfully loaded in HEVs and functionally delivered to Cre reporter cells. Moreover, HEVs showed excellent cell tolerability and outperformed delivery efficiency of LNPs at high dose.

Summary/Conclusion: We developed a novel approach for effectively loading of RNA via HEVs. HEV is a promising strategy for functional delivery of RNA with the advantages of balancing safety and efficacy.

## 3D bioreactor culture in chemically‐defined medium for MSC‐EV production optimization for clinical translation
Yvan Courageux1; Alba López‐Fernández2; Cristina Prat‐Vidal2; Luciano Rodríguez2; Silvia Torrents3; Carolina Gálvez‐Montón1; Antoni Bayes‐Genis4; Joaquim Vives2; Sergi Querol2; Santiago Roura4; Marta Monguió‐Tortajada4

1ICREC Research Program, Health Science Research Institute Germans Trias i Pujol, Can Ruti Campus, Badalona, Spain, Barcelona, Spain; 2Servei de Teràpia Cel·lular, Banc de Sang i Teixits, Barcelona, Spain, Barcelona, Spain; 3Laboratori Cel·lular, Banc de Sang i Teixits, Barcelona, Spain, Barcelona, Spain; 4ICREC Research Program, Health Science Research Institute Germans Trias i Pujol, Can Ruti Campus, Badalona, Spain, Badalona, Spain

Introduction: Mesenchymal Stromal Cell‐derived Extracellular Vesicles (MSC‐EV) have increasing clinical interest as new versatile therapy against broad human conditions related to immune modulation and tissue regeneration. Nevertheless, the optimum MSC culture scale up fostering potent EV secretion is still a major concern. Also, the implemented strategy has to be designed for reproducibility and meet standards of clinical manufacturing.

Methods: We comparatively analyzed the production efficiency of EV derived from clinical‐grade Wharton jelly‐derived MSC (MSC,WJ) cultured in 2D scale up cell culture flasks or 3D microcarriers‐based bioreactor using either serum‐supplemented medium (SER) or chemically‐defined medium (CHEM). Cell growth, viability and phenotype were monitored for each condition and MSC,WJ‐EV were purified by size exclusion chromatography after concentration by ultrafiltration or tangential flow filtration. Next, MSC,WJ‐EV were characterized in terms of identity, purity, yield and functional activity to better design MSC,WJ‐EV scale‐up production, and to assess the suitability of the delineated analytical methods.

Results: MSC,WJ expressed a stable phenotype across the different culture settings. Cell recovery was lower in both CHEM conditions as well as in both 3D cultures, although aggregate formation difficulted cell harvest in the latter. Purified MSC,WJ‐EV were recovered from each condition, and bead‐based flow cytometry confirmed expression of both EV and MSC markers. CHEM‐based cultures yielded higher EV protein and RNA quantities relative to cell numbers and culture volumes. In terms of immunomodulatory activity, EV derived from 3D cultures showed greater inhibition of T cell proliferation, and 3D‐CHEM condition further correlated to superior CD73 enzyme activity.

Summary/Conclusion: While 2D and SER culture conditions yielded higher cell production, 3D‐CHEM culture conditions enabled MSC,WJ‐EV harvests with superior immunomodulatory potential.

Funding: MICINN (Madrid, Spain) ‐ PID2019‐110137RB‐I00 / PRE2020‐095357 ; Instituto de Salud Carlos III (Madrid, Spain) ‐ ICI20/00135 / RD21/0017/0022

Keywords: Bioreactor, Immunomodulation, Production optimization, Chemically defined

## This abstract is about the programmed delivery and stimulate neural differentiation of Ginseng‐derived EVs
Lihua Peng1; Minhong Tan2; Maoze Wang1

1College of Pharmaceutical Sciences, Zhejiang University, Hangzhou, China (People's Republic); 2School of Materials Science and Engineering, Zhejiang University, Hangzhou, China (People's Republic)

Introduction: Inducing the neural differentiation of endogenous mesenchymal stem cells (MSCs) is a momentous strategy for promoting neural regeneration. The combined‐delivery of biological‐electrical cues has exhibited great efficiency in neurogenesis and repair. However, biological factors have always been scarce due to low delivery efficiency or immunological risk, and recently plant‐derived Exos have been explored that may have great potential in promoting neural differentiation of MSCs. Moreover, the inconvenience and immune risk of traditional electrode have always been great obstacles. Herein, a novel nanoplatform for delivering biological signals to efficiently induce rapid nerve regeneration based on ginseng‐derived EVs (G‐Exos) was constructed, assisted by self‐powered electrical signals to further accelerate sensation restoration.

Methods: The directed neural differentiation efficiency and cells genetic alterations of MSCs by the isolated G‐Exos were investigated in vitro. The effects and mechanism of nerve regeneration and sensation restoration by delivering biological‐electrical signals were evaluated in vivo.

Results: G‐Exos has been confirmed to accelerate the neural differentiation of MSCs by activating the Pi3k‐Akt signaling pathway, increasing the expression of neurotrophic factors, and promoting the activation of Ras signaling pathway. On this basis, electrical signals further promote nerve regeneration and sensory conduction recovery through calcium signaling pathway.

Summary/Conclusion: The results demonstrated that G‐Exos promote the neural restoration and increase the nerve regeneration by delivering biological signals. In this sense, plant‐Exos possess great potential to be a promising nanoplatform for disease therapy, along with the higher production yield, simple fabrication, and less immunological risk. Furthermore, the programmed delivery of multiple biological‐electrical cues also grants a universal strategy for regenerating tissues and organs.

Keywords: Ginseng‐derived EVs, bioloigcal‐electrical cues, programmed delivery, neural restoration

PT12.15.

## Exosomes mediated delivery of Recombinant Human Interleukin 24 for breast cancer
Muhammad I. Amirzada1; Hamid Bashir2

1Department of Pharmacy, COMSATS University Islamabad, Abbottabad, Pakistan; 2Centre for Applied Molecular Biology, University of The Punjab, Lahore, Pakistan, Lahore, Pakistan

Introduction: Interleukin 24 (IL24) has drawn attention as an antiproliferative and antitumor activity. IL24 has ability to induce the specific death of cancer cells. Application of viral vectors for delivery of recombinant interleukins reported toxicity and immunogenicity. Exosomes are cell secreted nanoparticles play an important role in intercellular communication. Exosomes has potential to targeted drug/gene delivery due to innate stability, low immunogenicity, and excellent tissue/cell penetration. The aim of the current study was to develop exosomes mediated Recombinant Human Interleukin 24 (Exo‐IL24) formulation for breast cancer.

Methods: Exosomes were isolated from plasma by modifying the isolation method of Wu X et al 2019. The isolation procedure involves ultracentrifugation and dialysis. Plasma was ultracentrifuged to remove unwanted contaminating components. Furthermore, ultrafiltration was carried out on differentially centrifuged plasma by dialysis. Exo‐IL24 nanoparticles were formulated by the modified calcium chloride transfection method of Jin Y et al 2017. Nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA), scanning electron microscopy (SEM) and SDS‐PAGE were used to characterize free exosomes and Exo‐IL24. The cytotoxicity of Exo‐IL24 was determined by MTT assay on MCF‐7 cells.

Results: SDS‐PAGE of free exosomes revealed the expression of CD1, CD63, CD81 and LAMP1. Nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) confirms that the particle size of Exo‐IL24 was 203 nm larger than free exosomes. The typical structure of the exosome can be seen in SEM. After loading with IL24, their size is greater than that of free exosomes. The cytotoxicity test showed a decrease in cell viability with increasing concentration of Exo‐IL24.

Summary/Conclusion: Exosome's incorporation in the delivery of Interleukin 24 to breast cancer cells resulted to modify the release profile and improve IC50 of IL24. Exosomes mediated Recombinant Human Interleukin 24 formulation could be a promising strategy to combat the breast cancer via targeted delivery to cancerous cells.

Funding: Self

Keywords: Exosomes, Interleukin 24, Breast cancer

## Development of an exosome‐based therapy with skeletal muscle targeting capabilities for Pompe disease
Althea R. Lobo1; Claudia Hill1; Lydia Kgosana1; Sally Allison1; Lorenzo Errichelli1; David Lowe1; Justin Hean2; Mario Buono1; Virginia Castilla‐Llorente1

1Evox Therapeutics, Oxford, United Kingdom; 2Evox Therapeutics Limited, Oxford, UK., Oxford, United Kingdom

Introduction: Pompe disease is a rare inherited lysosomal storage disease in which lysosomal glycogen catabolism is reduced or depleted due to deficiency in acid‐alpha‐glucosidase (GAA). The current approved enzyme replacement therapy (ERT) has clear therapeutic benefit; however, it poorly targets to skeletal muscle. Consequently, higher doses are administered leading to increased immunogenicity. To overcome these challenges, we aimed to develop a potential therapeutic candidate based on skeletal muscle targeted exosomes loaded with GAA.

Methods: Different exosome engineering strategies were utilised in order to load GAA either in the lumen or on the surface of exosomes (Exo‐GAA EVs), as well as to target the exosomes to skeletal muscle. The plasmids encoding for the engineered proteins were transfected into human cells and the exosomes were purified following Evox proprietary/standard methods. The purified material was subjected to a thorough characterization following ISEV guidelines and assessed for functional delivery in vitro.

Results: When compared with an equivalent dose of the current ERT, Exo‐GAA EVs showed a similar significant recovery of C2C12 GAA‐/‐ myotubes. Strikingly, the inclusion of an M12 muscle targeting peptide on the surface of the Exo‐GAA EVs increased post‐uptake GAA activity and led to an increase in glycogen removal in comparison with the current ERT.

Summary/Conclusion: The results highlight the potential of engineered exosomes as a modality for delivery of therapeutic proteins with the added capability to increase targeting to specific tissues often hard to reach, such as skeletal muscle.

Funding: Funded by Takeda.

Keywords: Protein loading, Targeting, Skeletal Muscle, Pompe disease, Drug delivery.

PT12.17.

## Exosomal delivery of celastrol for lung cancer management
Farrukh Aqil1; Raghuram Kandimalla2; Disha Moholkar2; Mohd Saeed3; Ahmad Almatroudi4; Ramesh C. Gupta5

1University of Louisville, Louisville, USA; 2University of Louisville, Louisville, KY 40202, Louisville, USA; 3College of Sciences, University of Hail, PO BOX. 2240, Hail, Saudi Arabia, Hail, Saudi Arabia; 4Department of Medical Laboratories, College of Applied Medical Sciences, Qassim University, Qassim 51431, Saudi Arabia., Qassim, Saudi Arabia; 53P biotechnologies and Department of Pharmecology & Toxicology and Brown Cancer Center, University of Louisville, Louisville, KY 40202, Louisville, USA

Introduction: Cancer is a growing concern and a leading cause of deaths worldwide. Celastrol (CEL), a triterpenoid, has attracted significant attention for its strong anticancer properties. However, due to low bioavailability and poor pk parameters, it has been restricted from clinical applications. Exosomes are natural nanoparticles with inherent properties such as stability, biocompatibility, low immunogenicity and ability to overcome biological barriers. Owing to these superior advantages, exosomes have been utilized as potential drug carriers to overcome the challenges of oral delivery of CEL and other drugs. In this report, we demonstrate the antiproliferative and colony forming inhibition activity of CEL and its exosomal formulations against drug‐sensitive and drug‐resistant lung cancer cells and inhibition of lung tumors by oral administration of ExoCEL.

Methods: Exosomes were isolated from bovine milk by differential centrifugation and characterized for size by Zetasizer and AFM and protein markers by Western blot. Cells were treated with CEL and ExoCEL and cell survival and inhibition of colony formation was measured by MTT assay and trypan blue staining, respectively. For antitumor activity, s.c. lung tumor‐bearing mice were treated orally with CEL, ExoCEL or vehicle; all regimen were given 3‐times a week orally and tumor growth was monitored. The mechanism of CEL activity was analyzed by RNAseq analysis followed by confirmation with western blot.

Results: CEL when tested in A549 and H1299 lung cancer cells showed time‐ and dose‐dependent antiproliferative activity with IC50 values of 0.63 and 1.8 μM against A549 and H1299 cells. The activity was enhanced with ExoCEL. The ExoCEL also showed enhanced efficacy in inhibition of A549 colony formation, would healing and cell migration assay. The ExoCEL enhanced therapeutic response compared to the CEL in tumor xenograft model. The ExoCEL showed 77% reduction of s.c. tumor growth while CEL provided 52% inhibition compared to vehicle treatment. Mechanistically, ExoCEL and CEL suppressed TGF‐β1 and TNF‐α induced NF‐κB activity and showed effect on multiple markers related to cell cycle, EMT signaling, PI3K‐Akt signaling, p53 signaling and pathways related to lung cancer and its metastasis.

Summary/Conclusion: Orally delivered ExoCEL formulation exhibit significantly higher inhibition of lung tumors compared with free CEL. Thus, oral delivery of CEL could potentially be developed against lung and other cancers to achieve a user‐friendly, cost‐effective alternative of the traditional bolus dose i.v. therapy which carries severe side effects. Furthermore, exosomal formulation of poorly bioavailable compounds such as CEL can achieve significant biological effects presumably by enhancing its bioavailability and sustained release.

Funding: Supported From RDO grant # RDO‐2003.

Keywords: Milk exosomal, oral delivery, celastrol, lung cancer.

PT12.18.

## Engineered Cas9 extracellular vesicles as a novel gene editing tool
Xabier Osteikoetxea1; Andreia M. Silva2; Elisa Lázaro‐Ibáñez3; Nikki Salmond4; Olga Shatnyeva3; Josia Stein4; Jan Schick4; Stephen Wren5; Julia Lindgren6; Mike Firth7; Alexandra Madsen8; Lorenz M. Mayr9; Ross Overman10; Rick Davies4; Niek Dekker2

1Semmelweis University ‐ Hungarian Centre of Excellence for Molecular Medicine, Budapest, Hungary; 2Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D AstraZeneca, Gothenburg, Sweden, Molndal, Sweden; 3Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Gothenburg, Sweden, Gothenburg, Sweden; 4Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Alderley Park, United Kingdom, Alderley Park, United Kingdom; 5Global Product Development, Pharmaceutical Technology & Development, AstraZeneca, Macclesfield, United Kingdom, Alderley Park, United Kingdom; 6Translational Genomics, Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Gothenburg, Sweden., Gothenburg, Sweden; 7Quantitative Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Cambridge, UK, Cambridge, United Kingdom; 8Genome Engineering, Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Gothenburg, Sweden, Gothenburg, Sweden; 9Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Cambridge, United Kingdom, Cambridge, United Kingdom; 10Discovery Biology, Discovery Sciences, BioPharmaceuticals R&D, AstraZeneca, Alderley Park, United Kingdom, Cambridge, United Kingdom

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have shown promise as biological delivery vehicles, but therapeutic applications require efficient cargo loading. Here, we developed new methods for CRISPR/Cas9 loading into EVs through reversible heterodimerization of Cas9‐fusions with EV sorting partners.

Methods: Cas9‐loaded EVs were collected from engineered Expi293F cells using standard methodology, characterized using nanoparticle tracking analysis, western blotting, and transmission electron microscopy and analyzed for CRISPR/Cas9‐mediated functional gene editing in a Cre‐reporter cellular assay.

Results: As we have shown earlier, light‐induced dimerization using Cryptochrome 2 combined with CD9 or a Myristoylation‐Palmitoylation‐Palmitoylation lipid modification resulted in efficient loading with approximately 25 Cas9 molecules per EV and high functional delivery with 51% gene editing of the Cre reporter cassette in HEK293 cells. New data shows that this approach could also reach 25% editing if the Cre reporter cassette in HepG2 cells as well as targeting knock‐down of the therapeutically relevant PCSK9 gene with 6% indel efficiency. Importantly, Cas9 transfer was detergent‐sensitive and associated with the EV fractions after size exclusion chromatography, indicative of EV‐mediated transfer.

Summary/Conclusion: Considering the advantages of EVs over other delivery vectors we envision that this study will prove useful for a range of therapeutic applications, including CRISPR/Cas9 mediated genome editing.

Funding: This work was funded by AstraZeneca with a part of support from the European Union's Horizon 2020 Research and Innovation Programme under grant agreement No 739593.

Keywords: extracellular vesicles, exosomes, CRISPR/Cas9 delivery, gene editing, optogenetics

PT12.19.

## Exosome‐derived lipid nanoparticles for mRNA delivery
Minsub Chung

Hongik University, Bryn Mawr, USA

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs), primarily exosomes and microvesicles, hold great promise for development of new disease diagnostic techniques, drug delivery systems, and therapeutics. Exosomes can be biocompatible natural carriers of mRNA, which successfully cross the cellular membrane and bypass drug delivery obstacles. However, studies on the roles and functions of these exosome components have often encountered difficulties due to the complexity of the EVs, and the need for an artificial lipid membrane model with high reliability and utility is emerging.

Methods: HEK293T cell line, known to their high transfection efficiency, was used as source of EVs, which were prepared by sequential ultracentrifugation using the cell culture supernatant. We isolated 3 different fractions of vesicles by collecting the pellets upon 2.000xg, 12.200xg and 120.000xg spins. Each fraction of EVs were treated with 20% ethanol and ionizable lipids, followed by FLuc mRNA. After buffer exchange, integrity of different EV fractions was studied by electron microscopy, dynamic light scattering (DLS), and zeta‐potential. The mRNA encapsulation was measured by Ribogreen assay. The mRNA transfection and translation were visualized and examined by luciferase assay.

Results: EVs from different spin fraction with different average size became similar size in range of 100 ∼ 300 nm after mRNA treatment and buffer exchange. Their wide size distribution was confirmed by TEM and showed the expected size distribution by DLS. Over 80% of mRNA was encapsulated. Preliminary data showed that the mRNA‐modified EVs were successfully delivered, and their luciferase activity was proportional to the amount of mRNA.

Summary/Conclusion: A new engineering exosomes methods developed to utilize them as RNA drug delivery carriers. The physical properties, encapsulation and transfection profile of the engineered exosomes also are provided.

Keywords: Exosome, drug delivery, lipid nanoparticles, mRNA

## PT13: Late‐Breaking Poster Session 2: Technologies and Methods I

## Chair: Veronika Kralj Igič, n/a – Biomedical Research Group, Faculty of Health Sciences, University of Ljubljana, SI‐1000 Ljubljana, Slovenia

## Chair: Olesia Gololobova, n/a – Center for Nanotechnology in Drug Delivery, Eshelman School of Pharmacy, University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill

PT13.01.

## Differential detection of cancer‐derived extracellular vesicles using combined antibody functionalized magnetic beads and infrared spectroscopy
Gregory E. Rice1; Thomas Stewart2; Tamkin Ahmadzada3; Emily Stein4; Peter French5; Michelle Wood6; Joonsup Lee3; Elham Hosseini‐Beheshti7; Georges Grau7; Peter Lay8; Sophia Amerena‐Cowie2

1INOVIQ Limited, Notting Hill, Australia; 2University of Sydney, Sydeny, Australia; 3Sydney University, Sydeny, Australia; 4Inoviq Limited, Minneapolis, USA; 5Sydney University, Ballina, Australia; 6Sydney University, Sydney, Australia; 7School of Medical Sciences, The University of Sydney, Camperdown, NSW, Australia, Sydney, Australia; 8Sydney Univerity, sydeny, Australia

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EV) play a pivotal role in cellular functions including signalling pathways involved in the maintenance of normal physiological processes. EVs surface antigens and molecular cargo are altered in response to challenges to cellular homoeostasis and disease onset and progression. Rapid and simple methods for isolating EVs and characterizing their cell type‐specific biomarkers and molecular cargo are requisite to realizing their diagnostic potential. In this study, cancer cell‐derived EVs were isolated using antibody functionalized magnetic beads and infrared spectroscopy was used to identify cell and phenotypic biomarkers. In particular, changes in the concentrations of biomolecular classes were identified that separated EVs from different cancer cell lines into different regions of the principal component analysis (PCA) scores plots.

Methods: EVs were isolated from lung cancer cell line A549 (unstimulated and IFN‐gamma stimulated) and the mesothelial cell line MeT‐5A cell‐conditioned media using EXO‐NET® (according to manufacturer's instructions). EV‐loaded beads (5 μl) was placed on a CaF2 window then air‐dried. Vibrational spectra were recorded (Bruker Lumos, Tensor or Alpha FTIR spectrometers). The Alpha and Tensor instruments measured spectra from the entire dried drop, whereas point maps across individual dried drops were recorded with the Lumos instrument. The latter enabled an examination of variability across the dried sample. Spectra were vector‐normalized to the 1800‐1050 cm‐1 region and subjected to the Savitsky‐Golay algorithm with 13 smoothing points before PCA.

Results: The spectra from both cell lines and unstimulated vs stimulated A549 cells were clearly differentiated from one another, as well as the spectrum from the beads alone. The mapping experiments showed that random points across the dried drop of EXO‐NET bead samples (loaded and unloaded) had less variation than those between different cell lines and treatment groups when appropriate spectral regions were chosen for the analysis. Spectra of EVs isolated using sucrose gradients were confounded by FTIR sucrose peaks and not‐fit‐for‐purpose.

Summary/Conclusion: On‐bead analysis using vibrational spectroscopy provides a rapid and simple method for determining the relative concentrations of EV biomolecular classes. Distinct clustering of the spectra by sample type occurred, indicating that the different sub‐populations of cell‐derived exosomes could be differentiated using the vibrational spectroscopic method. The data obtained evidence the utility of EXO‐NET combined with FTIR to differentiate cancer type from the position in PC1 and the phenotype from the position along PC2, both of which are important diagnostically and in triage to treatment.

Funding: na

Keywords: EV isolation infrared spectroscopy

PT13.02.

## A novel tool for extracellular vesicle characterization: interferometric nanoparticle tracking analysis (iNTA)
Martin Blessing; Anna Kashkanova; André Gemeinhardt; Vahid Sandoghdar

Max Planck Institute for the Science of Light, Erlangen, Germany

Introduction: Nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) is one of the most widely used techniques for EV characterization; the label‐free method works with small volumes, is very user‐friendly, and provides realistic size distributions. However, it falls short of expectations regarding resolution, the limit of detection, and the ability to identify EVs in complex media. Here, we present a novel tool for precise particle sizing, matching up with electron microscopy (EM) resolution. Moreover, based on their refractive indices, we distinguish urinary EVs from co‐isolates and provide additional information about their inner composition.

Methods: In iNTA, the single‐particle tracking was performed with an interferometric scattering microscope (Taylor & Sandoghdar 2019), resulting in an enhanced particle signal even at high frame rates (5kHz). We inferred size distributions and refractive indices from trajectory analysis and signal strength. Measurements of iNTA and classic NTA were compared using (i) inorganic particles, (ii) liposomes, and (iii) human urine after centrifugation. We identified urinary EVs by EM and western blot. The urine sample was further treated with detergent buffer and proteinase K.

Results: In contrast to classic NTA, our interferometric approach resolved polydispersions of gold nanoparticles < 30nm and provided size distributions comparable with EM. In human urine, iNTA revealed two subpopulations based on their refractive indices. Upon treatment, the sub‐populations differed in their response, suggesting one to be urinary EVs. Based on a spherical model, we determined their inner refractive index to be 1.373, corresponding to a protein content of about 13%. This finding was validated by measuring liposomes prepared in water.

Summary/Conclusion: iNTA can reliably identify EVs from other co‐isolates in complex media, demonstrating the potential to become the next‐generation particle sizer for extracellular vesicle research.

Funding: AK acknowledges funding from Alexander von Humboldt‐Stiftung

Keywords: NTA, iNTA, urinary EV, size, refractive index, liposome

PT13.03.

## Isolation of extracellular vesicles with high purity using mixed mode resin (MMR) method
Wendy Trieu1; Dmitry Ter‐Ovanesyan2; Bogdan Budnik3; George Church3; David Walt3

1Stanford, Stanford, USA; 2Harvard University, Cambridge, USA; 3Wyss Institute for Biologically Inspired Engineering, Boston, USA

Introduction: It is difficult to separate extracellular vesicles (EVs) from free proteins in biofluids such as plasma. We set out to develop a method for high purity EV isolation for applications such as mass spectrometry.

Methods: Therefore we developed a novel, high purity EV isolation method which uses mixed mode resin (MMR) traditionally used in chromatography columns in a slurry format. We optimized our MMR Slurry method to work by measuring the tetraspanins CD9, CD63, CD81, and albumin, as well as total protein to find the optimal parameters for isolation.

Results: We optimized our MMR Slurry isolation method to isolate EVs from human CSF as a one‐step protocol and plasma as a two‐step protocol after size exclusion chromatography (SEC) with high purity. We found that we retain high levels of EVs while eliminating quantifiable albumin. Furthermore, we can tune the purity by changing the ratio of resin to free protein.

Summary/Conclusion: We have developed a novel method, MMR Slurry, for isolation of EVs from biofluids with high yield and purity. This method is well suited for mass spectrometry on clinically relevant volumes of biofluids and can be scaled for large numbers of patient samples.

Funding: Chan Zuckerberg Initiative; Open Philanthropy Project; Wyss Institute

PT13.04.

## Development of microsphere‐based solid support for the separation of extracellular vesicles and suppression of non‐specific binding
Dario Brambilla; Marcella Chiari

National Research Council of Italy ‐ Institute of Chemical Sciences and Technology (CNR ‐ SCITEC), Milan, Italy

Introduction: To evaluate the biological roles of specific extracellular vesicles (EVs) subpopulations, the choice of a proper separation protocol is mandatory. Especially when dealing with low represented EVs subpopulations, the need for enrichment strategies that display high specificity and selectivity is crucial. Contamination might result in misleading and data.
The immuno‐affinity separation of EVs offers unmatched selectivity. The use of antibodies allows the recognition of surface antigens that potentially discriminate different populations of EVs. Unfortunately, antibodies represent only one of the factors that impact on the presence of contaminants. Indeed, the characteristics of the solid surface are equally important. The ideal surface should provide an aqueous‐like environment, an optimal orientation, and accessibility of probes, and possess antifouling properties to prevent non‐specific adhesion of biomolecules.
Within this work, the polymer MCP‐6 has been applied in the coating of microspheres. The solid support, used to immobilize biomolecules, provides the efficient separation of EVs with significant reduction of contaminants.

Methods: Microspheres were coated using MCP‐6, a polymer functionalized with azide groups. The beads were functionalized with biomolecules exploiting the strain‐promoted azide alkyne cicloaddition (SPAAC).
Beads were used to immobilize DNA antibody conjugates (DACs), consisting of a DNA able to direct the protein on the surface of beads, and an EV‐directed antibody.
Finally, DACs‐functionalized beads were used to perform the separation of EVs.

Results: Incubation of microspheres with a solution of MCP‐6 provided a stable coating. Polymeric coating confers antifouling properties to microspheres towards proteins with different isoelectric points.
MCP‐6 coated beads can immobilize biomolecules modified with dibenzocyclooctine groups (DBCO). Probes retain their activity after immobilization, and their density on the surface can be finely tuned.
Beads functionalized with biological probes were used to immobilize DACs, and used to separate EVs from complex media.

Summary/Conclusion: A novel strategy to provide the coating of beads with an antifouling copolymer was developed. The resulting surface, exposing azido groups, can be used to immobilize DBCO‐functionalized biomolecules exploting SPAAC reaction. Additionally, the surface ensures exceptional antifouling properties, essential in reducing non‐specific binding on the surface.
Different biomolecules have been immobilized on the surface and were used to capture DACs. Thanks to DACs, EVs were separated from complex biological media as intact vesicles which can be further analyzed using imaging techniques or even destroyed to analyze cargo composition.

Funding: This work was supported by the European Union Horizon 2020 under Grant 766466: INDEX, Integrated Nanoparticle isolation and Detection system for complete on‐chip analysis of EXosomes.

PT13.05.

## Characterising extracellular vesicles from individual low volume cerebrospinal fluid samples, isolated by SmartSEC
Yael Hirschberg1; Kurt Boonen1; Karin Schildermans1; Annemieke van Dam2; Isabel Pintelon3; Charysse Vandendriessche4; Yannick Vermeiren5; Inge Mertens1

1VITO/UAntwerp, Mol, Belgium; 2Amsterdam UMC, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 3Laboratory of Cell Biology & Histology Antwerp Centre for Advanced Microscopy (ACAM) University Antwerp Universiteitsplein 1, Antwerp 2610, Belgium, Antwerpen, Belgium; 4VIB/Ghent University, Ghent, Belgium; 5WUR/UAntwerp, Wageningen, Netherlands

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have recently been considered as a potential biomarker source for a variety of diseases, including neurodegenerative disorders. EVs are important mediators of intercellular communication due to their capacity to transfer genetic material, lipids and proteins. By means of their communication role, interesting biomarkers are often enriched in EVs compared to total biofluid. In order to isolate EVs from a biofluid, like cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), a variety of isolation methods are available of which each methods shows different results, and, to date, there is no golden standard. Our study evaluates the use of size exclusion chromatography (SEC) by the SmartSEC HT kit for EV isolation from CSF.

Methods: EVs were obtained from 500μL CSF samples, isolated by SmartSEC HT. SmartSEC traps contaminants, like bulk proteins, into the resin beads and subsequently elutes EVs. To evaluate this isolation method, various characterisation analyses were run. To detect cargo or surface proteïns, analyses on the ExoView R100, Surface Plasmon Resonance imaging (SPRi) and LC‐MS based proteomics analysis were performed. Concentration, and size of the vesicles were evaluated by use of transmission electron microscopy (TEM), and fluorescence nanotracking analysis (Zetaview).

Results: By means of the ExoView, tetraspanins such as CD81 and CD9 were measured on the surface of the EVs. Other EV‐specific proteins, e.g. lactadherin, flotillin‐1, and HSP70, were detected by SPRI. Untargeted protein analysis revealed the cargo proteins. TEM images visualized the presence of EVs. Particle size and concentration was calculated by nanotracking analysis, and more specifically the particles with a lipid membrane, like EVs, with fluorescence nanotracking analysis.

Summary/Conclusion: As EVs hold a lot of promise in the biomarker field, a standard high‐performance isolation method ‐ regarding purity and yield – is essential. From our data, SmartSEC HT is suggested to be an efficient size exclusion chromatography EV isolation method for low volume CSF samples, especially for high throughput, since samples are loaded on a 96‐well plate.

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles ; cerebrospinal fluid ; size exclusion chromatography; low sample volume

## Fluorescent‐labelling strategy for phenotyping heterogeneous extracellular vesicles in complex biological matrices
Danilo Mladenović1; Kristjan Orrin2; Andras G. Miklosi3; Saara Laitinen4; Sergei Kopantšuk5; Zoltán Giricz6; Delaram Khamari7; Kairi Koort2; Natasa Zarovni8

1Tallinn University / Hansabiomed Life Sciences, Tallinn, Estonia; 2Tallinn University, Tallinn, Estonia; 3Oxford Nanoimaging Limited (ONI), Oxford, UK, Oxford, United Kingdom; 4Finnish Red Cross Blood Service, Helsinki, Finland; 5University of Tartu, Tartu, Estonia; 6Semmelweis University, Department of Pharmacology and Pharmacotherapy, Budapest, Hungary; Pharmahungary ltd., Szeged, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 7Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunbiology, Semmelweis University, Budapest, Hungary; 8HansaBioMed Life Sciences Ltd, Mäealuse 2/1, 12618 Tallinn, Estonia, Tallinn, Estonia

Introduction: Single extracellular vesicle (EV) analyses have always been a challenging task, especially for complex biofluids, such is plasma, that contain heterogeneous population of EVs, as well as many lipoproteins (LPs). Fluorescent labelling facilitates EVs analyses to some extent, however, many of the lower expressing markers and underrepresented subpopulations would end up being undetectable due to the lack of sensitivity, or few traceable events in instrument's measuring range.
Here we propose a methodology combining NTA and plate reader for single‐particle and bulk fluorescence analyses, respectively, that provides sufficient data for meta‐analyses of EVs subpopulations and marker abundance.

Methods: Human blood plasma was purified with SEC, EV fractions were pooled, and exact number of particles was stained either with CMG, CFSE, or fluorescent antibodies. After washing, samples were analyzed on NTA and plate reader (bulk fluorescence of EVs). Total number of antibodies was estimated from bulk fluorescence (known amount of soluble antibody in serial dilutions was used as a reference) and normalized to CFSE+ particles.
Purified EVs from different sources, as well as LPs, were used as controls. For comparison, marker abundance per single EV was also assessed using orthogonal methods, such as super‐resolution microscopy.

Results: EVs make up a small portion of total particles in SEC‐purified plasma (< 20%). Dominant marker for EVs was CD9, with ∼100 antibodies bound per single EV, while the other tetraspanins, CD63 and CD81, had more moderate expression (15‐25 antibodies per EV). Markers denoting specific cell origin were spanning from 3‐5 antibodies per particle for CD36, CD42b, and HLA I, and ∼30 for CD41 and CD235a.

Summary/Conclusion: We provide a simple proof of concept that could enable close estimation of marker abundance in heterogeneous EV populations, especially for the events that are not detectable in conventional single‐particle analyses, due to the low epitope density.

Funding: This research has received funding from the Tallinn University Research Fund (project TF7320) and European Union's Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme, under the FET Proactive grant agreement No. 952183.

Keywords: single extracellular vesicle analysis; marker abundance; fluorescent labelling; nanoparticle tracking analysis; super‐resolution microscopy

PT13.07.

## Effect of triggering strategy using flow cytometry for detecting extracellular vesicles directly in platelet poor plasma samples
Rikke W. Rasmussen1; Jaco Botha2; Mathilde Sanden3; Maiken Mellergaard1; Aase Handberg1

1Department of Clinical Biochemistry, Aalborg University Hospital, Aalborg, Denmark, Aalborg, Denmark; 2Department of Clinical Biochemistry, Aalborg University Hospital, Copenhagen, Denmark; 3Aalborg University Hospital, Department of Clinical Biochemistry, Aalborg, Denmark

Introduction: High‐resolution flow cytometry is a widely used method for characterization of extracellular vesicles (EV). It is, however, important to consider how to collect and analyse data. Using different combinations of light scatter and/or fluorescence triggering threshold strategies is a common way to select for events of interest and subtract background noise. It has previously been reported that higher EV concentrations can be detected using fluorescence triggering compared with light scatter triggering. Here we tested two triggering strategies for rare and abundant EV‐phenotypes in human plasma.

Methods: Platelet poor plasma samples from six anonymous individuals were stained with a mixture of lactadherin, CD41, and CD36, or lactadherin and corresponding isotype controls. Samples were analysed on an Apogee A60 Micro‐PLUS flow cytometer collecting events using light scatter trigger or a combination of light scatter and fluorescence trigger. Gating was performed by using detergent lysis controls for defining lactadherin‐binding (phosphatidylserine positive, PS+) populations and matched isotype controls for CD41 and CD36 on PS+ populations.

Results: Results showed higher concentrations of detected EV populations when using light scatter alone compared with combined light scatter and fluorescence thresholds. This applied to PS+, CD36+, CD41+ as well as double CD36+, CD41+ positive populations.

Summary/Conclusion: We wanted to know how the threshold strategy in general affects EV data analysed on our flow cytometer and hypothesized that fluorescence triggering would detect more of the rare EV phenotypes compared with a light scatter threshold. Surprisingly, we found that we could detect more plasma EVs using light scatter alone. This suggests that fluorescence triggering in this case would fail to collect dim fluorescent EV populations that could be of interest. More studies are needed to further clarify if this is also the case in other designs.

Keywords: Flow cytometry, Threshold, EV quantification

PT13.08.

## Interaction modes of membrane active peptides with etracellular vesicles (EVs)
Tasvilla Sonallya1; Imola Csilla Szigyártó2; Tünde Juhász3; Zoltán Varga3; Tamas Beke‐Somfai4

1Research centre of natural science, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 2Research Centre for Natural Science, Institute of Materials and Environmental Sciences, Budapest, Hungary; 3Research centre natural science,Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 4Research Centre for Natural Science, Budapest, Hungary

Introduction: Membrane active peptides are promising biomaterials with antimicrobial and anticancer applications. They impart their biological applicability either by altering the cell membrane through perturbation or lysis or by enabling targeted drug delivery. The mechanism of interaction of each peptide with the membrane is unique and different models such as carpet, toroidal pore and barrel stave have been used to describe these mechanisms. There are several aspects, from joint presence on infection sites roles to EV cargo loading, where interaction EV – MAP interactions could be relevant, however, our knowledge here is scarce. Based on our initial investigations (1), we have selected 16 antimicrobial peptides and studied the interactions red blood cell derived EV (REV)s to gain an overview on their interaction and action mechanisms.

Methods: Several well‐known peptides i.e. Indolicidin, Aurein 1.2., Dermcidin (DCD‐1), DHVAR 4, Bactenecin, Protegrin, Transportan, Buforin IIb, KLA, Temporin‐La, LL37, FK16, Mellitin, Polybia MPI, Histatin 5 and Penetratin were selected, and their interaction with REV was investigated using several techniques from membrane biophysics.

Results: The biophysical studies demonstrated that that some of the MAP mechanisms were vescicle‐penetrating, while others lytic, or lead to removal of protein corona. More specific, CM15 efficiently removes surface proteins, Melittin has strong membrane disrupting effect whereas, Tempo‐La, Polybia MPI and Bactenecin acts with a different mechanism which has lower disrupting affinity on the original REV composition.

Summary/Conclusion: These results give an insight into an overview of the surface interactions of membrane active peptides with the REVs help us to attain a broad perspective on the molecular level interactions which could, in turn, provide vital information on engineering the surface and the interior of Evs with short MAPs.

Funding: This work was funded by the Ministry of Innovation and Technology of Hungary from the National Research, Development and Innovation Fund, financed under the TKP2021‐EGA‐31 and the 2020‐1‐1‐2‐PIACI‐KFI_2020‐00021 funding schemes. Support from Eötvös Loránd Research Network, grant n.o. SA‐87/2021, is also acknowledged.

Keywords: red blood cell, extracellular vesicle, membrane active peptide, light spectroscopy, protein corona

PT13.09.

## EV isolation kit comparison for stroke biomarker discovery
Olga Neustroeva1; Cristiana Pistono1; Jacopo Zini2; Jere Kekkonen2; Marjo Yliperttula2; Tiia Turunen1; Nea Korvenlaita1; Tarja Malm1

1A.I. Virtanen Institute for Molecular Sciences, University of Eastern Finland, Kuopio, Finland, Kuopio, Finland; 2Division of Pharmaceutical Biosciences and Drug Research Program, Faculty of Pharmacy, University of Helsinki, Helsinki, Finland, Helsinki, Finland

Introduction: Ischemic stroke (IS) affects around 15 million people annually. Survived patients suffer from long‐term consequences and the outcome for those cannot be predicted due to lack of reliable biomarkers. Brain‐derived extracellular vesicles (EVs) secreted to the blood stream may carry molecules indicative of brain status of stroke‐exposed patients. As blood is enriched with liposomes, platelet‐derived EVs and a high diversity of proteins that can complicate EV isolation in the blood stream, there is need to standardize the isolation methods and carry out in depth evaluation of the EV composition to ensure reliable biomarker discovery

Methods: There are a number of methods for isolation of EVs including ultracentrifugation, polymer‐based precipitation, centrifugation in a density gradient and size exclusion chromatography (SEC). Each of the methods faces challenges as some of them are time‐consuming and some give a poor yield or poor quality of EVs. The most appropriate method should be chosen depending on the specific aim. In biomarker discovery it is important to choose a method that allows us to work with a high number of samples with a sufficient yield for several type of analysis and quality controls. We carried out in depth characterization of three different isolation methods: lectin‐based purification, precipitation, and SEC. We used nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) to determine the efficiency of isolation and to measure EV quantity.

Results: Our data demonstrate that precipitation gives the highest number of particles compared to lectin‐based purification or SEC. However, calculation of particles/protein ratio revealed that SEC results in the purest preparations. Cryo‐EM analysis showed that EV samples obtained by precipitation are enriched with lipoproteins and cell debris. Instead, SEC yielded samples were enriched in EVs and contained less contaminating particles. Western blot (WB) confirmed that purity of SEC fractions was higher compared to other two methods. Raman spectroscopy analysis revealed that fractions 2 and 3 clustered together.

Summary/Conclusion: Considering that WB and Cryo‐EM analysis showed that these fractions are abundant in EV‐associated markers and less contaminated with liposomes, our data supports that isolation of fractions 2 and 3 by SEC as the method of choice for isolating EVs from plasma samples.

Funding: Academy of Finland and Business Finland

Keywords: biomarkers, ischemic stroke, size exclusion chromatography, lectin‐based purification, precepitation, EV characterisation

PT13.10.

## An integrated fluidic‐plasmonic lab‐on‐chip for the label‐free characterization of extracellular vesicles for liquid biopsy applications
Sabrina Romanò1; Flavio Di Giacinto1; Maria Eleonora Temperini2; Leonetta Baldassarre2; Valeria Giliberti2; Riccardo Di Santo1; Massimiliano Papi1; Alberto Augello1; Marco De Spitiro1; Michele Ortolani3; Gabriele Ciasca1

1Fondazione Policlinico Universitario A. Gemelli IRCCS & Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore, Roma, Italy, Rome, Italy; 2Dipartimento di Fisica, Sapienza University of Rome & Istituto Italiano di Tecnologia, Center for Life Nanoscience, Rome, Italy; 3Dipartimento di Fisica, Sapienza University of Rome & Istituto Italiano di Tecnologia, Center for Life Nanoscience, ROme, Italy

Introduction: EVs are considered a promising source of cancer biomarkers. Despite this potential, the EV translational process in diagnostics is still at its birth, and the development of novel medical devices is highly demanded. Here we present a lab‐on‐chip platform for EV characterization with application in cancer diagnostics. The device combines three cutting‐edge technologies: i) a SEIRA metasurface for the biochemical characterization of EVs, ii) an SPR sensor for mass quantification, and iii) a hydrophobicity‐driven wet sample holder that enables IR measurements in liquid.

Methods: The metasurface was fabricated in gold on CaF2 using EBL and, subsequently, functionalized with Anti‐CD63 for EV immunocapture. The fluidic holder was realized in PLA with 3D printing. EVs extracted from human cancer cells were used as a model system, after extensive ultrastructural characterization. IR measurements were carried out with an FTIR spectro‐microscope.

Results: The proposed sensor is endowed with label‐free chemical sensitivity, being tunable on the specific absorption bands of biomolecules within EVs, namely the Amide I‐II (1700‐1500 cm‐1) and lipid (3000‐2750 cm‐1) bands. The 3D‐printed holder enables IR measurements in liquid using a 10 μl droplet. The device allows real‐time monitoring of the metasurface functionalization with antibodies and the subsequent EV immunocapture. A protein sensitivity down to the zeptomoles range is demonstrated. Most importantly, the sensor allows for measuring the specific IR spectral fingerprint of EVs in the Amide‐I and II regions.

Summary/Conclusion: Thanks to the high protein sensitivity and the possibility to work with small sample volumes ‐ two key features for ultrasensitive EV detection ‐ our lab‐on‐chip can positively impact the development of novel liquid biopsy approaches for cancer diagnosis based on the label‐free characterization of EVs.

Funding: The Italian Ministry of Health is gratefully acknowledged (grant n. GR201602363310)

Keywords: Microdevice, Liquid Biopsy, Plasmonics

PT13.11.

## Extracellular Vesicles (EVs) as targetable drug delivery vehicles
Maria Chiara Ciferri1; Daniele Reverberi2; Silvia Bruno3; Cansu Gorgun3; Chiara Gentili3; Rodolfo Quarto4; Roberta Tasso3

1University of Genova, Genova, Italy; 2IRCCS Ospedale Policlinico San Martino, Genova, Genova, Italy; 3Department of Experimental Medicine, University of Genova, Genova, Italy; 4Department of Experimental Medicine, University of Genova and IRCCS Ospedale Policlinico San Martino, Genova, Genova, Italy

Introduction: EVs have been recently considered as valid delivery vehicles thanks to their activity as short and long‐distance mediators of intercellular communication. In this study we propose a two‐step EV‐engineering protocol to obtain a tissue‐specific drug delivery system, based on: (i) loading with a medicinal cargo, and (ii) surface functionalization to be used as a platform to bind molecules.

Methods: Plasma‐derived EVs have been isolated from healthy donors by size exclusion chromatography coupled with iodixanol density cushion and characterized by Nanoparticles Tracking Analysis (NTA), non‐conventional flow cytometry (FCM) and western blot. Drug (Paclitaxel ‐ PTX) loading has been achieved by sonication using a probe sonicator (30 s on/30 s off, 20% W amplitude at RT, 2 min cooling × 6 cycles), while membrane functionalization has been obtained through a copper‐free click chemistry reaction (alkyne‐azide Huisgen 1,3‐dipolar cycloaddition). As a proof of concept, a fluorescent azide (AF647 azide) and a fluorescent Paclitaxel (Pacific Blue Paclitaxel) have been used to easily verify both PTX internalization and the conjugation of the fluorescent azide to the EV membrane, by flow cytometry and fluorescent NTA. The uptake of engineered EVs by responder tumor cells (MDA‐MB‐231, a human breast cancer cell line) has been evaluated in vitro and analyzed by flow cytometry and confocal microscopy

Results: Both copper‐free click chemistry and sonication turned out to be a valid surface engineering technique, since about 50% of EVs were AF647‐positive and Pacific Blue‐positive. Engineered EVs are efficiently internalized by MDA‐MB‐231 cells, inducing significant cytotoxic effects.

Summary/Conclusion: Our EV membrane functionalizing system can be used to bind to the EV surface azido‐modified antibodies/molecules, making the engineered EVs (loaded also with a therapeutic cargo) valuable natural drug nanocarriers able to be directed to a specific antigen/target and so potentially applied in various pathological conditions.

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, drug delivery, click‐chemistry

PT13.12.

## High‐resolution Atomic Force Microscopy as a tool for topographical mapping of surface budding
Carolina Sbarigia1; Stefano Tacconi2; Francesco Mura3; Marco Rossi4; Luciana Dini2; Simone Dinarelli5

1Department of Biology and Biotechnology "C.Darwin", Sapienza University of Rome, Roma, Italy; 2Department of Biology and Biotechnology "C. Darwin", Sapienza University of Rome, Rome, Italy; 3Research Center of Nanotechnologies for Engineering (CNIS), Sapienza University of Rome, Rome, Italy; 4Research Center of Nanotechnologies for Engineering (CNIS), Sapienza University of Rome, Roma, Italy; 5Institute for the Structure of Matter (ISM), National Research Council (CNR), Rome, Italy

Introduction: Development of a new methodology to study extracellular vesicles (EVs) morphological features and spatial distribution over the cell surface and their alterations under different physiological conditions, evaluating the efficacy of an Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM) system compared to a conventional Electron Microscopy (EM) method.

Methods: Control and H2O2‐treated SH‐SY5Y neuroblastoma cells were analyzed by SEM and AFM. Large vesicles‐enriched fractions were concentrated by ultracentrifugation from both cells and their morphology, quantity and size distribution were quantified by TEM and AFM. In AFM analysis, defined square areas on the cell nuclear and peripheral regions were selected to map the spatial distribution of the surface budding.

Results: The SEM‐AFM comparative analysis revealed that AFM detected a rougher surface of both control and H2O2‐treated cells, being also capable to identify the presence of vesicles in their terminal portions with respect to the SEM. The buds size distribution and mean diameter were comparable within the two methods. The TEM and AFM analysis of isolated vesicles from control and H2O2‐treated cells displayed the same morphological appearance and range diameter, compatible with large vesicles, further confirmed by the presence of the specific marker annexin A1 by western blotting. We demonstrate how AFM allowed to visualize the 3D structure of isolated vesicles and measured a higher mean diameter of both the populations. Cell roughness and waviness measurements in the cell nuclear region and periphery, according to AFM imaging, showed an increased budding in the periphery of H2O2‐treated cells with respect to the nuclear region and to control cells.

Summary/Conclusion: The results obtained from this comparative study may provide interesting perspectives for the use of AFM for morphological characterization of large vesicles and mapping of surface budding, validating a morphometric approach to understand the pathophysiological state of the cell.

Funding: No external funding

Keywords: Atomic Force Microscopy; surface budding; topographical mapping; roughness

PT13.13.

## Assessment of extracellular vesicles in pancreatic cancer patient samples for the detection of tumor specific biomarkers
ramsha Masood1; Jerome Nouvel2; George Daaboul3; Uwe Wittel4; Gerhard Puetz5; Irina Nazarenko6

1exosomes and tumor biology group university medical center freiburg, Freiburg im breisgau, Germany; 21 Institute for Infection Prevention and Hospital Epidemiology; Medical Center ‐ University of Freiburg, Faculty of Medicine, University of Freiburg, freiburg, Germany; 3NanoView Biosciences, Boston, USA; 4Department of General and Visceral Surgery, Medical Center ‐ University of Freiburg, Faculty of Medicine, University of Freiburg, freiburg, Germany; 52 Institute of Clinical Chemistry and Laboratory Medicine, Medical Center ‐ University of Freiburg, Faculty of Medicine, University of Freiburg, freiburg, Germany; 6Institute for Infection Prevention and Hospital Epidemiology, Faculty of Medicine, University Medical Center Freiburg, Freiburg, Germany, Freiburg, Germany

Introduction: Pancreatic cancer (PDAC) is considered as one of the deadliest cancers in the world. It has the lowest survival rate of all solid cancers. The poor prognosis of pancreatic cancer is mainly because it is asymptomatic due to which, it cannot be detected at early stages. One strikingly promising source of biomarkers for early detection and patients stratification are the Extracellular Vesicles (EVs). However, the methods allowing EV baseline purification from complex biofluids, such as cell‐free plasma or serum are limited. There are two main challenges to solve: i. non‐EV components, especially lipoproteins, have similar size and density as EVs; ii. concentration of lipoproteins, such as VLDL and LDL is highly variable and exceeds several orders of magnitude the concentration of EVs

Methods: Firstly, potential impact of the pre‐analytic routinely used by the biobank for the storage of cell‐free plasma and serum was controlled by examining haemolysis, amount of remaining platelets, and particle number. Addressing the robustness of quantification of disease‐associated biomarkers, we estimated the presence of different lipoproteins, EV‐, and tumor‐biomarkers in EVs derived from the serum samples of PDAC patients. Comprehensive characterization of biomarker distribution was performed for each of the SEC fractions using either bulk methods, such as ELISA and beads‐assisted flow cytometry with latex‐beads and MACSPlex; or single particle‐ analysis, using fluorescent NTA and interferometric imaging (SP‐IRIS).

Results: We observed a very good consistency of data obtained from different patients regarding the amount and distribution of remaining lipoproteins and tumor‐specific biomarkers in EVs, supported feasibility of EV usage for liquid biopsy. Despite of a certain amount of lipoproteins remained in EV‐enriched fractions, bulk EV analysis, such as ELISA and beads‐assisted flow cytometry showed highly consistent results, which was further confirmed by the single‐EV analysis. While EVs derived from the platelets and harbouring the platelets‐specific surface protein CD41, and EV‐specific biomarkers CD9 and CD63 were mostly concentrated in the fraction F2, the fractions F3 contained majority of EVs positive for EpCAM, Tspan8, and the integrin beta6 pancreatic cancer EV biomarkers.

Summary/Conclusion: Our data indicate that if for the analysis of EV lipidomics, generation of pure EV fractions should be mandatory, for the detection of disease‐specific EV proteins, a certain amount of non‐EV components in EV fractions may be tolerated. Robustness of data across a number of PDAC patients confirmed EV surface signature as promising biomarker. Separation of platelets and cancer‐derived EVs in different fractions suggested that even a short path length of a column may allow to differentiate between EVs of different size and different origin.

## PF09: Late‐Breaking Poster Session 3: Technologies and Methods II

## Chair: Marta Garcia‐Contreras – University of Miami, Diabetes Research Institute

## Chair: Malene Møller Jørgensen

PF09.01.

## Label‐free, real‐time detection of extracellular vesicles expressing CXCR4 using surface plasmon resonance
Arnaud Boonen; Sam Noppen; Yagmur Yildizhan; Dragana Spasic; Cindy Heens; Frank Vanderhoydonc; Tom Van Loy; Johan Swinnen; Jeroen Lammertyn; Christophe Pannecouque; Dominique Schols

KU Leuven, Leuven, Belgium

Introduction: Analysis of extracellular vesicles (EVs) is getting more attention as their role showed to be more important than previously thought. EVs entrap nucleic acids, cellular proteins and metabolites in order to act as carrier vesicles between cells. CXCR4 is a G protein‐coupled receptor known to be expressed on the membrane of EVs. Aside from its natural physiological functions, this protein plays an important role in several pathologies including cancer. CXCR4 is found to be overexpressed on various types of tumor cells. EVs derived from these tumor cells can carry a high amount of CXCR4. This suggests that the analysis of CXCR4 positive EVs can be an important tool in cancer diagnostics. Therefore, we developed a label‐free, real‐time binding assay to detect CXCR4 positive EVs using surface plasmon resonance (SPR).

Methods: EVs were obtained from HEK293 wild‐type, HEK293 CXCR4‐transfected, MOLT4, MCF7, U87 wild‐type, U87 CXCR4‐transfected and MT4 cells. The isolation protocol was optimized using recombinant EVs. EVs were isolated by ultrafiltration paired with size exclusion chromatography. They were characterized by nanoparticle tracking analysis. SPR was used to identify EV biomarkers (CD81, CD63, CD9) as well as CXCR4. Fiber‐optic SPR (FO‐SPR) was used to detect the EV markers and CXCR4 in non‐concentrated media.

Results: SPR detected a difference in EV markers between the EVs isolated from different host cell types. HEK293 and MOLT4 EVs contained mostly CD81. U87 EVs had more pronounced CD63 expression compared to the other markers. MCF7 EVs depicted a high expression of both CD81 and CD63. MT4 EVs contained mostly CD9. Furthermore, overexpression of recombinant CXCR4 in HEK293 and U87 cells translated to an increased CXCR4 expression on the EVs. Endogenously expressed CXCR4 was detected in EVs from most of the cell types, with MT4 and MOLT4 showing the highest response even surpassing their EV biomarker responses. FO‐SPR allowed us to detect colocalized EV markers and CXCR4 in non‐concentrated media.

Summary/Conclusion: Our assay was able to distinguish between endogenously expressed‐ and overexpressed CXCR4 and enabled the detection of cancer cell derived EVs expressing a high amount of CXCR4. In addition, CXCR4 was detected endogenously in EVs from most of the cell types, indicating it could represent a new EV biomarker.
