## Membrane labelling of extracellular vesicles for high‐resolution flow cytometry
Maiken Mellergaard; Rikke W. Rasmussen; Anders Askeland; Morten Hjuler Nielsen; Aase Handberg

Department of Clinical Biochemistry, Aalborg University Hospital, Aalborg, Denmark, Aalborg, Denmark

Introduction: Flow cytometry is a powerful tool for characterizing extracellular vesicle (EV) phenotypes. However, proper EV labelling and specificity of such is challenging and continuously debated. In example, studies have shown superiority of using lactadherin staining compared with other non‐antibody‐based labels such as carboxyfluorescein succinimidyl ester (CFSE), while CFSE labelling was seemingly superior to CD9 and CD81 staining of purified EVs. Previous data from our group showed that lactadherin and Annexin V to some extent also label ApoB‐containing lipoproteins. Finally, findings that a corona of proteins including ApoB and other common markers can form on the EV surface further complicates this issue.

Methods: In this preliminary study, we used high‐resolution flow cytometry (adhering to MIFlowCyt‐EV guidelines) to investigate EV labelling directly in human plasma, to compare and optimize EV staining in terms of incubation time and temperature. We used a cocktail of CD9, CD63, and CD81 (CD9/CD63/CD81) and compared with lactadherin staining for 30 minutes, 2 hours, or overnight (ON) at 4°C or room temperature (RT).

Results: Our preliminary results suggest that maximal lactadherin staining is reached after 2 hours at RT, while CD9/CD63/CD81 labelling, on the contrary increases continuously over time with highest labelling after ON incubation, especially at 4°C.

Summary/Conclusion: In conclusion, more studies are needed to verify and clarify our findings. However, these early data could suggest that lactadherin staining in plasma is saturated faster than labelling using a CD9/CD63/CD81 antibody cocktail, underlining the necessity for specifically optimized staining protocols.

PF09.03.

## NEXOS: A n electro‐optical bead‐nanochip technology for the ultrasensitive and multi‐dimensional detection of small extracellular vesicles and their markers
Tomas Dias1; Ricardo Figueiras1; Susana Vagueiro1; Renato Domingues1; Yu‐Hsien Hung1; Elnaz Persia1; Pierre Arsene2

1Mursla, Cambridge, United Kingdom; 2Mursla, London, United Kingdom

Introduction: Small extracellular vesicles (sEVs), including exosomes, are enriched in multiomics information mirroring their parental cells. They have been investigated in health and disease and utilised in several applications from drug discovery to diagnostics. In disease diagnostics, sEVs can be sampled via a blood draw, enabling the convenient liquid biopsy of the tissue they originate from. However, few applications with sEVs have been translated into clinical practice. We hypothesize this is due to various factors including the relative limited sensitivity and limited information obtained with the gold standard sEV detection techniques.

Methods: We developed a novel technology, named Nanoparticle EXOsome Sensing (NEXOS), for the ultrasensitive and multi‐dimensional detection of sEVs. NEXOS comprises two methods, Electro‐NEXOS (E‐NEXOS) and Optical‐NEXOS (O‐NEXOS). E‐NEXOS is a novel nanoelectronics method which uses nanochips and O‐NEXOS is a high‐throughput optical method. Yet, they share the same steps for immunocapture and labelling of sEVs and can be combined to derive differentiated measured parameters.

Results: We present the working principle of the technologies and, as proof‐of‐principle, show the linear detection and sensitivity of the methods in detecting target CD9+CD81+ and CD9+HER2+ sEVs, diluted in PBS or spiked in processed plasma, originated from breast cancer cell cultures. We also provide an approach for the determination of new dimensions, unseen with the gold standards or with emerging technologies, namely the detection of target sEVs (TEVs), target epitopes in sEVs (TEPs) and epitopes per target sEV.
Additionally, we demonstrate the higher sensitivity of O‐NEXOS when compared to the gold standards and demonstrate that E‐NEXOS is, at least, on par with those, albeit only powered by 36 nanogap sensors.
Finally, we lay the groundwork for the scaling and electronics miniaturization of E‐NEXOS with millions of nanogap sensors for the translation of NEXOS into routine sEVs analysis.

Summary/Conclusion: To conclude, we presented a novel technology for the ultrasensitive detection of sEVs which also determines new dimensions such as the detection of TEVs, TEPs and number of target epitopes per sEV.
At its present state, O‐NEXOS demonstrates higher sensitivity than the tested gold standard sEV detection technologies while E‐NEXOS, with the indicated scaling and electronics miniaturisation from 36 to 2,000,000 sensors, has the potential to achieve extremely high sensitivity and translate sEV‐based liquid biopsies into routine clinical care practices.

PF09.04.

## Unbiased and UMI‐informed sequencing of plasma EV‐associated miRNAs at single‐nucleotide resolution
M.A.J. van Eijndhoven1; E. Aparicio‐Puerta2; Cristina Gómez‐Martín3; J.M. Medina2; E.E.E. Drees4; E.J. Bradley3; Leontien Bosch1; C. Scheepbouwer3; M. Hackenberg2; Dirk M. Pegtel1

1Department of Pathology, Cancer Center Amsterdam, Amsterdam UMC location Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 2University of Granada, Granada, Spain; 3Amsterdam University Medical Centers, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 4amsterdam university medical centers, amsterdam, Netherlands

Introduction: Recent advances show that apart from 21nt mature miRNAs, miRNAs with non‐templated additions (NTAs), called isomiRs have biological relevance that can dramatically change their pre‐processing, stability and targetome. Deconvolution of these posttranscriptional modifications is challenging in particular for low‐input samples, e.g. plasma extracellular vesicles (pEV) that are considered as a source for minimally‐invasive diagnostics.
Both for biological studies as well as for diagnostic purposes accurate detection and distinction of mature miRNAs and isomiRs are essential.

Methods: RNA sequencing is currently the only reliable method that can distinguish mature miRNAs from isomiRs with single‐nucleotide resolution and that can truthfully reveal isomiR diversity in biological samples. Improvements aside, current small RNA sequencing strategies remain imprecise. We developed “IsoSeek” that diverges from these methods by making use of randomized 5’‐ and 3’‐adapters combined with a 10N unique molecular identifier (UMI).

Results: Using synthetic mature miRNA and isomiR spike‐ins in multiple optimization rounds, we show that IsoSeek has reduced ligation and PCR amplification bias leading to improved accuracy in the detection of isomiRs.
To investigate the detection of isomiRs in a biological background, we sequenced HEK293T cells in which the terminal uridylyl transferases TUT4 and TUT7 were ablated by CRISPR/Cas9 (TUT4/7 DKO). The overall reduction in uridylation of miRNAs is much more pronounced when using IsoSeek compared to commercial protocols. Looking at individual miRNAs, we characterized the terminal uridylase dependent miRNA uridylome and discovered extensive uridylation of disease‐associated miRNAs.
When sequencing pEV samples we show that IsoSeek captures the full complexity of isomiRs in comparison with commercial protocols. Finally we tested our IsoSeek protocol by preparing libraries from 188 DLBCL patient pEV samples. Hierarchical clustering of the sequence results before and after UMI correction shows a vast difference. This suggests that when analyzing low‐input clinical liquid biopsy samples UMI correction can reduce PCR bias based clustering.

Summary/Conclusion: IsoSeek is optimized for qualitative and quantitative miRNA profiling at single‐nucleotide resolution for functional and target prediction studies in vitro as well as in ultra low‐input biological samples.

PF09.05.

## A‐TEEM spectroscopic characterization of exosome standards and their mixtures
Linda H. Kidder1; Adam M. Gilmore2

1HORIBA Scientific, Elliott City, USA; 2HORIBA Scientific, Piscataway, USA

Introduction: Absorbance‐Transmission Excitation Emission Matrix spectroscopy (A‐TEEM) is sensitive to protein composition, rapid (< 60 sec data acquisition times), and non‐destructive. The purpose of this study is to test the feasibility of using A‐TEEM to quickly and sensitively differentiate exosomes in solution. With access limited to only commercially available samples, the study was limited in scope, but shows promise in rapid qualitative and quantitative characterization of exosomes.

Methods: Lyophilized Exosome Standards PC‐3 (ab239689) and MCF7 (ab239691) were purchased (Abcam, Cambridge, MA). All sample measurements were made on the Aqualog UV‐800 (HORIBA Scientific, Piscataway, NJ), with excitation from 220‐450 nm. A blank measurement was collected with PBS solvent. PC‐3 and MCF7 were reconstituted to 1 ug/ul with PBS, and then diluted an additional 200X and placed in a standard UV/Vis 1 cm path length cuvette for measurement. The PC‐3/PBS solution was measured, and mixtures with MCF7/PBS solution in the following proportions were measured: 75%, 50%, 25%. Pure MCF7/PBS solution was also measured. Data analysis employed multivariate methods. PARAFAC (Parallel Factor Analysis) was used for qualitative purposes, differentiate between the two exosome types. A PLS (Partial Least Squares) approach was used to predict the amount of the two in a mixture.

Results: The fluorescence EEMs profiles of these samples indicate that there are measurable differences in protein signatures between the samples. The PARAFAC model was clearly able to distinguish between the exosomes samples as well as each mixture. The PLS model was predictive of concentration, with an r‐squared value of > 0.99, and SD of ∼2%.

Summary/Conclusion: This feasibility study was undertaken with a limited scope to test the potential for this approach for the characterization of exosomes in solution. A‐TEEM was readily able to distinguish between the two exosomes, as well as their mixtures, and to predict the concentrations of the two components in the mixtures.

Funding: This research was fully funded by HORIBA Scientific, a for‐profit company that develops and sells analytical instrumentation.

Keywords: A‐TEEM, Fluorescence EEMs

PF09.06.

## Microbead‐based isolation of EVs from urine provides high specificity and effective yield with minimal co‐isolation of contaminants
Andres Lõhmus1; Riccardo Vago2; Natasa Zarovni1

1HansaBioMed Life Sciences Ltd, Mäealuse 2/1, 12618 Tallinn, Estonia, Tallinn, Estonia; 2UniSR Università Vita‐Salute San Raffaele, Milan, Italy

Introduction: Urine features an ideal source of non‐invasive diagnostic markers for diverse clinical conditions. Urine EVs (uEVs) originate from different tissues, with the expected enrichment from the urogenital route. Many uEV isolation protocols for proteomic and transcriptomic analysis exist, likely to isolate different subpopulations or at least enable different uEV yield and purity, appropriate for different clinical questions. Out of these, affinity pull‐down protocols provide scalability and single‐step EV isolation, allowing the processing and analysis of both routinely harvested and biobanked urine samples. Such protocols are amenable and user friendly, can be automated and serve different analytical platforms, allowing for integration to end‐to‐end diagnostic assays. However, they typically trade‐off yield for purity, while a delicate balance of both is needed for reliable biomarker detection.

Methods: Micro‐beads of different size and material are compared to peptide based isolation kits in the purification of model EVs, both HEK293 and human urine‐derived particles. Pre‐labelled and recombinant fluorescent EVs are purified from buffers and human urine. The recovery of particles and common urine contaminants such as uromodulin is studied by F‐NTA, FIA, qPCR and WB.The specificity of immunoaffinity pull‐down is assessed by isotype antibodies and non‐human derived EV controls.

Results: We show that pre‐labelled and recombinant fluorescent EVs can be a very sensitive tool in assessing particle recovery from buffers and human urine. Using these tools we compare EV affinity‐isolation methods and show that magnetic immuno‐beads provide high effective yield and minimal co‐purification of contaminants compared to precipitation and centrifugation‐based isolation methods. In addition, we assess the impact of urine preanalytical variables, such as pH and variations in salts, and operative features such as cost‐effectiveness and time consumption of the affinity isolation protocols.

Summary/Conclusion: Affinity‐isolation of EVs with magnetic immunobeads provides high effective yield and is superior in specificity and purity compared to precipitation and centrifugation‐based isolation methods.

Funding: Funding: MARVEL Project ID 951768.

Keywords: immuno‐affinity, microbeads, magnetic beads, urine

PF09.07.

## Investigating the capture and fractionation of extracellular vesicles using heparin
Benjamin Barnes1; Thomas Caws2; Samantha Thomas2; Alex P. Shephard3; Randolph Corteling2; Paul Hole2; Daniel Bracewell1

1University College London, London, United Kingdom; 2ReNeuron, Bridgend, United Kingdom; 3NanoView Biosciences, Malvern, United Kingdom

Introduction: Despite the rapidly growing commercial interest in extracellular vesicles (EVs), manufacturing processes for EV production are still in the development phase. The recovery and purification of EVs remains a major hurdle in the progression of fundamental research and the application of EV‐based products.

Methods: The use of the glycosaminoglycan, heparin, to capture EVs is a relatively novel and unexplored method of EV purification. Heparin affinity chromatography (HAC) is a highly scalable and reproducible form of pseudo‐affinity chromatography. In this study, we demonstrated the use of a HAC protocol to fractionate EVs based on their affinity to heparin.

Results: This protocol fractionated EVs into two discrete eluted populations that exhibited statistically significant differences in tetraspanin immunophenotypes, variable expression of the exosome‐associated protein TSG101 and differences in purity. On average, the removal of 98.8% of residual protein and 99.0% of DNA was achieved using this chromatography protocol.

Summary/Conclusion: In summary, HAC shows promise as a method to fractionate EVs and with further development may be capable of isolating EV subpopulations with more desirable properties. However, a more detailed proteomic analysis of the eluted EV populations, with a focus on known heparin‐binding proteins, is needed to establish whether this protocol is truly isolating EV subpopulations with differing molecular profiles, or the observed differences are a result of heterogeneity in marker expression.

Funding: This work was supported by a 4‐year EPSRC EngD programme at University College London that was sponsored by ReNeuron (grant code: EP/L01520X/1) and by an Industrial Fellowship from the Royal Commission for the Exhibition of 1851.

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, Heparin, Purification, Isolation

PF09.08.

## Identification and surface characterization of individual HIV‐1 virions amongst an extracellular vesicle background using single‐particle interferometric reflectance imaging sensing (SP‐IRIS)
Zach A. Troyer1; Olesia Gololobova2; Zhaohao Liao3; Kenneth W. Witwer2

1Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, USA; 2Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Baltimore, USA; 3Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Johns Hopkins School of Medicine, Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, MD, USA, Baltimore, USA

Introduction: Enveloped viruses can be considered a type of extracellular vesicle that is influenced by a viral genomic program. Both host and viral EVs are nanoscale, lipid bilayer particles that share common egress pathways. Much effort has been spent attempting to separate true enveloped virions, such as those of HIV, from non‐viral EVs. The gold standard, density gradient ultracentrifugation, is costly and time‐consuming. Recently, the idea of using the EV marker tetraspanins CD9, CD63, and CD81 to immunopurify EVs away from HIV has been suggested. However, HIV virions also carry tetraspanins. Single‐particle surface characterization of HIV would be beneficial to the development of immunocapture separation methods. Here, we demonstrate that single HIV‐1 virions can be analyzed for surface markers using single‐particle interferometric reflectance imaging sensing (SP‐IRIS).

Methods: HIV‐1 (BaL strain) was concentrated from the conditioned medium of chronically‐infected PM1 cells. Uninfected PM1 cells provided an EV control. Conditioned media was processed via differential ultracentrifugation to enrich an HIV/EV or EV‐containing pellet. Samples were UV‐inactivated according to various protocols before incubation on SP‐IRIS chips. An AF488‐conjugated anti‐gp120 antibody was used as a fluorescent probe. SP‐IRIS was done with the NanoView ExoView R100 and tetraspanins capture chips.

Results: SP‐IRIS detected a distinct and relatively heterogeneous population of presumed HIV virions against a background of EVs. HIV particles were mostly C63+/CD81+/gp120+ and were approximately 100 nm in diameter. This distinct size population did not appear in the uninfected EV control.

Summary/Conclusion: The detected virus population likely represents a mixture of mature and immature HIV virions. In ongoing studies, we will use fluorescence‐linked antibodies to phenotype viruses for additional surface proteins. Use of a fluorescent probe targeting HIV gRNA in the presence of permeabilizing agents may also identify true infectious virions. This approach, including the inactivation protocols we optimized, can be used to study enveloped viruses outside of containment laboratories.

Keywords: HIV, EVs, SP‐IRIS, surface, characterization, separation

PF09.09.

## Advanced In‐situ Liquid Phase Transmission Electron Microscopy: A Revolutionary Technique for Extracellular Vesicle Research
Hugo Perez1; Hongyu Sun2; Vasilis Papadimitriou2; Eva Bladt2

1DENSsolutions B.V., Delft, Netherlands; 2DENSsolutions, Delft, Netherlands

Introduction: EV imaging by cryo‐Transmission Electron Microscopy allows for the observation of EVs in their near‐native state, achieving a detailed understanding of the morphology, size distribution, and phenotypes. However, the enumeration of EVs by this approach is affected by a systematic error, and the process prevents the possibility of enabling high‐throughput studies.

Methods: We introduce our technology for in‐situ Liquid Phase Transmission Electron Microscope (LPTEM) studies, which is an add‐on and a plug‐and‐play system. The latter relies on a Micro Electro‐Mechanical System (MEMS)‐based device as a smart sample carrier, referred to as the Nano‐Cell, which contains an integrated set of biasing electrodes or an integrated microheater. These capabilities enable comprehensive in‐situ studies on extracellular vesicles and pharmaceutical research as a function of temperature/biasing/pressure/flow rate. Similarly, the device contains chemical scavenging capabilities to mitigate the effect of the chemical radicals (i.e. radiation damage).

Results: The system enables high level of control over the microfluidics and high throughput studies. As part of the workflow, the user can: a) flow the liquid containing the EVs, b) enable static analysis of the batch (e.g. count the amount of EVs in the field of view, analyze their area and size distribution, or even study the particle trajectory), c) flush the batch and flow in a new batch, d) repeat the analysis. The waste can be collected to do further analysis. We have reported single particle analysis of proteins with 4.5 angstrom resolution while being in liquid. Additionally, we can use Brownian motion of the particle to resolve its 3D structure while being in liquid. Furthermore, the user can avoid this liquid‐air interface. Additionally, the system allows to control the mass transport, enabling the user to optimize the biochemistry on‐the‐fly. We have shown that these capabilities allow to visualize in‐situ the synthesis of vesicles (e.g. for drug delivery purposes). To fight radiation damage, we have successfully integrated Graphene onto our Nano‐Cell, providing chemical scavenging. We have studied micelles, where we can clearly see that without Graphene the particle starts getting destroyed with a dose rate of 0.3 electrons/square Angstrom, 1s/frame, whereas with Graphene we can successfully increase the dose by 3.5 times without attacking the sample.

Summary/Conclusion: Our development provides the unique possibility to visualize biological processes in real time with angstrom resolutions, while the biosample is in its native environment (as a function of different stimuli). This opens up several possibilities such as in‐vivo studies of protein‐protein interactions, biomedical/pharmaceutical applications of EVs (e.g. disease biomarkers, drug delivery vehicles or vaccines) and fundamental studies in biophysics and biochemistry.

PF09.10.

## Extracellular vesicle‐free foetal calf serum and human platelet lysate to sustain high extracellular vesicle production from human cells
Bileyle Lorenzini1; Chloé Lézin1; Sylvie Goulinet2; Sabrina Soave1; Philippe Mauduit2

1INSERM UMR‐MD 1197, Villejuif, France; 2Inserm UMR‐MD 1197, Villejuif, France

Introduction: Therapies using human Mesenchymal Stromal Cells (MSCs) or Endothelial Progenitor Cells (EPCs) are getting a growing interest in a wide range of indications. A substantial part of their therapeutic effects relies on cell‐secreted factors and the EV are proposed as a cell‐free surrogate for therapy. Commonly used culture additives such as Fetal Calf Serum (FCS) or Human Platelet Lysate (HPL) contains large amounts of EV. Purification and characterization of cell secreted EV therefore needs either starvation conditions or the prior elimination of contaminant EV from these additives. Starving to produce EV often limit cell survival thus EV production. Hence, we compare the effect of starvation to EV‐free FCS or HPL addition on cell survival and EV production from both human adherent primary cells: MSC and EPC and non‐adherent human cell lines: JURKAT and THP1.

Methods: EV are removed from FCS/HPL by tangential‐flow‐filtration (TFF). After an amplification phase in complete media MSCs, EPCs, JURKATs and THP1 were placed in medium with or without EV‐free FCS/HPL. After 72h, the media were collected for EV quantification by NTA and replaced by fresh media for a new 72h production cycle. Cell number, morphology and EV production were followed at each step and in each incubation condition.

Results: EV‐free FCS or HPL allowed survival of all 4 different cell types for at least 3 cycles of 72 hours. On contrary, starvation did not allow to maintain EPCs, JURKATs and THP1 survival for more than one 72h cycle. MSC survival was less impaired but also clearly limited with cell showing phenotypical sign of stress. In any case, EV production was fully correlated to cell survival.

Summary/Conclusion: EV‐free FCS/HPL containing medium and not starvation could be used to sustain the EV production from MSCs, EPCs, JURKAT and THP1 cells. EV‐free HPL will allow large volumes of xenobiotic‐free conditioned media such as in bioreactors and large‐scale production of therapeutic EV.

## A comparison of methods for the enrichment and separation of human salivary extracellular vesicles
Zhaohao Liao1; Olesia Gololobova2; Bonita H. Powell3; Gabriela Lima3; Kenneth W. Witwer2

1Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Johns Hopkins School of Medicine, Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, MD, USA, Baltimore, USA; 2Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Baltimore, USA; 3Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Baltimore, USA

Introduction: Saliva is a non‐invasively accessed biofluid for biomarker discovery. Sample acquisition is easier and safer than blood collection and can be done in‐home, convenient for the elderly and small children. Salivary extracellular vesicles (EVs) can be used as potential biomarkers for certain oral cancers and dental diseases and have recently attracted much attention. However, most biomarker studies use polymer‐based precipitation and do not include rigorous EV characterization per the Minimal Information for Studies of EVs (MISEV). Here, we present a comparison study of salivary extracellular vesicles separation techniques, including ultracentrifugation (UC), several variations of size exclusion chromatography (SEC)/bind‐elute chromatography, and a combination of sizing and affinity capture.

Methods: Saliva was collected by passive drool from healthy donors directly into 2 ml protein low‐binding tubes. Cells and debris were removed by centrifuging the samples at 12,500xg for 10 min. SEC/bind‐elute separation was performed using IZON qEV original 70nm columns, SmartSEC HT plates, Exo‐spin 96 plates, and Superdex 200 Increase 10/300GL (GE Healthcare, IL, USA) column capped with fast performance liquid chromatography system. UC pellet was obtained by centrifugation of the samples at 120,000xg for 90 min at 4°C. Sizing/affinity capture was performed with EXO‐NET® beads. The final product was characterized using Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM), Nanoflow Cytometry Analyzer (NanoFCM), Western blot (WB) for EV markers (CD9, CD63, TSG101, and Syntenin‐1), and co‐isolate markers (Albumin, ApoB100, ApoE, Calnexin, and GM130), and single‐particle tetraspanin assays (SP‐IRIS: ExoView).

Results: All presented methods allowed us to obtain EV‐rich fractions that were positive for EV markers and of similar size distribution, mostly within a detected size range of 50 to 120 nm. SP‐IRIS indicated a tetraspanin profile with higher expression of CD9 and CD63 than CD81. However, WB characterization of the UC pellet and fractions obtained by some of the SEC approaches (qEV, Exo‐spin 96) showed presence of co‐isolating material like low‐density lipoproteins and albumin.

Summary/Conclusion: All evaluated methods can be used to enrich salivary EVs. However, additional purification may be required for UC and some SEC/bind‐elute methods if highly pure EVs are needed for further analysis.

Keywords: Saliva, Extracellular vesicles, Size Exclusion Chromatography, EV separation

PF09.12.

## Detection of extracellular vesicles using the BD FACSymphony™ A1 Cell Analyzer
Tina Van Den Broeck1; Estefanía Lozano‐Andrés2; Mark Dessing3; Annette Meyer4; Tim Schenkel4; Xiaoshan Shi5; Ludovic Monheim1; Stephanie Widmann6; Marca H.M Wauben2; Aaron Tyznik6

1Becton Dickinson, BD Biosciences, Europe, Erembodegem, Belgium; 2Division of Cell Biology, Metabolism & Cancer, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands; 3Becton Dickinson, BD Biosciences, Europe, Allschwil, Switzerland; 4Becton Dickinson, BD Biosciences, Europe, Heidelberg, Germany; 5Becton Dickinson, BD Biosciences, CA, United States, San Jose, USA; 6Becton Dickinson, BD Biosciences, CA, United States, San Diego, USA

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are small lipid‐based vesicles secreted from cells. Flow cytometry provides an attractive analytical approach as the heterogeneous EV populations can be studied with high‐throughput, single‐vesicle, multiparameter analysis. Conventional flow cytometry instruments and methods optimized for cell analysis are not well suited for the measurement of EVs as the small size and low refractive index of EVs make it challenging to resolve them from noise. Improvement in instrument performance, method robustness, experiment design, data analysis and cross‐measurement standardization are necessary to support analysis of EVs using flow cytometry.

Methods: In this study, we evaluated the BD FACSymphony™ A1 Cell Analyzer with BD® Small Particle Detector Option for analysis of small particles including EVs and sub‐micron beads. Following the MIFlowCyt‐EV framework for publication, we stained and examined EVs purified from the human MCF7 breast cancer cell line, along with necessary controls, using a novel small particle side scatter channel (SP‐SSC). We also evaluated the sensitivity, size resolution, noise levels and dynamic range using various sub‐micron beads. Replicate experiments were performed on multiple instruments and over different days.

Results: The BD FACSymphony™ A1 Cell Analyzer with BD® Small Particle Detector Option was set up in a standardized fashion. Using this methodology, we show that multiple instruments can consistently resolve 90‐nm NIST‐traceable polystyrene particles from noise and correctly determine their concentration as low as 1E+06 beads per mL on SP‐SSC threshold, with an R‐square value >0.99 upon 2‐fold serial dilutions, proving single small particle detection over a significant dynamic range. Furthermore, upon staining with a membrane dye and a fluorochrome‐labeled tetraspanin CD9 antibody, we showed that purified EVs from MCF7 cells can be detected and that a combined “OR” thresholding (SP‐SSC “OR” fluorescence) can add value over SP‐SSC or fluorescence only.

Summary/Conclusion: EVs and sub‐micron particles can be detected using BD FACSymphony™ A1 Cell Analyzer. Standardized instrument setup, calibration, proper experimental controls and “OR” thresholding can help improve EV flow cytometry measurements.
Class 1 Laser Product.
For Research Use Only. Not for use in diagnostic or therapeutic procedures.
BD and BD FACSymphony are trademarks of Becton, Dickinson and Company or its affiliates. © 2022 BD. All rights reserved. 0322

Funding: European Union's Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska‐Curie grant agreement No [722148]

PF09.13.

## Revealing unique surface profile of cell‐derived vesicles by combining proteome analysis and nanoparticle flow cytometry
Hui‐Chong Lau1; Ilaria Passalacqua2; Jik Han Jung1; Yerim Kwon1; Jeong Eun Park1; Sung‐Soo Park1; Davide Zocco2; Senthil Ramaswamy3; Seung Wook Oh1

1BioDrone® Research Institute, MDimune Inc., Seoul, Republic of Korea; 2Lonza Siena Srl, Toscana, Italy; 3Lonza Walkersville Inc., Rockville, USA

Introduction: The cell‐derived vesicles (CDVs) are obtained from virtually any cell by applying MDimune's proprietary extrusion technology. With superior productivity and versatility, this technology has garnered increasing attention among drug developers, especially those who wish to use them as a drug delivery vehicle. We previously described the similarity and differences between CDVs and exosomes. Particularly, among well‐known exosome markers, CD9 and CD81 are less represented in CDVs compared to exosomes while CD63 is more prominent in CDVs. In an early survey of CDV markers at the single‐particle level, we also found that three tetraspanin markers are differentially expressed between CDVs and exosomes. Therefore, a more systematic survey of marker expression profiles was necessary to better understand CDVs and their therapeutic potentials.

Methods: Multiple batches of CDVs were produced from Lonza's HEK293 cells by extruding cells serially through membrane filters (10, 3, and 0.4 μm). Exosomes were obtained from the culture medium of the same cell sources. Then, both CDVs and exosomes were purified using the same purification processes to minimize artifacts associated with purification methods. Then, CDVs and exosomes were subjected to proteome analysis and nanoparticle flow cytometry using the NanoAnalyzer (NanoFCM Co).

Results: Here, we present the proteome analysis results of CDVs derived from HEK293 cells in comparison with exosomes or source cells. We first identify membrane proteins uniquely enriched in CDVs compared to exosomes or source cells. Then, we examine their expression patterns at the individual nanoparticle level as well as confirm them using FACS and western blot. The characterization results of both nanoparticle flow cytometry and other conventional biochemical means coherently reveal a unique surface profile of CDVs.

Summary/Conclusion: This study broadens our understanding of this intriguing technology to the particle level. The current findings provide essential insights into the biogenesis of CDVs as well as facilitate the further development of CDVs in diverse therapeutic applications.

Keywords: Cell‐derived vesicles, Single‐particle analysis,

PF10.01.

## Extracellular vesicles in the peptide‐membrane interaction landscape
Tamás Beke‐Somfai1; Imola Szigyártó2; Zoltán Varga3; Anikó Gaál3; Judith Mihäly2; Diana Kitka2; Lilla Turiák4; László Drahos2; Mayra Queme Pena5; Lívia Fülöp6; Priyanka Singh5; Sonallya Tasvilla2

1Research Centre for Natural Sciences, Budapest, Budapest, Hungary; 2Research Centre for Natural Sciences, Budapest, Hungary; 3Biological Nanochemistry Research Group, Institute of Materials and Environmental Chemistry, Research Centre for Natural Sciences, Budapest, Hungary; 4Research Centre for Natural Sciences, Institute of Organic Chemistry, Budapest, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 5Research Centre for Natural Science, Budapest, Hungary; 6Department of Medical Chemistry, University of Szeged, Szeged, Hungary

Introduction: Host defense membrane active peptides (HDPs) are most often cationic species exerting not only antimicrobial, antiviral, antifungal and anticancer effects, but also a diverse set of roles in immunomodulation. In this respect, extracellular vesicles (EVs), are highly mobile lipid bilayer bodies, and can have widespread important roles both for human host and invasive bacterial cells. However, we know comparatively very little about the potential EV‐HDP interactions, and their relevance in blocking, distritbuting, or assembling EV cargo and associated biomolecular compounds at important locations such as infectious wound sites. In this presentation, we aim to address the various aspects of these interactions and demonstrate on selected studies how Nature could use HDPs to manipulate EVs and their content.

Methods: The action mechanism of HDPs on red blood cell‐derived extracellular vesicles (REV) were tested using flow‐linear dichroism, microscale resistive pulse sensing and microscale thermophoresis. In addition standard biophysical techniques were also employed (e.g. DLS, TEM, IR, CD spectroscopy)

Results: We have sown [1,2] that the selected HDPs can remove and redistribute REV elements efficiently, by both removing the surface proteins of vesicles at lower concentrations, but also by disrupting the vesicles and forming larger, lamellar bilayer structures, that can be beneficial during tissue repair, or in separating protein and lipid components.

## Reference
