Membrane Active Peptides Remove Surface Adsorbed Protein Corona From Extracellular Vesicles of Red Blood Cells. Front. Chem. 2020, 8. https://doi.org/10.3389/fchem.2020.00703.

Summary/Conclusion: The studies provided molecular level insight to EV‐HDP processes which may also offer therapeutic strategies for related areas.

Funding: This work was funded by the Ministry of Innovation and Technology of Hungary from the National Research, Development and Innovation Fund, financed under the TKP2021‐EGA‐31 and the 2020‐1‐1‐2‐PIACI‐KFI_2020‐00021 funding schemes. Support from Eötvös Loránd Research Network, grant n.o. SA‐87/2021, is also acknowledged.

Keywords: Red blood cell, extracellular vesicle, membrane active peptide, light spectroscopy, protein corona.

PF10.03.

## PC‐3 prostate cancer cells constitutively release beta‐2 adrenergic receptor via extracellular vesicles
Krizia Sagini1; Ilaria Guerriero2; Alicia Llorente3

1Oslo University Hospital Oslo, Department of Molecular Cell Biology, Institute for Cancer Research, Oslo, Norway; 2Biogem, Biology and Molecular Genetics Institute, Ariano Irpino, Italy; 3Oslo University Hospital Oslo, Department of Molecular Cell Biology, Institute for Cancer Research, oslo, Norway

Introduction: Beta‐2 adrenergic receptor (ADRB2) belongs to the G protein‐coupled receptor (GPCR) family. Evidence suggests that GPCRs can be secreted in extracellular vesicles (EVs), which may result in the horizontal transfer of functional receptors. In advanced prostate cancer (PCa), ADRB2 is considered a molecular switch for neuroendocrine differentiation, which is associated with poor prognosis. Moreover, ADRB2 level correlates with tumor progression. The aim of this study is to investigate whether PCa cells release ADRB2 via EVs and how this affects the tumor microenvironment.

Methods: EVs were isolated by differential centrifugation (large‐medium EVs at 10,000g and small EVs (sEVs) at 100,000g) from the conditioned media of the highly metastatic cell line PC‐3. In selected experiments, EVs were further purified by high resolution density gradient. EVs were characterized by immunoblotting, radioligand binding assay and nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA). In some experiments, cells were treated with the ADRB2 agonist isoproterenol and/or the antagonist propranolol.

Results: Our results show that PC‐3 cells constitutively secrete ADRB2 in annexin‐positive sEVs. Treatment with the agonist reduced the level of ADRB2 in released EVs, whereas the antagonist prevented this effect. NTA showed that the lower ADRB2 level in EVs was not due to a general inhibition of EVs secretion by isoproterenol.

Summary/Conclusion: Our results suggest that ADRB2 is released by PC‐3 cells via sEVs budding from the plasma membrane, as these vesicles contain annexins. Indeed, treatments modifying ADRB2 localization (activation) affected its extracellular release. Finally, we have generated a stable cell line expressing fluorescent ADRB2‐neonGreen will allow to monitor the release of ADRB2 and to track its transfer to recipient cells thus providing further insight into the role of tumor EVs in the horizontal transfer of ADRB2.

Funding: The Norwegian Cancer Society and the South‐Eastern Norway Regional Health Authority

PF10.04.

## Profiling the small RNA cargo in extracellular vesicles derived from oxidised low‐density lipoprotein‐stimulated vascular smooth muscle cells
Eleni Charla1; Stuart A. Nicklin2; Pasquale Maffia3

1University of Glasgow, Glasgow, United Kingdom; 2Institute of Cardiovascular & Medical Sciences, University of Glasgow, Glasgow, UK, Glasgow, United Kingdom; 3Institute of Infection, Immunity and Inflammation, University of Glasgow, Glasgow, UK, Glasgow, United Kingdom

Introduction: Oxidised low‐density lipoprotein (oxLDL) is a pro‐atherogenic factor that induces signaling responses in endothelial cells (ECs) and vascular smooth muscle cells (VSMC). Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are secreted from cells and are implicated in intercellular communication predominantly via microRNA transfer. Here the effect of oxLDL stimulation on EVs isolated from VSMC was investigated.

Methods: Primary human coronary artery SMC (HCASMC) were treated ± 10μg/mL oxLDL for 24h prior to EV isolation from the media by Size‐exclusion chromatography (SEC). Small RNAs were extracted from HCASMC‐derived EVs, and small RNA sequencing was performed. 12 miRNA‐mRNA prediction algorithms were used to find predicted gene targets for small RNAs identified by RNASeq. Validation of RNASeq in SMC‐derived EVs was performed using RT‐qPCR. HCAEC were treated with HCASMC‐derived Control and oxLDL‐EVs labeled with PKH67 and uptake and EV trafficking were assessed.

Results: OxLDL treatment of HCASMC did not alter the concentration of secreted EVs compared to control HCASMC. RNASeq HCASMC‐EVs identified 475 small non‐coding RNAs (112 miRNAs, 206 snRNAs, 145 small nucleolar RNAs (snoRNAs), and 12 small Cajal‐body specific RNAs). Differential expression analysis showed that oxLDL treatment resulted in 4.5‐fold downregulation of miR‐125b‐5p (p< 0.05) and 4.5‐fold upregulation of 3 snRNAs, RNU2‐7p, RNU2‐29p, RNU2‐57p (p< 0.05). Bioinformatic analysis revealed 303 predicted and validated gene targets for miR‐125b‐5p. Validation of miR‐125b‐5p and snRNAs in HCASMC‐EVs was successful using RT‐qPCR (p< 0.05). Assessing PKH67‐labelled EV uptake into HCAEC revealed that the level of oxLDL‐EV uptake was significantly lower than control EV uptake (p< 0.05).

Summary/Conclusion: These data suggest that oxLDL treatment alters the small RNA EV cargo in SMC‐derived EVs. Future work will investigate whether miR‐125b‐5p‐EV transfer to HCASMC and HCAEC could alter cell migration or proliferation of recipient cells.

Funding: British Heart Foundation and the University of Glasgow.

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, MicroRNAs, atherosclerosis

PF10.05.

## Yeast Mitochondrial Derived Vesicles (MDVs) have membrane potential and contain functional ATP synthase
‏1Reut Hazan; ophry Pines2; Neta Regev‐Rudzki3; Dvora Lintzer4

1Hebrew university, Hadassa medical school, Jerusalem, Jerusalem, Israel; 2Hebrew university, Hadassa medical school, jerusalem, Jerusalem, Israel; 3Weizmann Institute of Science, Rehovot, Israel; 4Hebrew university, Jerusalem, Israel

Introduction: Mitochondria are responsible for the production of energy and for essential metabolic pathways in the cell, as well as fundamental processes such as apoptosis and aging. Interestingly, while the import machinery of proteins into mitochondria is thoroughly understood, there is currently no known protein export mechanism from mitochondria. Vesicular transport is a means of inter‐organellar communication, and in fact cells release vesicles in order to shuttle lipids, proteins, RNA and DNA between one another.

Methods: Vesicles isolation methodes, Microscopy, Biochemistry, Masspec

Results: Here we show for the first time that functional mitochondria, isolated from Saccharomyces cerevisiae can release vesicles, independent of the fission machinery. Mitochondrial secreted vesicles are relatively uniform in size, of about 100nm and carry a selection of cargo; only certain proteins are carried by these vesicles, of which some are matrix soluble and some are membrane proteins. We further demonstrate that yeast MDVs harbor a membrane potential, a functional ATP synthase complex, produce ATP, and fuse with mitochondria.

Summary/Conclusion: We suggest, that Yeast mitochondria generate MDVs which may function in organellar protein export, recovery and communication.

Keywords: Mitochondria, Mitochondrial derives vesicles

PF10.06.

## Exosome Mediated Translational Regulation in Ovarian Cancer
Sonam Mittal1; Pradeep Chaluvally Raghavan2; Sunila Pradeep3

1Medical College of Wisconsin, Milwaukee, USA; 2Megical college of Wisconsin, Milwaukee, USA; 3OB‐GYN, Medoical college of wisconsin, Milwaukee, USA

Introduction: Dysfunctional translational regulation often leads to uncontrolled cell growth and proliferation and is a key attribute cancer. Misexpression of the eukaryotic translation initiation factor eIF4E, an mRNA 5' cap‐binding protein, promotes tumorigenesis and has been implicated in cancer development and progression. Aberrant eIF4E function also disrupts key pathways important for anti‐tumor immune responsiveness in ovarian cancer, offering a possible explanation to the minimal success of an immunotherapy approach for this disease setting. We unexpectedly found that eIF4E co‐localizes and associates with extracellular vesicle (EV) proteins in ovarian cancer cells. Furthermore, our data suggests increased uptake of eIF4E packaged EVs (eIF4E‐EVs) leads to enhanced translational activity and activation of PD‐L1 expression. Unfortunately, a coherent model that explains how eIF4E‐packaged EVs impair immune response in the tumor microenvironment remains undeveloped and will be necessary to develop novel immunotherapy strategies that encourage immune cells to recognize and attack cancer cells.

Methods: We used polysome fractionation to determine whether eIF4E‐EVs affect global translation. We used multiple in vivo models by altering eIF4E expression in Rab27a KO using CRISPR/Cas9 model to study the effect of EV packaged eIF4E in tumor progression and its effect on immunotherapy. Furthermore, we also determined the effect of eIF4E packaged EVs on both protein and mRNA stability of PD‐L1 by directly measuring decay rates of endogenous mRNAs using kinetic labeling techniques and transcriptional inhibitors.

Results: This study shows that tumor cells employ EVs to deliver eIF4E to the tumor microenvironment, facilitating translational adaptation, and PD‐L1 expression to support tumor growth. In addition, our data shows eIF4E‐EVs enhances protein synthesis and eIF4E‐EVs promote tumor growth by increasing PD‐L1 expression. Our study uncovers a novel mechanism that details how eIF4E‐EVs promote translational adaptation in high‐grade serous ovarian cancer cells and facilitate to fuel tumor progression.

Summary/Conclusion: Our findings shows that inhibiting eIF4E synergizes with PD‐1 blockade, providing much needed evidence that supports the possibility of an immunotherapy‐based treatment regimen for ovarian cancer.

Funding: This work was supported in part to S.P. by the Ovarian Cancer Research Fund Alliance (OCRFA 457466, 657713), the Women's Health Research Program (WHRP), MCW Cancer Center Institutional Research Grants from the American Cancer Society (ACS‐IRG 19‐138‐34), and the Department of Defense (DoD W81XWH‐21‐1‐0361, W81XWH2110413).

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, Translational Regulation, Ovarian cancer

PF10.07.

## The role of executioner caspases in extracellular vesicle biogenesis
Christina Nedeva; Suresh Mathivanan; Sai V. Chitti

La Trobe University, Melbourne, Australia

Introduction: Almost 30 years ago the first executioner caspase was identified in C. elegans, which was proposed to have apoptotic functions. This ground‐breaking discovery paved the way for an explosion of studies investigating the roles of caspases in apoptosis, establishing their pro‐death role in effectively all metazoan species. More recently, nonlethal roles for caspases have been described including activity in cellular remodeling, differentiation and even survival. Moreover, studies have described non‐apoptotic activity of the protease caspase‐3 in chick auditory brain development. Further investigation revealed an enrichment of caspase‐substrate proteins in auditory brainstem‐derived extracellular vesicles (EVs). EVs are small membrane‐bound structures released by virtually all cells that function in inter‐cellular communication. However, the role of caspases in EV biogenesis is yet to be investigated.

Methods: We have recently performed whole proteomic analysis of EV's isolated from established TK6 cell lines deficient in executioner caspases 3 and 7. Analysis of these cells and their respective EV's has allowed identification of caspase substrates that participate in the EV biogenesis process. These substrates have been validated using western blotting and rescue experiments.

Results: Our results showed that cells lacking caspase 3 or 7 produced EVs that were smaller and fewer in number compared to WT‐derived EVs. Quantitative proteomic analysis highlighted that loss of caspases modified the protein cargo of small and large extracellular vesicles. Functional annotation analysis deduced from proteomics data revealed that several proteins involved in cell adhesion, exocytosis and in ESCRT III assembly are down‐regulated in the absence of caspases 3 and/ or 7. These protein candidates include VPS4 and CHMP proteins.

Summary/Conclusion: Our early findings suggest a novel mechanism for executioner caspases in regulating EV biogenesis via the regulation of known mediators of this process. These findings allow us to better understand how these processes work and how they may regulate pathology associated with their dis regulation.

PF10.08.

## A role for integrin beta 1 in extracellular vesicle‐mediated functional RNA delivery
Omnia M. Elsharkasy1; Olivier G de Jong2; Leanne van der Werff1; Raymond M. Schiffelers3; Pieter Vader4

1CDL Research, University Medical Center Utrecht, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands; 2Utrecht University, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands; 3University Medical Center Utrecht, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands; 4CDL research, University Medical Center Utrecht, Utrecht, The Netherlands, Utrecht, Netherlands

Introduction: While various uptake mechanisms for extracellular vesicles (EVs) have been widely explored, more work is required to fully unravel the underlying molecular mechanisms by which EVs are able to provide functional RNA transfer to the recipient cells. Here, we demonstrate a role for integrin beta 1 (ITGB1) in recipient cells in EV‐mediated uptake and RNA delivery.

Methods: To study RNA transfer we used a fluorescent stoplight reporter system which is activated by Cas9 protein upon functional sgRNA delivery. HEK293T reporter cells were transfected with ITGB1 siRNA or non‐coding siRNA as a control. ITGB1 knockdown efficiency was determined using RT‐qPCR. EVs from MDA‐MB‐231 cells expressing targeting sgRNA were isolated by tangential flow filtration followed by size exclusion chromatography and were then added to HEK293T reporter cells. Functional RNA transfer was quantified by flow cytometry. For uptake experiments, EVs were labelled using MemGlow640.

Results: ITGB1 mRNA knockdown efficiency in recipient cells was ∼ 85%. EVs containing targeting sgRNA showed functional RNA delivery in cells transfected with non‐coding siRNA but not in cells transfected with ITGB1 siRNA, both in a co culture setting and using isolated EVs. Complementarily, knockdown of ITGB1 or blockage using neutralizing antibodies resulted in a significant decrease in EV uptake in the recipient cells.

Summary/Conclusion: MDA‐MB‐231‐EV uptake and EV‐mediated functional RNA transfer into HEK293T recipient cells is mediated by ITGB1. This demonstrates a role for ITGB1 in EV‐mediated RNA transfer, which could contribute to achieving more efficacious EV‐mediated RNA delivery and improved RNA therapeutics.

Funding: The work of O.M.E, L.v.d.W, R.M.S. and P.V. is supported by the European Union's Horizon 2020 Research and Innovation Programme under grant agreement No. 825828. O.G.d.J. is supported by a VENI Fellowship from the Netherlands Organisation for Scientific Research (NWO) VI.Veni.192.174.

Keywords: RNA delivery, EV uptake, drug delivery

PF10.09.

## A novel role for Caveolin‐1 in extracellular vesicles (EV) production and dynamics under mechanical stress
Cristian Saquel1; Christine Viaris de Lesegno2; Cedric Blouin2; Christophe Lamaze2

1Institut Curie, Paris, France., Paris, France; 2Institut Curie, Paris, France

Introduction: Caveolae, along with their main constituents Caveolin‐1 (Cav1) and Cavin1, have been established as new mechanosensors and mechanotransducers by our laboratory. Thus, caveolae can flatten in response to different types of mechanical stress, which in turn protects the cell by providing extra membrane that was stored in the caveolar invagination, acting thereby as a membrane tension buffer. Interestingly, Cav1 has also been found on extracellular vesicles (EV) derived from cancer cells that have been observed to promote malignancy traits in recipient cells including enhanced invasion and migration. My aim is to analyze the role of caveolae, Cav1 and Cavin1 in EV biology, including composition, cargo, dynamics i.e. release and uptake by cells, in different models of cancer cells under mechanical stress.

Methods: I used 2D hypo‐osmotic shock and 3D dextran compression as mechanical stress models, I purified EVs from these conditions and characterised the samples by electronic microscopy and western blot analysis for EV markers. To measure EV release I used a pH‐sensitive dye (pHluorin‐CD63) that allowed me to visualise in real time MVB‐PM fusion events. For the analysis of the uptake of EVs I used a nanoluciferase‐HSP70 construct and GFP‐HSP70, that allowed me to visualise the uptake by luminescence and by fluorescence in recipient cells.

Results: I observed that mechanical stress led to a significant increase of released EVs with typical morphology and markers of small EVs; I was able to visualise this effect in live cells using live microscopy. In both cases, the depletion of Cav1 or Cavin1 inhibited this increased release of EVs from donor cells.
When I analysed the effect of the absence or overexpression of caveolar components in the uptake of EVs I observed that the overexpression of Cav1 showed an increase uptake of EVs. Interestingly, the uptake was shown to be Cav1 dependent but not Caveolae dependent as shown on knock out experiments were the expression of only Cav1 is enough to observe the increased uptake phenotype.

Summary/Conclusion: Taken together, these results open a new and interesting link between caveolae and EV dynamics including release and uptake by cells under mechanical stress. For the remainder of my project I will delve deeper into the molecular mechanisms underlying these changes and further validate my in vitro results with a physiological model more relevant to cancer biology in vivo.

Funding: Fonds France‐Canada pour la Recherche (FFCR)

Keywords: Caveolae, Caveolin‐1, Mechanical stress, EV secretion, EV uptake

PF10.10.

## PLD2‐MVB12B support small extracellular vesicle production
Antonio L. Egea‐Jimenez1; Lukas Hyka2; Stéphane Audebert3; Monica Castro‐Cruz4; Rania Ghossoub5; Jean‐Paul Borg6; Guido David7; Luc Camoin3; Pascale Zimmermann8

1INSERM, Marseille, France; 2KU Leuven, Leuven, Belgium; 3Centre de Recherche en Cancérologie de Marseille (CRCM), Aix‐Marseille Université, Marseille, France; 4KU leuven, Leuven, Belgium; 5Cancer Research of Marseille ‐ Aix‐Marseille University, Marseille, France; 6CRCM, Marseille, France; 7Centre de Recherche en Cancérologie de Marseille, Marseille, France; 8KU Leuven, MARSEILLE 09, France

Introduction: Phospholipase D2 (PLD2) is a multidomain protein converting phosphatidylcholine into phosphatidic acid (PA) and choline. We previously reported that PLD2 works downstream of the small GTPase ARF6 to stimulate endosomal budding (EB) and the production of syntenin and CD63‐positive exosomes (Ghossoub, 2014). Here we further investigated the role of PLD2 in the production of extracellular vesicles (EV).

Methods: We prepared mutants by site‐directed mutagenesis; silenced protein expression by siRNA treatment; inhibited PLD2 activity with VU0364739; purified EV by differential centrifugation, characterized protein composition by western blot and differential proteomics; studied endosomal budding by electron & fluorescence microscopy; studied protein‐protein interactions by co‐immunoprecipitation; characterized protein‐lipid interactions by surface plasmon resonance.

Results: We show that PLD2 effects on EVs go beyond syntenin and CD63 exosomes in MCF‐7 cells, impacting on the secretion of CD9, CD81 and flotillin positive small EV (sEV), but not on large EVs. Inhibition of PLD2 activity or PLD2 silencing reduce sEV number by 60%, while its overexpression increases the overall sEV number. Effects rely on several PLD2 domains. Yet, PLD2‐dependent EB strictly depends on the lipase activity. We reveal that PLD2 recruits the ESCRTI component MVB12B to late endosomes, and that MVB12B binds to PA. We found that MVB12B loss‐of‐function phenocopies the effect of PLD2 loss‐of‐function on EB, and also the general effects on sEV number and composition.

Summary/Conclusion: We propose that PLD2 works in sEV biogenesis by producing PA on late endosomes, recruiting MVB12B to drive EB for the production of exosomes. Since MVB12B has broad effects, PLD2‐MVB12B might also work at the plasma membrane or alternatively sEV production is restricted to endosomes in MCF‐7 cells.

Funding: FWO‐G0C5718N, ANR‐18‐CE13‐0017, Ligue contre le cancer France, EU Horizon 2020 Marie Sklodowska‐Curie.

PF10.11.

## Effect of Atg8 knockdown in HeLa cell lines on secretion of CD81+ extracellular vesicles
Berglind E. Benediktsdóttir1; Ingibjorg Steinunn Sigurbjörnsdottir2; Þengill Fannar Jónsson3; Margrét Helga Ögmundsdóttir4

1Faculty of Pharmaceutical Science, School of Health Sciences, University of Iceland, Reykjavík, Iceland; 2Faculty of Pharmaceutical Science, School of Health Sciences, University of Iceland, Akranesi, Iceland; 3Faculty of Medicine, School of Health Sciences, University of Iceland, Reykjavík, Iceland; 4Faculty of Medicine, School of Health Sciences, University of Iceland, Reykjavik, Iceland

Introduction: Autophagy is a degradation process important for cellular homeostasis and is impaired during various disease progression such as cancer. Various authophagy related genes (ATGs) encode for proteins participating in the autophagosomal membrane formation, including the ATG8 protein family (LC3s and GABARAPs). The possible interplay between extracellular vesicles (EVs) and autophagy proteins is becoming increasingly clear. ATGs have been implicated in cellular secretion of EVs in a function distinct from their role in the classical autophagy pathway. The aim of this study was to determine if knockouts (KOs) of ATG8 genes would affect EV release and content.

Methods: The HeLa cell lines: wild‐type (WT), HexaKO (LC3 and GABARAP), triple KO (TKO) of LC3 and TKO of GABARAP were cultured in serum free medium (+antibiotics) 48h (cell viability 95‐98%) before EV isolation with CD81 Fab‐TACS affinity capture (IBA) according to manufacturers protocol. NanoSight was used for particle concentration and size determination: camera level 15, 60 sec capture, four repeats. Concentrated samples were analyzed with capillary western blotting (WB; anti‐CD9 1:40, Cell Signalling, 13403) and TEM. Statistical tests include students paired t‐test, p < 0.05 regarded as statistically significant.

Results: WT isolates contained significantly higher number of particles compared to HexaKO isolate (1.56*107 compared to 0.83*107 particles/ml/million cells) (n = 4). Early results on the TKO cell lines (n=2) indicate reduced particle release for those cell lines as well. EVs from the different HeLa cell lines all had similar mean size (94.8‐104.9 nm) and mode size (74.2‐80.4 nm). Cell lysates (CL) were positive for CD9 (32 kDa) whilst EV samples had WB bands appearing at ∼144kDa. TEM images revealed vesicles in the size range of 80‐150 nm. This suggests positive isolation of EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: KO of all ATG8 specific genes reduced the number of secreted EVs, suggesting that ATG proteins participate in CD81+ EVs release and possibly their biogenesis. The mechanism by which the ATG8 proteins participate in the EV process remains somewhat unclear. Dissecting this interplay at a molecular level will be important for better understanding the role of autophagy and EVs in health and disease.

Funding: Icelandic Research Fund 228586‐051 (MHO).

Keywords: Autophagy,ATG8

PF10.12.

## Investigation of extracellular vesicles derived from cardiac cell lines under normoxia and hypoxia
Anna Koncz1; Krisztina Németh1; Lilla Turiák2; Dorina Lenzinger1; Krisztina V. Vukman1; Péter Lőrincz3; Helga Zelenyánszki4; Edit I. Buzás5; Tamás Visnovitz1

1Semmelweis University, Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Budapest, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 2Research Centre for Natural Sciences, Institute of Organic Chemistry, Budapest, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 3Eötvös Loránd University, Department of Anatomy, Cell and Developmental Biology, Budapest, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 4Eötvös Loránd University, Department of Plant Physiology and Molecular Plant Biology, Budapest, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 5Semmelweis University Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Budapest, Hungary

Introduction: Myocardial dysfunctions are one of the leading cause of death in developed countries. Extracellular vesicles (EVs), released by these cells may help in early diagnostics and therapy. Cardiac cell lines (such as H9c2, AC16 and HL1) are widely used in cardiovascular EV research as in vitro models. Increasing number of studies are using these cell lines, although they have significant limitations. We have decided to perform comparative characterization of EVs released by differentiated H9c2, AC16 and HL1 cells. The aim of the recent project was to compare cardiac cells derived EVs under normal and hypoxic conditions.

Methods: Cell lines, AC16 (human), HL1 (mouse) and H9c2 (rat) cardiomyoblast cells were used in this study. Following differentiation protocols, small‐ medium‐ and large‐sized EVs (sEV, mEV, lEVs) were collected from conditioned medium of normal and hypoxia treated cells. EVs separated by a combination of gravity filtration, differential centrifugation and tangential flow filtration (TFF). EVs were characterized according to MISEV 2018 by MicroBCA, SPV‐Lipid assay, Nanoparticle Tracking Analysis (NTA) and transmission electron microscopy (TEM). The protein profile of sEV and mEV were determined by mass spectrometry. Some of the proteomic hits were validated by Western blotting, flow cytometry and immunogold TEM. EV release was examined by fluorescent live‐cell imaging using GFP‐fusion protein transfected cells.

Results: One of our most relevant proteomic hits was endoplasmin (ENPL, grp94, gp96). It was specifically present in EVs released by hypoxia‐treated cells. Even though ENPL is an endoplasmic reticulum resident chaperon, its presence in EVs were confirmed by Western blotting, flow cytometry and Immunogold TEM. Secretion of ENPL containing EVs was analyzed by confocal microscopy using fixed and living HL1 cells.

Summary/Conclusion: We provide evidence that ENPL‐containing EVs are released by cardiac cell lines. We suggest that ENPL may take part in cardio‐protection during hypoxia through reducing ER stress.

Funding: This work was funded by the NVKP_16‐1‐2016‐0004 grant of the Hungarian National Research, Development and Innovation Office (NKFIH), Semmelweis Innovation Fund (STIA 2020 KFI), Hungarian Scientific Research Fund (OTKA K120237), VEKOP‐2.3.2‐16‐2016‐00002, VEKOP‐2.3.3‐15‐2017‐00016, Higher Education Excellence Program (FIKP) Therapeutic Thematic Programme.
Participation of Anna Koncz in ISEV2022 is supported by Bioxol Ltd.

Keywords: cardiac extracellular vesicles, endoplasmin, cardiac cell lines

## PF11: Late‐Breaking Poster Session 5: Physiology/Pathology I

## Chair: Janusz Rak, MD, PhD – Professor

## Chair: Houjian Cai, n/a – University of Georgia

PF11.01.

## Mouse Tumor Tissue‐Derived Extracellular Vesicles Induce Angiogenesis Through Macrophage Infiltration
Seoyoon Bae; Yong Song Gho

POSTECH, Pohang, Republic of Korea

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are lipid bilayer‐enclosed vesicles secreted by all types of cells. As EVs have emerged as mediators of intercellular communication, the roles of EVs in tumor microenvironment are being actively investigated. Angiogenesis, the formation of blood vessels from pre‐existing vasculature, is essential for tumor growth and metastasis. Several previous reports suggested that EVs from in vitro cultured cancer cells have potent angiogenic activities. However, the angiogenic role of in vivo tumor tissue‐derived EVs has never been investigated. In this study, we isolated mouse tumor tissue‐derived EVs (tEVs) with high purity and investigated the role of tEVs in vitro and in vivo angiogenic activities.

Methods: B16BL6 cells were subcutaneously injected into C57BL6 mice. On day 25 after injection, mice were sacrificed and viable regions of tumors were soaked into serum‐free MEM and incubated for 2 h at 4°C. After incubation, tEVs were isolated by the combination of sequential centrifugation, buoyant density gradient ultracentrifugation, and ultracentrifugation. The purified tEVs were characterized according to the MISEV 2018 guideline. Pro‐angiogenic activity of tEVs were evaluated by both in vitro endothelial cell proliferation, migration, and differentiation assay and in vivo Matrigel plug assay.

Results: The purified tEVs had approximately 140 nm in a diameter, spherical lipid bilayer structure, and enriched with EV marker proteins, including CD81 and CD9 while no contamination of subcellular organelles such as Golgi apparatus and nucleus. In vivo Matrigel plug assay showed that the tEVs had similar pro‐angiogenic activity to that of VEGF. However, tEVs did not induce endothelial cell proliferation, migration, and differentiation but induced macrophage migration in vitro. In addition, we observed that tEVs induce the infiltration of macrophage into tumor tissue. Furthermore, depletion of tumor tissue‐infiltrated macrophage by the treatment of clodronate liposome, a macrophage depletion reagent, completely blocked tEVs‐induced in vivo angiogenic activity in Matrigel plug assay.

Summary/Conclusion: Take together with all data, mouse tumor tissue‐derived EVs has strong in vivo angiogenic activity by induction of Macrophage infiltration into tumor tissue. This study provides the first direct evidence on the role of tEVs in tumor angiogenesis.

Funding: This work was supported by National Research Foundation of Korea (NRF) grant funded by the Korea government (MSIT) (2021R1A2C3005275).

Keywords: Tumor Tissue‐Derived Extracellular Vesicles, Tumor Microenvironment, Angiogenesis, Macrophage

PF11.02.

## Picornavirus proteins promote the release of virus‐carrying EV subsets via the modulation of host kinases
Kyra A.Y. Defourny1; Xinyi Pei1; Frank J.M. Van Kuppeveld2; Esther N.M Nolte‐'t Hoen1

1Division of Cell Biology, Metabolism & Cancer, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, Netherlands; 2Virology Section, Division Infectious Diseases & Immunology, Department of Biomolecular Health Sciences, Faculty of Veterinary Medicine, Utrecht University, Utrecht, Netherlands

Introduction: Virus infection can affect the release and composition of extracellular vesicles (EVs) produced by host cells. These EVs can affect disease severity by promoting virus spread or altering antiviral immune responses. Currently, little is known about the type of virus‐host interactions that influence the release of EVs by infected cells. We previously described an important role for the viral Leader protein of the picornavirus EMCV in the induction of EV subsets with distinct molecular and physical properties. These EVs contained mature virus particles and efficiently transmitted the infection. Here, we used recombinant viruses that differentially interact with host signaling pathways as well as various knock‐out cells to further delineate molecular pathways involved in the release of picornavirus‐induced EVs.

Methods: EVs released by infected cells were isolated by UC pelleting/PEG precipitation followed by iodixanol density gradient centrifugation. Quantitative and qualitative analysis of EVs was performed using high‐resolution flow cytometry and western blot analysis. Infectivity of EVs was assessed in CCID50 assays.

Results: Virus‐induced inhibition of the antiviral sensor PKR contributed to the release of virus‐containing EVs, whereas signaling pathways leading to type I interferon production were not involved in EV release. Moreover, we observed that the ability of recombinant viruses to efficiently induce EV release correlated with their ability to induce lytic instead of apoptotic cell death, along with the activation of the stress kinase P38 MAPK.

Summary/Conclusion: Our data highlight several host kinases that are key to inhibiting or promoting EV release during virus infection. These kinases are targeted by specific virus proteins during infection to promote the release of virus‐carrying EVs. Insights into the molecular regulation of EV release during infection can reveal candidate therapeutic targets to counter the effect of EVs in virus‐mediated pathology.

Funding: This work was supported by The Netherlands Organisation for Scientific Research (NWO‐ALW grant number ALWOP.351).

Keywords: virus infection, virus‐host interactions, naked viruses, antiviral signalling

PF11.03.

## Optimizing isolation and purification of EV‐like nanoparticles from ginger rhizome (Zingiber officinale) by enzyme‐assisted cell wall digestion
Francesca Loria1; Andres Lõhmus1; Danilo Mladenović1; Paolo Guazzi1; Jekaterina Kazantseva2; Raivo Vilu2; Natasa Zarovni1

1HansaBioMed Life Sciences Ltd, Mäealuse 2/1, 12618 Tallinn, Estonia, Tallinn, Estonia; 2Center of Food and Fermentation Technologies, Akadeemia tee 15A, 12618 Tallinn, Estonia, Tallinn, Estonia

Introduction: Extracellular Vesicle (EV)‐like nanoparticles have been recently recognized as bioactive components of ginger (Zingiber officinale), proven to hold health beneficial properties and displaying encouraging efficacy and safety profiles in pre‐clinical settings. Hitherto, most protocols for isolation and purification of ginger‐derived nanoparticles (GDNs) have relied on methods limiting or poorly promoting their extraction from the apoplast. Our objective was to investigate enzyme‐assisted ginger rhizome cell wall digestion for improved downstream GDN isolation and purification efficiency.

Methods: Ginger was washed, peeled and ground using a centrifugal juicer to extract juice and dry residues. Three concentrations of a commercially available multi‐enzyme cocktail containing a wide spectrum of carbohydrases [0%, 0.1% and 0.5% (v/w)] were used to perform overnight pre‐treatment, under constant shaking, at 37◦C and pH 5.5. For GDN isolation and purification, differential centrifugation, filtration and size exclusion chromatography were adopted. GDN characterization was executed by nanoparticle tracking analysis, protein quantification assay, enzymatic activity assays and fluorometric microplate‐based nucleic acid (NA) and lectin assays.

Results: Compared with control isolation and purification workflow, digestion with 0.1% multi‐enzyme cocktail enabled 47% increase in particle recovery, associated with particle size and protein content preservation, as well as only minor changes in particle enzymatic activity, NA content and lectin‐associated glycan expression. Pre‐treatment with 0.5% multi‐enzyme cocktail suggested substantial GDN degradation.

Summary/Conclusion: Enzymatic pre‐treatment of ginger rhizome resulted in optimization of original GDN isolation and purification protocol, as highlighted by the observed process yield improvement in correlation with preservation of tested biocomponents and enzymatic activity. This upgraded workflow, complemented with multiparametric quantity and quality assessment, has high translational potential into GDN functional testing and ultimate proof‐of‐concept clinical and/or industrial application.

Funding: This work is funded by the Horizon 2020 Framework Programme under the grant BOW ID: 952183.

PF11.04.

## Isolation and characterization of extracellular vesicles secreted by the pathogenic free‐living amoeba Naegleria fowleri
Lissette Retana Moreira1; María Fernanda Steller Espinoza2; Alberto Cornet Gómez3; Natalia Chacón Camacho2; Giovanni Saenz Arce4; Oscar Rojas Carrillo5; Antonio Osuna3; Elizabeth Abrahams Sandí1

1Departamento de Parasitología, Facultad de Microbiología, Universidad de Costa Rica; Centro de Investigación en Enfermedades Tropicales (CIET), Facultad de Microbiología, Universidad de Costa Rica., San José, Costa Rica; 2Departamento de Parasitología, Facultad de Microbiología, Universidad de Costa Rica., San José, Costa Rica; 3Departamento de Parasitología, Grupo de Bioquímica y Parasitología Molecular CTS‐183, Universidad de Granada, Granada, Spain; Instituto de Biotecnología, Universidad de Granada, Granada, Spain., Granada, Spain; 4Departamento de Física, Universidad Nacional, Heredia, Costa Rica., Heredia, Costa Rica; 5Laboratorio de Investigación y Tecnología de Polímeros, Escuela de Química, Universidad Nacional, Heredia, Costa Rica., Heredia, Costa Rica

Introduction: Naegleria fowleri is a thermophilic free living amoeba that produces primary amoebic meningoencephalitis (PAM), a severe and fulminant infection of the central nervous system (fatal in more than 95% of cases). Until 2019, more than 440 cases of PAM were reported worldwide.
Due to the characteristics of PAM, rapid and accurate diagnosis and early treatment are key to fight this infection. In this sense, a reliable diagnosis panel using the pathogen‐specific extracellular vesicles (EVs) could probably improve on‐time intervention and prognosis.
To our knowledge, the secretion of EVs by trophozoites of N. fowleri has not been reported yet. In this study, we present a characterization of EVs secreted by two clinic isolates of N. fowleri from Costa Rica.

Methods: EVs of trophozoites were isolated by ultracentrifugation. Characterization analyses performed included transmission electron microscopy, atomic force microscopy, nanoparticle tracking analyisis and dynamic light scattering. Protein profiles of EVs were compared after Coomasie and silver stainings. Protease activity was analyzed by zimography and recognition of EV proteins by polyclonal anti‐N. fowleri antibodies was achieved using Western blot. Preliminary proteomic analyses were also performed.

Results: The production of EVs from both clinic isolates of N. fowleri was confirmed. Protein profiles, protease activity and types of proteases of EVs from both isolates resulted very similar. Western blot analysis revealed differences in protein recognition by polyclonal anti‐N. fowleri antibodies; a higher recognition of EV proteins under 58 KDa in one of the isolates was identified.

Summary/Conclusion: The production of EVs by two clinic isolates of N. fowleri was confirmed. The results obtained in this study are necessary as they precede complete proteomic and functional analyses of EVs secreted by this pathogen, which will include an evaluation of effect of these EVs in pathogenesis and immunomodulation, and to latter explore the possibility of employing EVs for diagnostic purposes.

Funding: This work was funded by the "Vicerrectoría de Investigación" of the "Universidad de Costa Rica".
Research projects related to this work: 

‐C‐1061: "Caracterización de antígenos de excreción/secreción y antígenos somáticos en amebas de vida libre mediante empleo de anticuerpos policlonales producidos en roedores".
‐C‐2600: "Secreción de vesículas extracelulares por Naegleria fowleri y evaluación de su potencial rol inmunomodulador en un modelo in vitro".

Keywords: Primary amoebic meningoencefalitis, Naegleria fowleri, trophozoites

PF11.05.

## Body fluid‐derived EVs exert selective antiviral activity with variable potency
Rüdiger Groß1; Markus Hoffmann2; Mirko Cortese3; Dhanu Gupta4; Stefan Pöhlmann2; Ralf Bartenschlager3; Samir El Andaloussi5; Jan Münch6; Janis A Müller6

1Ulm University Medical Center, Institute of Molecular Virology, Ulm, Germany; 2Infection Biology Unit, German Primate Center, Göttingen, Germany, Göttingen, Germany; 3Department of Infectious Diseases, Molecular Virology, Heidelberg University, Heidelberg, Germany, Heidelberg, Germany; 4Department of laboratory medicine, Karolinska Institutet, Stockholm, Sweden; 5Department of Laboratory Medicine, Karolinska Institutet, Stockholm, Sweden, Huddinge, Sweden; 6Institute of Molecular Virology, Ulm University Medical Centre, Ulm, Germany, Ulm, Germany

Introduction: We have previously shown EVs from seminal plasma (SP) and saliva to inhibit Zika virus (ZIKV) infection in vitro. These findings offer clues on the lack of sexual and oral transmission of ZIKV despite high viral RNA levels in both body fluids. It is unclear, if this activity is common to EVs from all sources and which viruses are affected by it.

Methods: Tangential flow filtration combined with bind‐elute size exclusion chromatography was used to purify EVs from human SP, saliva, urine, breast milk, serum and HEK293T cell culture supernatant. EV preparations were analyzed by western blot and characterized using NTA, DLS, TEM and bead‐assisted flow cytometry to quantify EV membrane proteins. Antiviral activity of these EVs was evaluated against ZIKV, West Nile Virus (WNV), HIV‐1, herpes simplex viruses (HSV) 1/2, SARS‐CoV‐2, and Ebola VLPs.

Results: EV purification was confirmed by enrichment of CD9, Flotillin‐1, Alix and Hsp70 and depletion of matrix proteins. EVs from all sources inhibited ZIKV with variable potency: EVs from cell culture, SP and saliva were most potent (IC50 ∼ 10ˆ10 particles/ml), while EVs from urine, breast milk and serum required 10x more particles. SP‐derived EVs prevented virion attachment and also inhibited infection of ex vivo vaginal explants and foreskin fibroblasts. These EVs further potently inhibited infection by WNV and Ebola VLPs, but not HIV‐1, HSV‐1/2 or SARS‐CoV‐2. MACSplex revealed a positive correlation of CD63 and CD81 levels with potency of anti‐ZIKV activity, in agreement with antiviral activity exerted by classical EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: Anti‐ZIKV activity seems to be a general property of EVs, but variable potencies suggest compositional differences between body fluids. SP‐EVs inhibited ZIKV infection in cells and tissue of the genital tract, supporting their role in restricting sexual ZIKV transmission. Antiviral activity against certain but not all enveloped viruses may offer hints on innate transmission barriers and the underlying mechanism.

Keywords: Body fluids, viruses, zika, ebola, west nile virus, hiv, hsv, immunity, innate immunity, sexual virus transmission

PF11.06.

## Role of P2X ionotropic receptors in exosomal release from cancer cells and consequences on tumors progression
Thomas Duret1; Stéphanie Chadet1; Roxane lemoine1; Audrey Héraud1; Pierre Besson1; Lili Fan2; Gang Yin2; Christophe Baron1; Sébastien Roger1

1University of Tours, Tours, France; 2Central South University, Changsha, China (People's Republic)

Introduction: Exosomes are a subtype of microvesicules originary from the Multivesicular Bodies (MVBs) compartment, well‐known to be involved in cancer cell aggressiveness. Even though it is known that the release of exosomes is due to the fusion of MVBs to plasma membrane, the molecular process involved is not very clear. In this study, we investigated the role of ionotropic purinoreceptors P2X4 and P2X7 in exosomes release, composition and consequences in cancer aggressiveness.

Methods: We have generated stable clones knocked down for the expression of either P2X4 (4T1‐CR4) or P2X7 (4T1‐CR7) from 4T1 cancer cell line, using CRISPR‐Cas9, and compared to a null‐target CRISPER‐Cas9 cell line (4T1‐CTL). Exosomes from supernatants of theses cell lines were purified by ultracentrifugation, characterized by scanning electron microscopy and nanoparticule tracking assay (NTA). The expression of intracellular CD9, CD63, and CD81 was observed by flow cytometry. Purified exosomes were labeled with PKH67 to evaluate their integrating capacity. We developed co‐culture assays between donor 4T1‐CTL, CR4, CR7, and 4T1‐CTL recipient cells, in presence or not of the exosomes release inhibitor GW4869, to investigate the capacity of exosomes to modulate the invasion of recipient cells.

Results: NTA from 4T1‐CTL supernatants after ultracentrifugation‐based purification indicates the presence of particles of size below 200 nm. These express CD63 and PD‐L1, as identified by immunogold labelling in scanning electron microscopy. Intracellular content of CD9, CD63, and CD81 increases in both 4T1‐CR4 and CR7 cell lines compared to 4T1‐CTL suggesting an intracellular accumulation of exosomes. PKH67‐stained exosomes can integrate into recipient 4T1‐CTL cell line. 4T1‐CTL recipient cells co‐cultured with 4T1‐CR4 donor cells show a reduced invasion to those co‐cultured with 4T1‐CTL donor cells.

Summary/Conclusion: P2X4 seems to modulate the release, or composition, of proinvasive exosomes by 4T1 cancer cells.

Funding: This project is funded by the Région Centre‐Val de Loire (Project CanalEx and PhD grant) and the INCa PLBIO_16110 “PURIN4EXO”.

PF11.07.

## Distinct EV profiles of rheumatoid arthritis patients in response to methotrexate treatment
Anne Rydland1; Fatima Heinicke2; Siri Flåm3; Anne‐Marie Siebke Trøseid4; Reidun Øvstebø5; Maria Mjaavatten6; Benedicte Lie7

1Department of Medical Genetics, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway. Institute of Clinical Medicine, University of Oslo, Oslo, Norway, OSLO, Norway; 2Department of Medical Genetics, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway. Institute of Clinical Medicine, University of Oslo, Oslo, Norway, Oslo, Norway; 3Department of Medical Genetics, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway, Oslo, Norway; 4The Blood Cell Research Group, Department of Biochemistry, Oslo University Hospital Ullevaal, Oslo, Norway, Oslo, Norway; 5Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway; 6Division of Rheumatology, Diakonhjemmet Hospital, Oslo, Norway, Oslo, Norway; 7Department of Medical Genetics, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway. Institute of Clinical Medicine, University of Oslo, Oslo, Norway. Department of Immunology, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway, Oslo, Norway

Introduction: Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) is an inflammatory autoimmune disease mainly affecting joints, although it can also lead to systemic alterations. If insufficiently treated RA can cause irreversible damage disabling affected individuals. Methotrexate (MTX) is usually the initial drug prescribed, however a proportion of patients do not respond and must undergo several treatment trials while suffering further damage. Molecular signatures for treatment response are hence warranted.

Methods: In this study, we assessed extracellular vesicle (EV) profiles of 8 RA patients stratified based on MTX treatment response and 5 matched healthy controls. EVs were isolated from 0.5 ml plasma by size exclusion chromatography and fractions 7‐9 was used in downstream analyses. Nanoparticle tracking analysis and transmission electron microscopy was performed on fresh EV material, while EV‐marker (CD9, CD63 and CD81) profiles were assessed in frozen EV aliquots utilizing the ExoView platform (NanoView Biosciences).

Results: We observed an increased concentration of EVs in RA that was driven by the responder group. Colocalization analysis revealed a decreased expression of CD9/CD81 double positive EVs in non‐responders, while responders showed distinct high expression of CD81 single positive EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: Our study revealed clear tetraspanin differences between MTX treated RA patients and controls that may be implicated in RA pathophysiology and patient treatment response.

Funding: This study was funded by the Research Council of Norway.

Keywords: rheumatoid arthritis, methotrexate, EV tetraspanins

PF11.08.

## Colorectal cancer derived EVs drive invasion and tumour microenvironment remodelling in a 3D organotypic culture model
Sonia Guarnerio1; Rob Tempest2; Christine Le Maitre1; Laura Cole1; Stuart Hunt3; Alex Mirnezami4; Nicholas Peake1

1Sheffield Hallam University, Sheffield, United Kingdom; 2NanoFCM, Nottingham, United Kingdom; 3University of Sheffield, Sheffield, United Kingdom; 4University Hospital Southampton NHS Foundation Trust, Southampton, United Kingdom

Introduction: Cancer growth and invasion is driven by numerous molecular and cellular factors in the tumour microenvironment (TME). Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have been discovered to play a pivotal role in remodelling the pre‐metastatic niche to support cancer cell invasion. Colorectal cancer (CRC) is the third most common cancer worldwide and it is frequently diagnosed in advanced stages, with a poor prognosis once metastasized. Thus, understanding the mechanisms involved in metastasis has become crucial for the improvement of diagnostic tools and treatments. Here, a 3D model was developed to evaluate the influence of CRC‐derived EVs on cancer invasion and stroma.

Methods: MRC5 fibroblasts were embedded on a mixture of collagen type I and GelTrex on a Transwell® system. SW480 colorectal cancer cells were allowed to invade into the models for 7 days. The 3D cultures were treated with EVs obtained by culturing primary and metastatic patient‐matched CRC cell lines (SW480 and SW620) in bioreactor flasks. CRC‐derived EVs were purified through size‐exclusion chromatography and fully characterised by nanoscale flow cytometry (NanoFCM). Invasive measurements were taken based on histological and immunohistochemical analysis. EV uptake was evaluated through confocal microscopy.

Results: CRC derived EVs were retained within the matrix of the 3D model, as observed through confocal microscopy. Quantitative analysis of the invasive layer highlighted changes in the invasion of cancer cells upon treatment with EVs. Invasive clusters length and size were significantly increased when the 3D culture was treated with SW620 EVs. Stromal cross‐linking activity around the invasive layer was also increased when the model was treated with CRC derived EVs. Interestingly, while inhibition of SW480 proliferation was observed in EV‐treated monocultures, MRC5 proliferation increased, indicating involvement of stromal mechanism surrounding the tumour.

Summary/Conclusion: The results obtained indicated a direct role of metastatic cancer derived EVs in driving cancer invasiveness. Conclusively, the 3D model developed was suitable for the analysis of stromal cancer cells invasion and provides a versatile system for the study of EVs within colorectal cancer metastasis.

Funding: Bowel Research UK, Sheffield Hallam University

Keywords: Cancer EVs, Colorectal cancer, Tumour microenvironment, 3D model

PF11.09.

## Insulin‐resistant M2‐CD163+ macrophages release extracellular vesicles affecting lipid metabolism and extracellular matrix gene expression in muscle cells
Stefano Tacconi1; Anna Maria Giudetti2; Emmanuelle Meugnier3; Serena Longo2; Federica Angilé2; Francesco Paolo Fanizzi2; Elisa Panzarini4; Francesco Mura5; Marco Rossi6; Marco Fidaleo1; Audrey Jalabert3; Benjamin Gillet7; Sandrine Hughes3; Rienk Nieuwland8; Sophie Rome3; Luciana Dini1

1Department of Biology and Biotechnology "C. Darwin", Sapienza University of Rome, Rome, Italy; 2Department of Biological and Environmental Sciences and Technologies, Lecce, Italy; 3CarMeN Laboratory (UMR INSERM 1060/INRA 1397), Lyon‐Sud Faculty of Medicine, University of Lyon, Lyon, France; 4Department of Biological and Environmental Sciences and Technologies, University of Salento, Lecce, Italy; 5Research Center of Nanotechnologies for Engineering (CNIS), Sapienza University of Rome, Rome, Italy; 6Research Center of Nanotechnologies for Engineering (CNIS), Sapienza University of Rome, Roma, Italy; 7Institut de Génomique Fonctionnelle de Lyon (IGFL), Université de Lyon, Ecole Normale Supérieure de Lyon, CNRS UMR 5242, Lyon, France; 8Department of Clinical Chemistry; Vesicle Observation Center; Amsterdam University Medical Center, University of Amsterdam, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Introduction: Skeletal muscle (SkM) insulin resistance is an early event in obesity‐induced diabetes, characterized by increased numbers of muscle‐resident macrophages. We tested whether muscle‐resident macrophages release extracellular vesicles (EVs) affecting SkM homeostasis.

Methods: Small and large EVs (sEV and lEV) were collected by differential centrifugation from macrophages treated with palmitate + oleate (FFA) with or without 15 mM D‐glucose (FFA/G15). Macrophages were characterized by quantifying polarization markers, lipid composition, and insulin sensitivity. C2C12 myotubes were treated with sEV or lEV to determine their possible effects on muscle lipid composition, gene expression, and insulin‐induced AKT phosphorylation.

Results: FFA‐treated macrophages polarized into M2‐CD163+ which released pro‐/anti‐inflammatory cytokines, accumulated triacylglycerols (TAG), FFA, phospholipids, and had reduced insulin‐sensitivity compared to untreated macrophages. Myotubes treated with lEV‐FFA accumulated TAG and FFA and had reduced insulin‐sensitivity compared to untreated cells, reproducing the FFA action on macrophages. lEV‐FFA also induced the expression of extracellular matrix components. sEV‐FFA reduced the expression of genes involved in lipid oxidation and mitochondrial respiration and induced TAG accumulation without affecting muscle insulin resistance. By increasing myotube sphingomyelin concentrations, sEV‐FFA reduced muscle ceramides content. Thus, in diet‐induced obesity, macrophages release EVs able to participate in maintaining muscle integrity. The macrophage FFA/G15 condition reduced CD163+ and IL‐10 and increased IFNα, IL‐1beta, IL‐8. Therefore, macrophage‐FFA/G15‐released EVs lost the beneficial action of EVs from macrophage‐FFA and induced insulin‐resistance in C2C12.

Summary/Conclusion: The biological effects of EVs mirror the phenotype plasticity of the releasing macrophages recapitulating the different events leading to SkM insulin‐resistance in obesity‐induced diabetes.

Funding: This work is supported by the FRENCH AGENCY OF RESEARCH (# ANR‐21‐CE14‐0081).

Keywords: macrophages, extracellular vesicles, inflammation, diabetes, skeletal muscle, lipids

PF11.10.

## Platelet‐derived extracellular vesicles alter metastatic melanoma cell functionality
Alessandra de sousa Faria; Zeynep Tavukcuoglu; Mari Palviainen; Pia Siljander

University of Helsinki, Helsinki, Finland

Introduction: Skin melanoma is one of the most aggressive skin cancers and responsible for most of the skin cancer deaths. Experimental evidence shows that in addition to hemostasis, platelets play a role in cancer progression. Upon activation, platelets generate heterogeneous platelet‐derived extracellular vesicles (PEVs) that may educate cancer cells to favor cell proliferation and enhance metastasis in the tumor microenvironment. Here, we studied how the treatment of melanoma cells with different PEVs affected the 3D‐melanoma spheroid growth and cancer EV production.

Methods: Human platelets were activated by different platelet activators and PEVs were isolated by size exclusion chromatography and analyzed according to MISEV2018 guidelines. (ii) Melanoma: A2058 and MV3 cells were cultured as 3D spheroids by using GrowDex hydrogel. (iii) PEV‐melanoma co‐culture: EVs from PEV‐educated spheroids were isolated by ultracentrifugation and characterized by NTA and SP‐IRIS. (iv) Cells were isolated from MV3 and A2058 spheroids and intracellular signaling was determined by analysis of p‐p38, p‐FAK and p‐Src with western blotting.

Results: The normal growth of the MV3 and A2058 spheroids was altered depending on the type of PEV used to educate the spheroids. Also, the EV generation from the spheroids was shown to depend on the PEV education. CRP‐XL‐PEVs increased both the size of the spheroids and the EV production in the A2058 cell line. Fucoidan‐PEVs increased the spheroid size of the MV3 cells, whilst the highest EV production was achieved with ionophore‐PEVs. EVs from the different PEV‐educated melanoma spheroids exhibited different tetraspanin profiles. PEVs also altered signal transduction of the melanoma cells, showing modulation of proliferation pathways.

Summary/Conclusion: In conclusion, PEVs produced by different agonists modified the functions of 3D melanoma spheroids, e.g. proliferation, survival and altered EV production. Platelets and their EVs are novel immunomodulatory agents in the tumor microenvironment.

Funding: This work was supported by Academy of Finland PLEASER grant (330486), Turkish Ministry of National Education, Oskar Öflunds Stiftelse, Ida Montinin Foundation and Orion Research Foundation.

Keywords: platelets, melanoma, platelet educated tumor

PF11.11.

## Comparison of extracellular vesicles derived from porcine oviduct epithelial cells in different culture models
Jianchao Huo1; Marcus Frank2; Alireza Fazeli3; Jennifer Schoen1; Shuai Chen1

1Department of Reproduction Biology, Leibniz Institute for Zoo and Wildlife Research (IZW), Berlin, Germany, Berlin, Germany; 2Medical Biology and Electron Microscopy Centre, Rostock University Medical Center, Rostock, Germany, Rostock, Germany; 3Unit of Reproductive and Developmental Medicine, Department of Oncology and Metabolism, The Medical School, University of Sheffield, Sheffield, UK, Sheffield, United Kingdom, United Kingdom

Introduction: Oviductal extracellular vesicles (EVs) are key mediators of the maternal‐gamete/embryo crosstalk. Several culture approaches are available for cultivating oviduct epithelial cells in vitro, which may differ in terms of EV production and cargoes of EVs. We aimed to characterize and compare EVs derived from three culture models of porcine oviduct epithelial cells (POECs).

Methods: The following cell culture models were analyzed in a serum‐free medium: a) Submerged 2D culture of POEC cell line (CCLV‐RIE270) for 3 passages (3P, 3pools/P). b) Submerged 2D culture of primary POEC (N=3, 3P). c) Air‐liquid interface (ALI) culture of primary POEC (3w, N=5). The medium was refreshed 48h before collection, and cells were evaluated for morphology/histomorphometry. EVs were isolated by polyethylene glycol/ultracentrifugation and assessed by transmission electron microscopy (TEM), micro bicinchoninic acid (BCA) assay, and nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA).

Results: The cell line exhibited a typical cobblestone shape. The 2D primary culture showed a similar phenotype, however, cell shape elongated over passaging. In contrast, ALI culture highly resembled oviduct epithelium, with both ciliated (55.9%) and secretory cell types (mean height: 19.93μm). NTA (normalized by 10⁶ cells) revealed that ALI culture mainly secreted EVs into the apical side (7.32x10⁷), with very low amount released into the basal medium (7.55x10⁵, P < 0.01). ALI culture produced more EVs than 2D primary culture (6.32x10⁶) and 2D cell line (1.4x10⁷), conforming to protein assessment. The size of EVs in the cell line was smaller than in other cultures (P < 0.05). The number of EVs secreted by the cell line slightly declined over passages, while an opposite tendency was noticed in 2D primary culture. TEM visualized the spherical shape of EVs and further confirmed the NTA findings.

Summary/Conclusion: Our preliminary data highlight the ALI culture as a model for EV production, and the directional release of EVs primarily to the apical pole of the oviduct epithelium.

Funding: The study was funded by the DFG (CH 2321/1‐1) and CSC (201908350115).

PF11.12.

## Staphylococcus aureus‐derived extracellular vesicles impair the osteogenesis‐angiogenesis coupling therefore causing bone loss
Yi Li1; Haikuan Yu1; Mingming Zhang2; Ruijing Chen1; Pengbin Yin1

1Department of Orthopedics, Chinese PLA General Hospital, Beijing 100853, China;, Beijing, China (People's Republic); 2Department of Orthopedics, General Hospital of Chinese PLA, Beijing, China, Beijing, China (People's Republic)

Introduction: Bone infection is a catastrophic complication in fracture patients. This study aimed to investigate the effect of extracellular vesicles (EVs) derived from Staphylococcus aureus (S. aureus) destruct the osteogenesis‐angiogenesis coupling in both in vitro and in vivo bone infection models.

Methods: EVs isolated from S. aureus were examined the morphology and contents by transmission electron microscopy, nanoparticle tracking analysis and proteomic analysis. Preosteoclasts were cultured with EVs during osteoclast differentiation. The PDGF‐BB levels from preosteoclasts were assessed by ELISA and qPCR, respectively. A mouse model of bone infection was established by Intramedullary injection of S. aureus. At 8 weeks after the model was established, the femur samples were collected for analysis the destruction of the osteogenesis‐angiogenesis coupling.

Results: After endocytosed with the preosteoclasts in vitro, EVs from S. aureus could affect the osteoclast differentiation and inhibit the secretion of PDGF‐BB. In vivo models indicated the femur bone mineral density levels were significantly reduced after infection with S. aureus. Meanwhile, the EVs from S. aureus decreased CD31hiEmcnhi endothelial cell formation.

Summary/Conclusion: In summary, we mapped the detailed proteomic landscapes of EVs derived from S. aureus and showed that their potential biological roles are important in coupling osteogenesis‐angiogenesis during bone infection.

PS10.01.

## The MHC Class I Peptidome of Extracellular Vesicles: Detection of Tumour Associated Antigenic Epitopes In Melanoma
Caitlin Boyne1; Sydney Brown2; Pankaj Kumar2; Sally Shirran2; Simon Powis2

1University of St Andrews ‐ School of Medicine, Cupar, United Kingdom; 2University of St Andrews ‐ School of Medicine, St Andrews, United Kingdom

Introduction: Melanoma is the form of skin cancer associated with the highest rate of mortality and the incidence of the disease is increasing more rapidly than any other form of cancer (1). Extracellular vesicles (EVs) are small membrane vesicles that have been indicated as key players in the process of cell communication, in particular the communication that exists between cancer cells and the host microenvironment, both locally and distally (2). The MHC class I immunopeptidome present on EVs released by cancer cells may be the defining feature of EVs that allows them to have a significant impact on the anti‐tumour immune response. Thus, in this study we isolated EVs from a melanoma cell line in order to characterise their peptide ligandome.

Methods: The melanoma cell line ESTDAB‐056 was utilised. Immunoblot analysis of cell and EV lysates were carried out along with Nanoparticle Tracking Analysis of cell culture supernatant and flow cytometry of ESTDAB‐056 cells grown in culture to confirm the characterization of the EVs isolated and their associated MHC class I expression. Peptides were successfully eluted from triplicate cell and EV lysates using the immunoprecipitation technique and were analyzed by mass spectrometry.

Results: EVs were successfully isolated and characterized. A total of 804 peptides were detected from the cell surface and 472 peptides from the extracellular vesicles of the cell line studied. Within the extracellular vesicle ligandome we identified 24 peptides derived from tumour‐associated antigenic proteins, of which 3 have known T cell reactivity.

Summary/Conclusion: The data achieved supports the possibility of identifying peptides that could be recognized by T‐cells and mediate a reaction. The discovery of these key antigens would be a major step toward the development of a novel immunotherapy for melanoma, as well as possibly providing a novel diagnostic or prognostic tool for the disease.

Funding: Funded by Melville Trust Scotland

Keywords: melanoma; cancer; HLA‐1; extracellular vesicles; immunopeptidome; antigen presentation; mass spectrometry; cancer immunology

## Adipocyte‐derived small extracellular vesicles from patients with metabolic dysfunction may induce insulin resistance in neural progenitor cells
Wade O'Brien1; Rachael Batabyal2; Laura Reck Cechinel1; Evan Nadler1; Kazue Hashimoto‐Torii3; Robert J. Freishtat1

1Children's National Hospital, Washington, USA; 2Children's National Hospital, Columbia, USA; 3Children's National Hospital and The George Washington University, Washington, USA

Introduction: Insulin signaling in the brain is critical to neurodevelopment and cognitive function. It has been implicated in several neurological disorders. Specifically, the PI3K/AKT signaling cascade is critical for learning and memory. Adipocyte‐derived small extracellular vesicles (ad‐sEVs) are a potential mechanistic link to obesity‐related disease. In patients with adiposopathy, ad‐SEVs contain miRNAs predicted to induce systemic insulin resistance. We hypothesize that ad‐sEVs from patients with obesity and diabetes will downregulate insulin signaling in neural progenitor cells (NPCs), leading to insulin resistance.

Methods: Induced pluripotent stem cell derived NPCs were cultured for 72 hours, then exposed to 6μg/mL of ad‐sEVs isolated form visceral adipose tissue from adolescents with obesity and diabetes (n=3, OD NPCs) or obesity and insulin resistance (n=3, OI NPCs) for 24 hours while controls received no ad‐sEVs (n=3). At 96 hours, RNA was extracted and sequenced on Illumina NovaSeq. RNA sequencing data were analyzed on PartekFlow and differentially expressed genes identified using GSA.

Results: NPCs exposed to ad‐sEVs from patients with obesity and diabetes (OD NPCs) showed 2482 differentially expressed genes (FC ≥|1.9|, p < 0.05) compared to control NPCs. NPCs exposed to ad‐sEVs from patients with obesity and insulin resistance (OI NPCs) showed 114 differentially expressed genes compared to control NPCs. Between OD NPCs and OI NPCs there were 1576 differentially expressed genes. Among these differentially expressed genes, we identified downregulation of mRNAs related to insulin signaling pathways between metabolic conditions in neural progenitor cells. INSR and PI3K were downregulated in the OD NPCs compared to OI NPCs. Compared to control NPCs, OD NPCs showed downregulation of INSR, PI3K, and AKT. No change in mRNA expression was observed in OI NPCs relative to control NPCs.

Summary/Conclusion: These data suggest that ad‐sEVs from patients with diabetes may induce insulin resistance in NPCs which could affect neurocognitive function.

Funding: Children's Research Institute

PS10.04.

## Pregnancy exosome release and hsa‐miR‐126‐3p expression vary according to pancreatic β‐cell functionality
Melissa Razo‐Azamar1; Rafael Nambo‐Venegas2; Martha Guevara‐Cruz3; Berenice Palacios‐González4

1National Institute of Genomic Medicine, MEXICO CITY, Mexico; 2Laboratorio de Bioquímica de Enfermedades Crónicas Instituto Nacional de Medicina Genómica, MEXICO CITY, Mexico; 3Departamento de Fisiología de la Nutrición, Instituto Nacional de Nutrición y Ciencias Médicas Salvador Zubirán, CDMX, MEXICO CITY, Mexico; 4Unidad de Vinculación Científica Facultad de Medicina UNAM en Instituto Nacional de Medicina Genómica (INMEGEN), CDMX, MEXICO CITY, Mexico

Introduction: Gestational diabetes mellitus (GD) affects around 15% of all pregnancies worldwide. Recently, it has been suggested that maternal and placental exosomes play a key role throughout gestation. However, the mechanisms underlying maternal metabolic adaptations in GD remain poorly understood. Thus, we assessed serum exosome profiles and the EV‐associated hsa‐miRNA‐126‐3p in early pregnancy stages in normal and pregnancies with GD according to low or high pancreatic β‐cell functionality (HOMA‐%β).

Methods: We collected serum from 81 pregnant women before 13 weeks of gestation; 19 women with GD (preDG+), and 62 women with a healthy pregnancy (preDG‐). Later, women were subdivided according to low HOMA‐%β or high HOMA‐%β. Exosomes were isolated from maternal serum obtained at early gestation using the exoRNeasy Midi Kit. Immunoblotting was used to assess the presence of exosome‐associated proteins (CD63, PLAP & Alix). RT‐PCR evaluated the EV‐associated miRNA hsa‐miRNA‐126‐3p.

Results: According to the diagnosis of GD vs NGT, there was no difference in CD63‐positive and PLAP‐positive exosomes. Interestingly, when evaluating exosome abundance according to diagnosis and β‐cell functionality, we found that the preDG+ low HOMA‐%β group had the highest abundance of CD63‐positive and PLAP‐positive exosomes (∼2‐fold and ∼3‐fold greater, respectively) vs preDG‐ high HOMA‐%β. Findings on exosome‐associated hsa‐miR‐126‐3p expression showed that the preDG+ high HOMA‐%β group had the highest expression of this miRNA (∼8‐fold greater) vs preDG‐ high HOMA‐%β) while still maintaining an adequate insulin‐sensitivity HOMA‐IR index among all the study groups assessed.

Summary/Conclusion: These findings suggest that either in normal or pregnancies that develop GD, exosome abundance variations and a miRNA targeted to regulate IRS on insulin‐sensitive tissues depend on the pancreatic β‐cell functionality status.

Keywords: gestational diabetes, exosome, early pregnancy, miRNA

## Mesenchymal stem cell‐derived extracellular vesicles for corneal epithelial repair
Vasudeva Tati1; Sreya Mitra1; Prabhudatta Das1; Sayan Basu2; Sachin Shukla1

1Sudhakar and Sreekanth Ravi Stem Cell Biology Laboratory, Centre for Ocular Regeneration, Prof. Brien Holden Eye Research Centre, L V Prasad Eye Institute, Hyderabad‐500034, India, Hyderabad, India; 2Sudhakar and Sreekanth Ravi Stem Cell Biology Laboratory, Centre for Ocular Regeneration, Prof. Brien Holden Eye Research Centre, The Cornea Institute, L V Prasad Eye Institute, Hyderabad‐500034, India, Hyderabad, India

Introduction: Corneal injuries are the leading cause of blindness. While mesenchymal stem cells (MSCs) have been shown to promote tissue repair at the ocular surface, the role of MSC‐derived extracellular vesicles (EVs) in corneal epithelial repair is largely unknown.

Methods: This study was approved by the institutional ethical committee (LEC‐BHR‐P12‐20‐559 and LVPEI‐IC‐SCR‐05‐21‐007). Human bone marrow‐derived MSCs (BM‐MSCs, Lonza Bioscience, #PT‐2501) and corneal epithelial cells (HCECs, ATCC, #HCE‐2 [50.B1] CRL‐11135) were cultured, characterized, and maintained in vitro as per the manufacturer's instructions. EVs were isolated from cultured BM‐MSCs using Total Exosome Isolation Reagent (#4478359, Invitrogen) and differential ultracentrifugation. Briefly, at 70% confluency, MSCs were starved in serum free media for 24 hours. Thereafter, culture medium was harvested and centrifuged in following steps: 300xg for 10 minutes, 2000xg for 20 minutes, 10000xg for 40 minutes, and 100000xg for 120 minutes using SORVALL MX 150+ Micro‐Ultracentrifuge (Thermo Scientific). All centrifugation steps were carried out at 40C, and after each step supernatant was transferred in a fresh tube and centrifuged further. Finally, the pellet was resuspended in PBS (filter‐sterilized). The EVs, thus isolated, were quantified (using EXOCET, #EXOCET‐96A, System Biosciences), characterized (as per MISEV 2018 guidelines using western blotting: CD9, #10626D; CD63, #10628D; CD81, #MA5‐13548, Calnexin, #MA3‐027; electron microscopy, etc.), and used in in vitro wound healing assay with HCECs. In EV‐based experiments, cells were cultured in basal medium supplemented with 10% exosome‐depleted FBS. Non‐EV‐treated HCECs were used as control.

Results: The BM‐MSCs and HCECs were characterized as CD90+CD105+CD45 CD34‐ and CK3/12+, respectively. The EVs were CD63+ and Calnexin‐. The scratched HCECs treated with BM‐MSC‐EVs (1x107) showed faster wound closure, relative to control.

Summary/Conclusion: BM‐MSC‐EVs may facilitate repair of the injured corneal epithelium in vitro.

Funding: Science and Engineering Research Board (SRG/2020/000842) and Department of Biotechnology (BT/PR32404/MED/30/2136/2019), Govt. of India, Hyderabad Eye Research Foundation

Keywords: Mesenchymal Stem Cells, Bone Marrow, Corneal Injury, Tissue Repair, Extracellular Vesicles, Secretome

PS10.06.

## DASH diet mechanism dissected by urine exosomes
Dana Bielopolski1; Luca Musante2; Henrik Molina3; Douglas Barrows3; Tom Carrol3; Jonathan N. Tobin4; Rhonda Kost3; Uta Erdbruegger2

1Rockefeller University, Tel Aviv, Israel; 2University of Virginia, Charlotessvile, USA; 3Rockefeller University, New York City, USA; 4Clinical Director Network, New York City, USA

Introduction: The DASH (Dietary Approach to Stop Hypertension) diet is a proven intervention in lowering blood pressure, yet it's mechanism has never been fully elucidated. We sought to characterize changes in urine exosome protein abundance pattern during nutritional changes, shifting from Western style diet to DASH diet.

Methods: The study was approved by the Rockefeller University IRB and informed consent was obtained prior to any research procedure. 9 hypertensive volunteers were admitted for a 2‐week period and transitioned from American style diet to DASH diet. Spot urine samples were collected on days 0, 5 and 11, spun down after addition of protease inhibitor, and frozen in (‐80). Urinary EVs were enriched by differential centrifugation to generate a low centrifugation pellet (20,000G, P20) and from the supernatant, a high (100,000G, P100) centrifugation pellet. Uromodulin was removed from both pellets by TCEP as validated by western blotting of TSG101. The P100 pellet was resolubilized in 6M guanidine in acidic condition and applied to a size exclusion chromatography (70nm). Both pellets were combined for mass spectrometry. Proteins were precipitated and re‐dissolved in 50uL 8M Urea/50mM ABC/20mM DTT followed by alkylation. Proteins were digested with LysC at less than 4M Urea and then diluted to less than 2M Urea for trypsinization. Digestions were halted by adding neat TFA and peptides were subjected to Solid Phase Extraction. Data were acquired on mass spectrometer coupled to a 12cm packed‐in‐emitter column and searched against a Human database. Statistical analyses were performed using R. An ANOVA test was used to identify all proteins changing across the time course (p < 0.05) and their levels were visualized in a heatmap. Hierarchical clustering of the genes from the ANOVA analysis, combined with pairwise comparisons of individual time points, were used to identify proteins throughout the time course.

Results: 1800 proteins were detected in the combined P20 and P100 urinary EV pellet. 292 of them were clustered into 5 different expression patterns: 38 in cluster 1, 24 in cluster 2, 87 in cluster 3, 61 proteins in cluster 4, and 82 proteins in cluster 5. Two proteins’ expression patterns coincided with our model: AQP2 (aquaporine 2) expression decreased from day 0 to day 5 and day 11. SLC12A3 (sodium chloride symporter; NCC) expression increased from day 0 to day 5 and day 11.

Summary/Conclusion: An increase in urinary EV's content of NCC and decrease in AQP2 might provide a mechanistic explanation to clinical changes of increased urine volume and blood pressure reduction following DASH diet implmentation.

Funding: These projects were supported in part by the National Center for Advancing Translational Sciences, National Institutes of Health, through Rockefeller University, Grant # KL2TR001865.
This work was supported by the Young Investigator Grant of the National Kidney Foundation

Keywords: DASH; uEV's, hypertension, NCC, AQP2

PS10.07.

## Effects of Montmorency tart cherry (L. Prunus cerasus) juice on inflammatory responses in human cells subjected to hyperglycaemia and hyperlipidaemia
Charlotte Lawson1; Soumya Sharma1; Natasha Ostrowski1; Terun Desai2; Lindsay Bottoms2

1Royal Veterinary College, London, United Kingdom; 2University of Hertfordshire, Hatfield, United Kingdom

Introduction: There is a global obesity epidemic, leading to increases in insulin resistance, metabolic syndrome and type two diabetes (T2D), which are associated with more than 50% greater risk of cardiovascular diseases (CVD), due to chronic systemic inflammation within the vasculature and other tissue. We have previously cultured human monocytes and endothelial cells in the presence of high glucose (10‐25mM) and palmitic acid (500nM) and shown increase in pro‐inflammatory CCL‐2 chemokine production, reactive oxygen species (ROS) production and extracellular vesicle (EV) release.
Montmorency tart cherry juice (MTCJ) provides high levels of beneficial polyphenols and supplementation has been shown to improve exercise parameters in healthy participants and in patients with metabolic syndrome.

Methods: Healthy volunteers were given MTCJ prior to exercise as published previously. Serum was collected pre and post exercise. EV were phenotyped by flow cytometry using fluorescently labelled antibodies to CD31, CD61, CD45, CD235 and Annexin V together with sizing beads and enumeration beads and confirmed by NTA. THP‐1 monocytes were treated with 18mM glucose, glucose oxidase with or without MTCJ (Cherry Active) for 24h. For ROS analysis, cells were pre‐loaded with dihydrorhodamine‐1,2,3. CCL‐2 was measured by ELISA, EV were analysed by flow cytometry.

Results: MTCJ reduced post‐exercise release of EV from platelets in vivo, reduced THP‐1 EV release, pro‐inflammatory chemokine production and reactive oxygen species production in vitro. Furthermore, the phenolic compounds vanillic acid, protocatechuic acid and chlorogenic acid, which have been identified in plasma after supplementation with MTCJ, modulated EV release, ROS production and CCL‐2 release in THP‐1.

Summary/Conclusion: MTCJ supplementation may be a useful dietary supplement for non‐pharmacologic intervention for patients at risk of obesity and associated chronic inflammatory syndromes.

Funding: Society for Endocrinology

Keywords: cardiovascular; inflammation
