## Investigating the role of extracellular vesicles in fibrosis with an in vitro model of Peyronie's disease
Marcus M. Ilg1; David J. Ralph2; Selim Cellek1

1Anglia Ruskin University, Chelmsford, United Kingdom; 2University College London Hospital, London, United Kingdom

Introduction: Peyronie's disease (PD) is a fibrotic condition characterized by plaque formation in the penile tunica albuginea (TA), driven by persistent myofibroblast presence. The role of extracellular vesicles (EVs) has not been studied in PD.

Methods: Human primary fibroblasts were isolated from TA of PD patients. Conditioned media was generated from cells with and without Transforming Growth Factor‐ß1 (TGF‐ß1) to induce myofibroblast transformation. EVs were isolated using size‐exclusion chromatography columns after concentrating media via centrifugal filters. EVs were analyzed via Tunable Resistive Pulse Sensing (TRPS). Fibroblasts were treated with various concentrations of EVs in presence/absence of TGF‐ß1 and myofibroblast transformation was quantified measuring myofibroblast marker α‐SMA using In‐Cell ELISA (ICE).

Results: TRPS analysis revealed that the TGF‐ß1‐treated cells produced more EVs (5.4x109 particles/mL) than untreated cells (9.2x108 particles/mL). Mean diameter was 92nm and 71nm for vesicles of untreated and TGF‐ß1‐treated cells, respectively. TGF‐ß1‐treated cells produced particles with increased blockage duration at various particle diameters, indicating different shape. Interestingly, EVs from TGF‐ß1‐treated cells exerted a concentration‐dependent anti‐fibrotic effect, preventing TGF‐ß1‐induced myofibroblast transformation, whilst fibroblast EVs showed no effect. No anti‐myofibroblast effect was observed for co‐isolated soluble protein fractions. The effect of EVs could be abrogated when cells were treated with 10μM of dynasore to inhibit vesicle uptake.

Summary/Conclusion: This is the first study to characterize the EVs derived from fibroblasts and myofibroblasts in PD and to demonstrate an anti‐myofibroblast effect of myofibroblast‐derived EVs. Further work is needed to identify the contents of the vesicles to see what drives the effect on fibroblast/myofibroblast biology.

Funding: European Society for Sexual Medicine (RG19‐04)

Keywords: Fibrosis, Peyronie's disease, Myofibroblast, Fibroblast

PS10.10.

## Unraveling the role of extracellular vesicles in Alzheimer's disease: from disease progression to neuroprotection
Andreu Matamoros‐Angles1; Mohsin Shafiq1; Emina Karadjuzovic1; Behnam Mohammadi2; Feizhi Song1; Santra Brenna3; Berta Puig4; Hermann Altmeppen1; Michaela Schweizer5; Friederike Zunke6; Markus Glatzel1

1Institute of Neuropathology, University Medical Center Hamburg‐Eppendorf, Hamburg, Germany, Hamburg, Germany; 2Institute of Neuropathology, University Medical Center Hamburg‐Eppendorf, Hamburg, Germany; 3Department of Neurology, Experimental Research in Stroke and Inflammation (ERSI), University Medical Center Hamburg‐Eppendorf, Hamburg, Germany., HAMBURG, Germany; 4Department of Neurology, Experimental Research in Stroke and Inflammation (ERSI), University Medical Center Hamburg‐Eppendorf, Hamburg, Germany., Hamburg, Germany; 5Core Facility of Electron Microscopy, Center for Molecular Neurobiology ZMNH, University Medical Center Hamburg‐Eppendorf, Hamburg, Germany, Hamburg, Germany; 6Department of Molecular Neurology, University Hospital Erlangen, Friedrich‐Alexander University Erlangen‐Nürnberg (FAU), 91054 Erlangen, Germany., Erlangen, Germany

Introduction: Alzheimer's disease (AD) is the leading cause of dementia worldwide. The trigger is still unknown, and available symptomatic treatments show modest efficacy at best. AD is characterized by a slow yet progressive cognitive decline affecting brain regions related to memory, personality, and daily activity performances. In AD brains, there is a progression of amyloid‐β (Aβ) aggregation in extracellular plaques and the formation of intracellular neurofibrillary tangles composed of phosphorylated Tau protein, suggesting that this propagation participates in the pathology. Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have been related to crucial brain functions, such as myelin maintenance and neurotransmission. However, the functionality and regulation of brain‐EVs are not fully understood. Recently, EVs emerged as relevant in AD and were described as biomarkers in AD patients' serum. On the one hand, EVs trap Aβ, which reduces Aβ free oligomers load and Aβ‐mediated toxicity, and participate in prosurvival signaling, thereby exerting neuroprotective functions. Conversely, EVs enhance the propagation of Aβ and Tau aggregates, thus promoting the disease progression, highlighting a seemingly dual role of these entities in AD.
Here we will describe in detail the AD brain‐derived EVs’ cargo to characterize the main protein actors in the aforementioned dual role of EVs.

Methods: EVs are isolated from AD patients’ brains by differential ultracentrifugation and iodixanol‐based density gradient. Nanoparticle tracking analysis, western blotting, and transmission electron microscopy are used to characterize them. Their RNA and protein profiles will be analyzed by transcriptomic and proteomic work‐ups. Lastly, using hypothesis‐driven experiments in human iPS cell models, we will study the role of differentially expressed genes/proteins in brain‐derived EVs biology in AD's pathophysiology.

Results: Our optimized EVs’ isolation protocol shows two subpopulations of EVs with a similar average mean size (∼170‐200 nm). The samples express the EV markers CD81, CD9, and Flotilin‐1 and AD‐relevant proteins, such as ADAM10 and the prion protein. The transmission electron microscopy reveals the expected cup shape of the purified particles. Omics analyses are currently being conducted, and the human iPSC‐derived neuronal model is established.

Summary/Conclusion: Our optimized EVs’ isolation protocol drastically reduces the generation of proteolytic artifacts. These EVs will be useful to accomplish our primary goal: to achieve better mechanistic insight into EVs’ function in AD, especially into their neuroprotective role.

Funding: This project has received funding from the European Union's Horizon 2020 research and innovation program under the Marie Sklodowska‐Curie grant agreement N°101030402 and from the PIER initiative Hamburg (Project PIF 2020‐10).

PS10.11.

## Angiostatic effects of autologous and heterologous extracellular vesicles on coronary endothelium
Anca D. Dobrian1; Nigeste Carter2; Allison M. Mathiesen1; Noel Miller1; MIchael Brown1; Ruben Colunga Biancatelli3; John Catravas4

1Eastern Virginia Medical School, Norfolk, USA; 2Virginia Commonwealth University, Richmond, USA; 3Old Dominion University, Norfolk, USA; 4Old Dominion UNiversity, Norfolk, USA

Introduction: We previously demonstrated that EVs released by endothelial cells (EC) of adipose tissue are angiostatic. However, it is not clear whether this is specific to adipose cells or is a general feature of EC‐EVs. Here, we compared the effects of EVs produced by human coronary artery endothelial cells (HCAEC, autologous) and adipose microvasculature (heterologous) on the endothelium, under basal and inflammatory conditions.

Methods: HCAEC and adipose ECs were treated for 6 days with a combination of pro‐inflammatory cytokines (PIC) TNFa, IFNg, and TGFb. EVs were isolated by ultracentrifugation, characterized by NTA and EM, and tetraspanin expression determined by ExoView R100. EV internalization was measured by imaging flow cytometry. HCAEC were then treated with EVs for 24 hours and in vitro tube formation and proliferation quantified. Barrier function and wounding were assessed using ECIS 1600R. Nanostring miRNA panel was used to identify miRNA cargo.

Results: All EV subtypes had similar morphology and size distribution. CD63 expression increased in autologous and heterologous EVs from PIC‐stimulated cells. EC showed reduced relative mesh formation in response to each EV subtype, compared to control cells. ECs displayed a significant reduction in proliferation, in response to each EV subtype. Autologous EVs isolated from PIC treated cells showed to damage HCAEC barrier function, while the same EVs isolated in basal conditions participated in wound recovery. We identified 8 miRNAs amongst all EV subtypes. IPA analysis identified target genes involved in VEGF signaling, cell cycle and extracellular matrix formation.

Summary/Conclusion: EVs from control‐ and PIC‐treated HCAEC reduced angiogenesis and proliferation possibly by targeting of genes via their miRNA cargo. EVs may exert an angiostatic effect during post‐ischemic myocardial events by competing with the pro‐angiogenic cues and by disrupting repair mechanisms.

Funding: American hear Association, NIH/NHLBI

## Proteomic analysis of extracellular vesicles derived from high‐grade serous ovarian cancer cells
Laura Lehtinen1; Milla Hollmén2; Katja Kaipio2; Johanna Hynninen3; Seija Grenman3; Olli Carpén4

1Cancer Research Unit, Institute of Biomedicine and FICAN West Cancer Centre, University of Turku, Turku, Finland, Paimio, Finland; 2Cancer Research Unit, Institute of Biomedicine and FICAN West Cancer Centre, University of Turku, Turku, Finland, Turku, Finland; 3Department of Obstetrics and Gynecology, University of Turku and Turku University Hospital, Turku, Finland, Turku, Finland; 4Department of Pathology, University of Helsinki and HUSLAB, Helsinki University Hospital, Helsinki, Finland, Helsinki, Finland

Introduction: High‐grade serous ovarian cancer (HGSOC) is a prime example of a disease, in which increased molecular knowledge has not yet translated to outcome improvement. The discussion between tumor cells and surrounding stroma is a critically important feature in disease progression in HGSOC, as most patients are diagnosed with extensively disseminated disease. Extracellular vesicles (EV) provide intercellular communication between tumor and stromal cells via several types of bioactive molecules, and may provide additional information on HGSOC biology.

Methods: Conditioned cell culture medium was collected from three commonly available HGSOC cell lines and three patient derived primary cell cultures (PD‐HGSOC). EVs were isolated from conditioned culture media with size‐exclusion chromatography and characterized for particle size and concentration with TRPS measurements. The EVs were visualized with transmission electron microscopy. The presence of known EV markers was studied with ELISA assays and western blotting. The protein composition of the individual EV samples was explored using LC‐ESI‐MS/MS. FunRich analysis tool was used to further explore the differences of EV protein compositions between commonly available and PD‐HGSOC cells.

Results: The characterization was conducted following MISEV2018 guidelines and confirmed successful isolation of highly pure EV samples. The identified proteins showed sample profiles typical for EVs. Preliminary analysis of proteomics data showed that HGSOC derived EVs contain various types of functionally important proteins. The protein composition differed between individual samples as well as between commonly available and PD HGSOC cells.

Summary/Conclusion: This study indicates interesting differences in EV protein composition between HGSOC cell lines and primary cell cultures. Comprehensive analysis of the proteomics data as well as in vitro validation assays continue.

Funding: The study was funded by The Academy of Finland.

Keywords: High‐grade serous ovarian cancer, patient derived cells, proteomics

## Circulating small EV‐related alterations in early stage of atherosclerosis
Krisztina Pálóczi1; Bernadett György2; András Försönits3; Júlia Opra4; Boldizsár Csernai3; Hargita Hegyesi5; András Gézsi6; Ádám Domonkos Tárnoki7; Dávid László Tárnoki7; Helga Szabó7; Dóra Melicher8; Edit I. Buzás3

1Semmelweis University Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Budapest, XVIII. kerület, Hungary; 2University of Physical Education‐Research Center of Molecular Exercise Science, Budapest, Hungary; 3Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Semmelweis University, Budapest, Hungary; 4Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Semmelweis University, Budapest, Hungary; 5Semmelweis University Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Budapest, Hungary; 6Department of Measurement and Information Systems, Budapest University of Technology and Economics, Budapest, Hungary; 7Medical Imaging Center, Semmelweis University, Budapest, Hungary; 8Department of Emergency Medicine, Semmelweis University, Budapest, Hungary

Introduction: Atherosclerosis is a disease with complex genetic background. Multiple environmental and genetic factors are responsible for its development. Here we focused on circulating extracellular vesicles (EVs) and studied their changes in atherosclerosis.

Methods: Atherosclerosis was assessed by carotid and femoral artery ultrasonography. Blood samples were collected from healthy controls (n=21), from patients with early‐stage atherosclerosis (=15) and from individuals with more advanced atherosclerosis (n=18). ACD anticoagulated blood samples were used to prepare platelet free plasma. The PFP samples were subjected to 18,000g centrifugation to remove the medium sized EVs. Then the supernatant was filtered by gravity through a 0.22 micron filter, and was concentrated by ultrafiltration (100kDa cut‐off). The filtrate was further purified by SEC (qEV‐70), and the EVs were analyzed by Western blotting, NTA, ELISA and by flow cytometry (MACSPlex Exosome Kit). We also measured the blood plasma Lp(a), MMP12 and MCP1 levels, and analyzed the ApoE polymorphism.

Results: We found that MMP12 concentrations were significantly elevated both in the PFP samples and in the small EV preparations in the advanced atherosclerosis group as compared to healthy subjects. Importantly, we also found a significant elevation of MMP12 in small EVs of early‐stage atherosclerotic patients, while there was no elevation in the PFP samples of the same patients at this early stage of the disease. We also detected early‐stage atherosclerosis‐related increase in the number of CD14+ and CD142+ sEVs, while the CD146+ sEVs were significantly elevated only in the advanced atherosclerotic group.

Summary/Conclusion: Our data suggest that by analyzing circulating small EVs, certain atherosclerosis‐related alterations can be detected at an earlier stage of the disease than by the analysis of blood plasma samples.

Funding: This work was supported by National Research, Development and Innovation Office NKFIH, Hungary, NVKP_16‐1‐2016‐0017, the Hungarian Scientific Research Fund (OTKA K120237), VEKOP‐2.3.2‐16‐2016‐000002, VEKOP‐2.3.3‐15‐2017‐00016, H2020‐MSCA‐ITN‐2017‐722148 TRAIN EV and Higher Education Excellence Program (FIKP) Therapeutic Thematic Programme, Hungarian Thematic Excellence Programme No TKP2020‐NKA‐26, EU H2020 grant agreement No. 739593.
We would like to thank the ÚNKP‐20‐5 New National Excellence Program of the Ministry for Innovation and Technology, from the source of the National Research, Development and Innovation Fund as well as the Bólyai János research scholarship supported by the Hungarian Academy of Sciences."

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, atherosclerosis, MACSPlex Exosome

PS11.03.

## Extracellular RNA (exRNA) as a biomarker for neurodegenerative diseases
Elizabeth Hutchins1; Cherae Bilagody2; Rebecca Reiman2; Jerry Antone2; Amber Logemann2; Laura Cox2; Bessie Meechoovet2; Dorothy Marie Palomares2; Farah Bitza‐Ray2; Kendall Jensen1

1Neurogenomics Division, Translational Genomics Research Institute, Phoenix, USA; 2Translational Genomics Research Institute, Phoenix, USA

Introduction: The study and treatment of neurodegenerative diseases has been hindered by the lack of biomarkers to monitor disease development and progression. In this study we focus on using extracellular RNA (exRNA) as a biomarker for Alzheimer's disease (AD) and Parkinson's disease (PD). ExRNAs are carried by extracellular vesicles and can be found in all biofluids. Biofluids such as blood and urine are non‐invasive, cost effective, and can easily be collected for multiple time points. To ascertain exRNA as a viable biomarker we plan to establish a profile of exRNAs associated with disease and to confirm that it is reliable over time.

Methods: Plasma and urine samples were collected from individuals with Alzheimer's disease (AD) or Parkinson's disease (PD), and healthy individuals (control) at two different time points. Collection was performed with Informed patient consent through a contracted vendor under an approved IRB protocol and in accordance with Declaration of Helsinki. Libraries were prepped from vesicular RNA and sequenced using Illumina NovaSeq6000 platform. Post hoc analysis included correlation studies between visits in addition to differential expression.

Results: For plasma and urine we detected >15,000 genes and > 60,000 transcripts. Kidney specific genes have a higher correlation in urine, and brain specific genes have a higher correlation in plasma. Urine shows a higher percentage of protein coding and long non‐coding RNA, while plasma has a higher percentage of non‐coding RNA. Plasma exhibits more uniquely mapped genomes in comparison to urine.

Summary/Conclusion: Urine and plasma are viable sources of biofluids for biomarkers of CNS diseases based on the abundance of CNS specific genes. Plasma samples were found to be more reproducible and consistent than urine, but urine has the potential to be reproducible by removing variable genes and had greater diversity. Additionally, we identified candidate genes for disease based on differential expression.

PS11.04.

## Identification of cardiomyocyte‐derived circulating extracellular vesicle in the bloodstream
Hargita Hegyesi1; Eva Pallinger1; Annamária Huszár2; Balazs Hornyak1; Csenger Kovácsházi3; Gabor B Brenner4; Zoltán Giricz5; Krisztina Paloczi1; Delaram Khamari1; Péter Ferdinandy5; Edit I. Buzás1

1Semmelweis University Department of Genetics, Cell‐ and Immunobiology, Budapest, Hungary; 2Department of Anatomy, Cell and Developmental Biology, Eotvos Lorand University, Budapest, Hungary; 3Semmelweis University, Department of Pharmacology and Pharmacotherapy, Budapest, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary; 4Semmelweis University Department of Pharmacology, Budapest, Hungary; 5Semmelweis University, Department of Pharmacology and Pharmacotherapy, Budapest, Hungary; Pharmahungary ltd., Szeged, Hungary, Budapest, Hungary

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) play a role in regulating the physiological and pathological functions of cardiomyocytes and it gives the potential to monitor cardiovascular diseases. The release of EVs is increased under cellular stress and cardiomyocyte damaging conditions. However, whether cardiomyocyte‐derived EVs eventually reach the systemic circulation and whether their number in the bloodstream reflects cardiac injury, remains unknown. Conditional transgenic mice (MerCreMer/mTmG) expressing green fluorescent protein (GFP) by cardiomyocytes were studied in lipopolysaccharide (LPS)‐induced systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS). We aimed to detect cardiomyocyte‐derived EVs in the blood.

Methods: EVs were separated both from platelet‐free plasma and from the conditioned medium of isolated cardiomyocytes of the left ventricular wall. Size distribution and concentration of the released particles were determined by Nanoparticle Tracking Analysis. The presence of GFP + cardiomyocyte‐derived circulating EVs was monitored by flow cytometry and cardiac function was assessed by echocardiography.

Results: In LPS‐treated mice, systemic inflammation and the consequent cardiomyopathy were verified by elevated plasma levels of TNFα, GDF‐15, and cardiac troponin I, and by a decrease in the ejection fraction. Furthermore, we demonstrated elevated levels of circulating small‐ and medium‐sized EVs in the LPS‐injected mice. Importantly, we detected GFP+ cardiomyocyte‐derived EVs in the circulation of control mice, and the number of these circulating GFP+ vesicles increased significantly upon intraperitoneal LPS administration. The cardiomyocyte‐derived GFP+ EVs were also positive for intravesicular troponin I (cTnI) and muscle‐associated glycogen phosphorylase (PYGM).

Summary/Conclusion: These data provide direct evidence that cardiomyocyte‐derived EVs are present in the circulation and that the increased number of cardiac‐derived EVs in the blood reflects cardiac injury in LPS‐induced systemic inflammation.

Funding: NVKP_16‐1‐2016‐0017,National Laboratories Excellence No NLP‐17

Keywords: Extracellular vesicles, Cardiomyocyte, Cardiomyopathy, Inducible transgenic mice, SIRS

PS11.05.

## Fast Isolation and Analysis of Functional Tumor‐related Exosomes (TREXs) from Head and Neck Squamous Cell Carcinoma (HNSCC) Plasma by Galectin‐based Glycan Recognition Particles
Dapi Menglin Chiang1; Laura Benecke2; Laurent Muller2; Michael W. Pfaffl1

1Chair of Animal Physiology & Immunology, Freising, Germany; 2Department of Otorhinolaryngology, Head and Neck Surgery, Basel, Switzerland

Introduction: Head and Neck Squamous Cell Carcinoma (HNSCC) is the eighth common cancer worldwide in 2021. Treatment failures are the main reason for the moderate overall survival, even though novel diagnostic methods and therapies have entered the clinics. Thus, better diagnostic methods are urgently needed for HNSCC follow‐up. Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have been recently studied as potential new targets in liquid biopsy for a successful and reliable cancer diagnosis. But, traditional high‐purity exosomes isolation method is a time‐consuming process, often with high degree of contaminating protein aggregates.

Methods: In this work, we present an EV isolation method for ‘pure’ and functional tumor‐related exosomes (TREXs) by galectin‐based glycan recognition particles, EXÖBead®. Bead‐based flow cytometry (BFC) results showed high abundant EV markers such as CD9, CD63, CD81 and TSG101. Intact eluted exosomes were observed by transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and Cryo‐EM after lactose buffer elution.

Results: A low lipoprotein contamination was demonstrated by measuring Apolipoprotein A1 which compared to size exclusion chromatography isolation. Classical PanEV markers (premixed CD9, CD63, and CD81 antibodies), common leukocyte antigen (CD45) and HNSCC biomarkers (PD‐L1, EpCAM and PanCK) were measured as TREXs biomarkers by BFC. CD45Neg PanEV+, EpCAM+ PanEV+, PD‐L1+ PanEV+, CD45Neg PanEV+ with triple HNSCC markers positive and CD45+ PanEV+ with double HNSCC markers positive were significantly higher in tumor patients (n=9) compared with healthy controls (n=9) and present an EV related circulating biomarker in HNSCC patients. Furthermore, HNSCC plasma eluted EVs by EXÖBead® isolation showed significantly higher immune suppression ability compared to Polyethylene glycol isolation (PEG; ExoQuick®). In peripheral blood mononuclear cells functional assay, PD1+ CD69neg and PD‐L1+ CD69neg T cells were also significantly increased from treatment with different patients’ plasma EVs (n=13) compared to elution buffer only controls (n=3).

Summary/Conclusion: EXÖBead® isolation revealed a fast and reliable method with low lipoprotein contamination and remained TREXs function from patient's plasma. Therefore, EXÖBead® showed great potential as a new EVs isolation tool with great diagnostic potentials in cancer diagnostics and further clinical settings.

Funding: This work was supported by the “pro patient” grant (pp18‐08) and Krebsliga 462 beider Basel (# 18‐463 2016).

Keywords: HNSCC, tumor‐derived EVs, low contamination, novel isolation technique, biomarker, galectin‐based glycan recognition particles, beads‐based flow cytometry

PS11.06.

## Computational deconvolution of plasma EV RNA by cell of origin improves detection of RNAs of interest
Robert Fullem1; Emily Laplante2; Aleksandar Milosavljevic1

1Baylor College of Medicine, Houston, USA; 2BCM, Houston, USA

Introduction: Extracellular RNA (exRNA) that is contained in extracellular vesicles (EVs) is a mixture of RNA originating from a number of cell types of origin. This creates challenges where informative EV RNA biomarkers of disease can be masked by more numerous EVs from non‐disease related cell types. The deconvolution of plasma EV RNA by cell type of origin can address this challenge by identifying RNA contributed by EVs originating from proximal cell types, and thus improving detectability of RNAs originating from a distal tissue of interest.

Methods: We performed RNA‐Seq of EVs isolated from Red Blood Cells, Platelets, and Neutrophils. Utilizing the XDEC tool, these reference samples were used to deconvolute a study of pre and post exercise plasma EV exRNA by cell type of origin. Additionally, these reference samples were used with publicly available eCLIP data to predict the presence of RNA‐Binding Protein (RBP) EVs through permutation testing of RBP associated exRNAs. Downstream analysis predicted target genes of miRNAs of interest that were not identified through non‐deconvolution differentiation analysis.

Results: 22 RNAs were found to be significantly upregulated in a post exercise associated deconvoluted EV profile. This included novel RNAs not identified when analyzing the bulk plasma exRNA samples. Downstream analysis of the gene targets of the 16 differentially expressed miRNAs identified exercise related processes as the top scoring gene ontology results. Additionally, permutation testing for RBP presence in cell types of origin EVs allowed prediction of which RBPs occupied cell type specific EVs under pre and post exercise conditions.

Summary/Conclusion: Using limited reference data, our analysis revealed novel characteristics of EV biology in pre and post exercise conditions through deconvolution and imputation testing. With further reference data of additional proximal cell types of origin, we can leverage these methods to further interrogate exRNA studies of interest.

PS11.07.

## Exploring the role of Rab3 in exosome release from prostate cancer cells
Silvana Romero1; Krizia Sagini2; Kirsten Sandvig2; Alicia Llorente3

1Dept. of Molecular Cell Biology, Institute for Cancer Research, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway; 2Institute for Cancer Research, Oslo University Hospital, Oslo, Norway; 3Oslo University Hospital Oslo, Department of Molecular Cell Biology, Institute for Cancer Research, oslo, Norway

Introduction: Exosomes are extracellular vesicles released by cells upon fusion of multivesicular bodies with the plasma membrane. However, the mechanisms underlying the secretion of exosomes are still unclear. Rab proteins play important roles in vesicular membrane trafficking along the endocytic and the secretory pathway. In particular, Rab3 has been involved in cellular secretion, and specifically Rab3D and Rab3B have been found to be enriched in urinary exosomes from prostate cancer patients compared to healthy males. In this study we investigated the role of Rab3 isoforms in exosome release in prostate cancer cells.

Methods: The role of Rab3A, Rab3B and Rab3D, in exosome release was investigated by siRNA depletion from the highly metastatic cancer cell line PC‐3. Knockdown efficiency was verified by western blot (WB) and specificity by qPCR. Small extracellular vesicles (sEVs) were then isolated by differential centrifugation from cell media and analyzed by nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) and WB. Moreover, confocal microscopy was used to investigate potential alteration of the endo‐lysosomal system in depleted cells. Alterations in other cellular pathways was investigated by following the trafficking of the toxin ricin.

Results: Our results indicate that depletion of Rab3D or Rab3B, but not Rab3A, in PC‐3 cells increase the release of sEVs enriched in exosomal markers such as Alix and Syntenin. Moreover, Rab3 depletion did not affect sEV size distribution, as shown by NTA. No significant changes in the transport of externally added ricin to the Golgi apparatus and cytosol were shown, suggesting that Rab3 is mainly associated with secretory processes.

Summary/Conclusion: This study reveals that Rab3D and RabB depletion in prostate cancer cells may play a role in exosome secretion, but further experiments are required to verify the endosomal origin of these sEVs.

Keywords: Exosomes, prostate cancer, Rab proteins.

PS11.08.

## Plasma extracellular vesicles: a promising tool for predicting ischemic stroke severity
Paola Monti1; Angelica Carandina2; Chiara Favero3; Mirjam Hoxha3; Nicola Montano2; Luca Ferrari4; Eleonora Tobaldini2; Valentina Bollati5

11) Department of Clinical Sciences and Community Health, University of Milan, Milan, Italy, Saronno, Italy; 21) Department of Clinical Sciences and Community Health, University of Milan, Milan, Italy 2) Department of Internal Medicine, Fondazione IRCCS Ca' Granda, Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Milan, Italy, Milan, Italy; 31) Department of Clinical Sciences and Community Health, University of Milan, Milan, Italy, Milan, Italy; 41) Department of Clinical Sciences and Community Health, University of Milan, Milan, Italy 2) Occupational Health Unit, Fondazione IRCCS Ca' Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Milan, Italy, Milan, Italy; 51) Department of Clinical Sciences and Community Health, University of Milan, Milan, Italy 2) Occupational Health Unit, Fondazione IRCCS Ca' Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Milan, Italy, Milano, Italy

Introduction: Ischemic stroke (IS) is a leading cause of death globally, with a huge burden in terms of mortality‐to‐incidence and disability. Non‐invasive tools for IS prognosis are currently lacking. Here, we focused on circulating EVs as potential predictive markers for acute and mid‐term IS outcomes.

Methods: After approval by local Ethics Committee, we enrolled 47 patients with acute IS, whose severity was evaluated at admission (T0) and after one week (Tw) using NIH stroke scale to assess neurological impairment, and after 3 months (Tm) using the mRs scale to assess functional disability. After giving informed consent, blood samples were collected at T0, at T1 i.e. soon after recanalization treatment or after 2 hours in non‐treated patients, and at Tw. Plasma EVs were enriched by ultracentrifugation. Total EV count and characterization of EV subpopulations (CD14+: monocytes/macrophages, CD61+: platelets, CD105+: total endothelial cells, CD25+: T‐cells, and CD62e+: activated endothelial cells) were obtained by NTA and flow cytometry, respectively. Multivariable regression models were applied to evaluate the relationship between IS outcomes and EVs.

Results: No association was found between EV count and early/long‐term outcomes. However, CD61+ EVs at T0 was positively associated with IS severity at T0 (∆%=0.18, p=0.002), and with severity of mid‐term outcome (∆%=0.30, p=0.016). CD25+ EVs at T0 were positively related to both early (∆%=3.93, p=0.009) and mid‐term IS outcomes (∆%=3.94, p=0.009). Finally, CD25+ EVs at T1 were positively related to mid‐term IS outcome (∆%=0.91, p=0.047).

Summary/Conclusion: The present study suggests that specific EV subtypes could be predictive of IS severity and both short‐ and long‐term outcomes. Our results provide new insights into the pathophysiological process of IS and propose EVs as a promising tool for better stratification of IS patients, for the choice of medical therapy, and for post‐recanalization treatment monitoring.

PS11.09.

## Extracellular Vesicles Expressing Tissue Factor, a Novel Biomarkers for Thrombotic Risk Assessment in Pancreatic Ductal Adenocarcinoma
Amineh Dadkhah1; Dimitar Tasev2; Moniek De Maat3; Kees Kluft4

1Good Biomarker Sciences (GBS) Leiden enrolled at the Department of Haematology, Erasmus University Medical Center, Leiden, Netherlands; 2Good Biomarker Sciences (GBS) Leiden, Leiden, Netherlands; 3Department of Haematology, Erasmus University Medical Center, Rotterdam, Netherlands; 4Good Biomarker Sciences (GBS Leiden), Leiden, Netherlands

Introduction: Cancer patients are at high risk for blood coagulation and venous thromboembolism (VTE) with a poor prognosis. The risk depends on different factors, including the type and stage of cancer, patient age and health background, and therapeutic methods used for cancer treatment. Pancreatic adenocarcinoma (PDAC) is a type of cancer with a high frequency of VTE incidents, but what makes PDAC such a thrombogenic disease remains unclear. Studies suggest that extracellular vesicles (EV) expressing tissue factor (TF) may play a crucial role in initiating thrombosis and causing VTE. Tissue factor (TF) is the primary initiator of blood coagulation and plays an essential role in haemostasis. Higher expression of this molecule in the pancreatic tissue can contribute to the local VTE in PDAC patients. Extracellular vesicles are a heterogeneous group of membranous lipid‐containing structures, including microvesicles and exosomes released from almost all types of cells under physiological conditions; however, pathological conditions, including cancer stimulate releasing a higher amount of EV in the tumour environment. These cancer‐derived particles contain procoagulant phospholipids, including phosphatidylserine. Circulating EV containing TF might be the key of VTE in far distant sites from primary tumours. This study set out to identify a novel biomarker for better risk assessment of thrombotic events in PDAC patients.

Methods: The pancreatic cancer cell line, BXPC‐3 was used as a cell model for a generation of EVs. Isolated EVs were characterized by nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) and different size‐fractions (1000, 450, 200 and 100nm) were isolated and assessed for TF by quantitative ELISA. A TF activity assay was used to determine the procoagulant activity of TF+ EVs.

Results: Our findings suggest that the EVs released from cancer cell lines under our experimental conditions express TF. The EVs are mostly in a range of 100‐400nm and smaller EVs are containing a higher concentration of TF.

Summary/Conclusion: Our study provides evidence that TF+‐EVs of a certain size are highly pro‐coagulant and might be used as biomarkers for thrombotic risk assessment in PDAC patients.

PS11.10.

## Can extracellular vesicles derived from different temozolomide‐treated glioblastoma multiforme cell lines be exploited as liquid biopsy?
Diana Vardanyan1; Stefano Tacconi2; Simone Dinarelli3; Maria Concetta Cufaro4; Elisa Panzarini5; Piero Del Boccio6; Luciana Dini2

1Department of Biological and Environmental Sciences and Technologies (Di.S.Te.B.A.), University of Salento,, Lecce, Italy; 2Department of Biology and Biotechnology "C. Darwin", Sapienza University of Rome, Rome, Italy; 3Institute for the Structure of Matter (ISM), National Research Council (CNR), Rome, Italy; 4Department of Innovative Technologies in Medicine and Dentistry, "G. d'Annunzio", University of Chieti‐Pescara, Chieti, Italy; 5Department of Biological and Environmental Sciences and Technologies, University of Salento, Lecce, Italy; 6Department of Pharmacy ,"G. d'Annunzio", University of Chieti‐Pescara, Chieti, Italy

Introduction: Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have been recently suggested as possible biomarkers of glioblastoma (GBM) multiforme, and thus used as "liquid biopsy" for diagnostic information, monitoring disease progression and response to clinical treatment. The overall purpose of the study is to define a specific molecular fingerprinting of EVs from four different GBM cell lines in presence or absence of temozolomide (TMZ).

Methods: EVs from four GBM cell lines (U87MG, T98G, U373MG, U251MG), in presence or in absence of 200μM temozolomide (TMZ) were isolated by ultrafiltration combined with size exclusion chromatography (SEC). The morpho‐quantitative analysis and size distribution of EV were conducted using high‐resolution microscopy (i.e., Atomic force microscopy, AFM, and scanning transmission electron microscopy, STEM). AFM was also used to determine the topographical distribution and size of budding vesicles directly on GBM cell surface. Further, the WB identification of EV‐associated markers and the proteomic analysis of isolated EVs will allow to determine the molecular profiles associated with each cell line in presence or absence of TMZ.

Results: The amount and the size of EVs produced under normal conditions varies between the four GBM cell lines and is further affected following treatment with TMZ. The cell surface topographical distribution of budding vesicles and their number was significantly different among the four GBM cells (U87MG>U251MG>U373MG>T98G) and, influenced by TMZ treatment. Specific proteins that can serve as biomarkers of the disease were identified by using proteomic analysis and discriminated the different cell lines in untreated and TMZ‐ treated conditions.

Summary/Conclusion: The combined ultrastructural and proteomic approaches are useful to categorize the four GBM cells‐derived EVs in the attempt to use them as non‐invasive diagnostic tools.

Funding: No external funding

Keywords: Temozolomide, Glioblastoma Multiforme, Atomic force microscopy, Scanning transmission electron microscopy, Ultrafiltration, Size‐exclusion chromatography, Proteomic analysis, molecular fingerprinting, budding vesicles

PS11.11.

## Effect of X‐ray irradiation on quantity and tetraspanin markers expression of extracellular vesicles (EVs) derived from peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs)
Zi Huai Chew; Christelle Chua

National University of Singapore, Singapore Nuclear Research and Safety Initiative, Singapore, Singapore

Introduction: EVs are involved in cell‐to‐cell communication after exposure to ionizing radiation (IR), although their exact functions remain to be fully elucidated. PBMCs are known to be particularly sensitive to IR, but it is only more recently that researchers have begun to characterize PBMC‐derived EVs 1. Previous studies have focused mainly on the impact on EV cargo after high doses of radiation typically applied for radiotherapy, and frequently at later time points of more than 24h2 .This study aims to fill in a gap by investigating if X‐ray irradiation at lower doses between 0 to 4 Gy would alter the basic characteristics of PBMC‐derived EVs after 24 hours in culture.

Methods: Whole blood or PBMCs collected from healthy donors were exposed to X‐ray irradiation and cultured in plasma for 24 hours. Plasma was collected and size exclusion chromatography was used to purify and isolate the EVs. Presence of EVs were confirmed by flow cytometry using a newly‐developed water‐soluble membrane dye (developed by the National University of Singapore, GRIP program). Once the presence of EVs were identified, interferometric imaging was used to quantify the difference in particle count and tetraspanin markers (CD9, CD63 and CD81) between irradiated and non‐irradiated samples.

Results: Interferometric imaging analysis revealed that the EVs averaged between 50‐70nm in size. Interestingly, the overall quantity of EVs does not appear to increase significantly, 24 hours after exposure. This is in contrast to expectations that the EV population always increases in response to IR, and suggests that at earlier time points this may not be the case. We also observed that CD9 is generally highly expressed and that the percentage of CD63 expression appears to increase with radiation exposure.

Summary/Conclusion: Our results suggest that exposure of PBMCs to ionizing radiation have differing extent of effects on the quantity of EVs and expression of various tetraspanin markers.

Funding: National Research Foundation (Singapore)

PS11.12.

## Identification of surface protein markers of human adult astrocyte extracellular vesicles

Keerthanaa B. Balasubramanian Shanthi
1; Ravindra Daddali1; Antti Kiviniemi2; Abhishek Sharma1; Ulrich Bergmann1; Seppo Vainio1; Genevieve Bart1

1University of Oulu, Oulu, Finland; 2University of Oulu, oulu, Finland

Introduction: In the frame of MindGap FET‐open project, our aim is to identify markers of successful mindfulness practice in cancer survivors, carried by brain‐derived EVs. To identify suitable surface protein for brain‐derived EV capture from blood we characterized EVs from adult human primary astrocytes in culture. Astrocytes are central nervous system restricted glial cells with a wide range of functions, evolving with brain activity. In the healthy brain they are involved in maintenance of blood brain barrier (BBB), regulation of synaptic function, and regulation of blood flow in the CNS. New discoveries also highlight their potential role in mood and other brain disorders. From our proteomics data, Calsyntenin1 (CLSTN1), a single pass transmembrane protein involved in synapse formation and synaptic plasticity was selected for further analysis.

Methods: Primary human adult astrocytes were grown to 85‐90% confluency, then switched to FBS free collection medium. EVs were prepared using serial low speed centrifugations, 0,8μm filtration and 2 hours 100k ultracentrifugation (EV‐TRACK ID EV220009). EV were characterized by NTA, Exoview, transmission electron microscopy and western blot, associated proteins were identified by mass spectrometry.
U87 glioma cell line was transduced with lentivirus encoding human CLSTN1 isoform 2 with C‐terminus EGFP tag, overlayed with rat tail collagen I and imaged by confocal microscopy.

Results: Mass spectrometry identified 194 proteins with high combined FDR, and GO term enrichment for: nervous system development, synapse and extracellular vesicles. CLSTN1 association with astrocyte EVs was confirmed by western blot.
3D confocal imaging of live CLSTN1‐EGFP U87 cells show they secrete fluorescent EV‐like particles.

Summary/Conclusion: Analysis of Astrocyte EVs and fluorescent EVs produced by CLSTN1‐EGFP U87 glioma cells show that CLSTN1 is a potential marker of brain‐derived EVs.

Funding: FET Open MindGap (ID: 829040) Horizon2020‐EU 1.2.

## PS12: Late‐Breaking Poster Session 8: Biomarkers II

## Chair: Saara Laitinen, PhD – Finnish Red Cross Blood Service

## Chair: Antonia Reale, MD – Monash University

PS12.01.

## Urinary EVs and SEC‐enrichment: a 150‐experience summary
Marta Clos Sansalvador1; Sergio G. Garcia2; Miriam Moron‐Font3; Marcella Franquesa3; Francesc E. Borràs3

11. IVECAT, REMAR‐IGTP Group & Nephrology Service, “Germans Trias i Pujol” Health Science Research Institute & University Hospital, Can Ruti Campus, Badalona, Spain 2. Department of Cell Biology, Physiology and Immunology, Universitat Autònoma de Barcelona, Bellaterra, Spain, Badalona, Spain; 21 IVECAT, REMAR‐IGTP Group & Nephrology Service, “Germans Trias i Pujol” Health Science Research Institute & University Hospital, Can Ruti Campus, Badalona, Spain, Badalona, Spain; 31. IVECAT, REMAR‐IGTP Group & Nephrology Service, “Germans Trias i Pujol” Health Science Research Institute & University Hospital, Can Ruti Campus, Badalona, Spain, Badalona, Spain

Introduction: Urinary extracellular vesicles (uEV) show great potential as a source of non‐invasive biomarkers for chronic kidney disease (CKD). Our lab was pioneer in using size exclusion chromatography (SEC) for uEV enrichment. Here we summarize the experience and main results of more than 150 SEC‐enriched uEV samples.

Methods: Urine samples from healthy controls (n=17), normo‐functional kidney transplanted patients (n=17) or chronic kidney disease (CKD) patients (n=98) had been frozen for several months/years at ‐80°C before enrichment. Thawed samples (20 mL each) were processed for uEV enrichment by SEC and screened for the EV markers CD9 and CD63 by bead‐based flow cytometry. Protein content was measured using either BCA or Nanodrop.

Results: While no differences were observed between the mean fluorescence intensity (MFI) of both CD9 and CD63 in all samples, analyzing the CD9/CD63 ratio in each individual sample clearly segregated two groups. Specifically, the CD9 MFI was increased in one group (G1, n= 69) and decreased in the other (G2, n=85), while CD63 MFI remained more stable among all samples. The reason for this difference is not clear, but it can be attributed neither to the protein content of the sample nor their pathological state. In fact, when comparing healthy controls and CKD patients, a significant reduction on the expression of both EV markers was observed in the pathological samples, maybe reflecting the pathology itself or the chronic treatment.
Technical reproducibility of the enrichment method has been tested in up to 34 urine samples. Each of these were isolated twice at different time points and using two independent home‐made SEC columns. The ratio MFI‐1/MFI‐2 (MFI from first isolation / MFI from second isolation) was 1.059 ± 0.29 and 1.037 ± 0.31 for CD9 and CD63 respectively, highlighting a great level of reproducibility.

Summary/Conclusion: Overall, our data clearly show that SEC is a robust and reproducible uEV isolation technic. In our experience, CD63 MFI seems to be more reliable for uEV labelling in normalization intended studies. Finally, we found the expression of both CD9 and CD63 is decreased in chronic kidney patients compared to healthy controls. An unexplained observation is the detection of two groups of samples based on the CD9/CD63 ratio.

PS12.02.

## Exovesicle concentration and cargo in patient derived ALS cellular model are modulated upon treatment with TDP‐43 modulators
Carmen Pérez de la Lastra1; Carlota Tosat‐Bitrián2; Alejandro Bueso3; Ana Martinez2; Angeles Martin‐Requero4; Manuel Izquierdo5; Valle Palomo6

1Centro de Investigaciones Biológicas Margarita Salas CSIC, Madrid, Spain; 2Centro de Investigaciones Biológicas Margarita Salas CSIC, Calle Ramiro de Maeztu, 9, 28040 Madrid; CIBERNED Centro de Investigación Biomédica en Red Enfermedades Neurodegenerativas, Calle de Valderrebollo 5, 28031 Madrid, Spain, Madrid, Spain; 3IMDEA Nanociencia, Calle Faraday 9, Madrid 28049, Spain, Madrid, Spain; 4Centro de Investigaciones Biológicas Margarita Salas CSIC, Calle Ramiro de Maeztu, 9, 28040 Madrid; CIBERNED Centro de Investigación Biomédica en Red Enfermedades Neurodegenerativas, Calle de Valderrebollo 5, 28031 Madrid, Spain, MADRID, Spain; 5Departamento de Metabolismo y Señalización Celular, Instituto de Investigaciones Biomédicas Alberto Sols, CSIC‐UAM, 28029 Madrid, Spain, Madrid, Spain; 6IMDEA Nanociencia, Calle Faraday 9, Madrid 28049, Spain; 3CIBERNED Centro de Investigación Biomédica en Red Enfermedades Neurodegenerativas, Calle de Valderrebollo 5, 28031 Madrid, Spain, MADRID, Spain

Introduction: Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS) is a progressive neurodegenerative disease characterized by the degeneration of the upper motor neurons in the brain and lower motor neurons in the spinal cord, which are responsible for voluntary muscle movement. Due to the heterogeneity of the disease and the lack of biomarkers the pharmacological evaluation of new drugs is a challenging task. Current animal models do not reflect the human pathology and diversity. Therefore we utilize a model of lymphoblasts derived from patients to characterize the pathology and its response to treatment. Extracellular vesicles (EVs) have shown to be dysregulated in the disease and here we explore whether pharmacological treatment would alter EVs biogenesis and cargo load in our model. We focused in TDP‐43 modulators since this protein is found aggregated in 97% of sporadic ALS patients and a severe dysregulation has been shown to be present in the disease.

Methods: Lymphoblastic cell lines derived from ALS patients and healthy controls were incubated in RPMI‐1640 media supplemented with 10% exosome depleted FBS and 1% penicillin streptomycin. They were treated with 2 μM CK‐1δ inhibitor IGS‐2.7 for 72 h. EVs were isolated by ultracentrifugation and utilizing Thermofisher Total Exosome Isolation Reagent. EVs were characterized by TEM, WB, nanoparticle tracking analysis, and TDP‐43 content was also analyzed by TR‐FRET. Finally EVs were incubated 2 h with biotinylated antibody anti‐human CD63, followed by a overnight incubation with streptavidin‐conjugated Quantum Dot (QDs) nanoparticles and were analyzed by nanoparticle tracking analysis.

Results: ALS lymphoblasts present a higher concentration of EVs than healthy control lymphoblasts, and it is reduced upon pharmacological treatment. In addition, the cargo loading of the isolated EVs was also modulated after μM CK‐1δ inhibitor IGS‐2.7 treatment considering an equal number of vesicles. TDP‐43 protein was able to be quantified using a TR‐FRET assay showing a correlation with both pathological events that was also modulated upon pharmacological recovery. Lastly, we present a method to characterize our EV samples using fluorescent QDs.

Summary/Conclusion: Patient derived models of sporadic ALS show EVs alteration when compared to healthy controls. This alteration was shown to be modulated upon pharmacological treatment against the most prominent pathological feature of ALS, which involves TDP‐43 phosphorylation.

Keywords: Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis

PS12.04.

## Are cytokines associated with extracellular vesicles? Investigation of EVs from biofluids and stem cell‐derived astrocytes and microglia
Tanina Arab1; Zhaohao Liao1; Rajini Nagaraj2; Evan Gizzie3; Vasiliki Mahairaki4; David A. Routenberg3; Kenneth W. Witwer5

1Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Johns Hopkins School of Medicine, Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, MD, USA, Baltimore, USA; 2MesoScale Diagnostics, LLC, Rockville, MD, USA, Bethesda, USA; 3MesoScale Diagnostics, LLC, Rockville, MD, USA, Rockville, USA; 4Department of Genetics, Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Baltimore, MD, USA. The Richman Family Precision Medicine Center of Excellence in Alzheimer's Disease, Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Baltimore, MD, US., Baltimore, USA; 5Department of Molecular and Comparative Pathobiology, Department of Neurology, Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Baltimore, MD, USA. Johns Hopkins School of Medicine, Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore, MD, USA. The Richman Family Precision Medicine Center of Excellence in Alzheimer's Disease, Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Baltimore, MD, US, Baltimore, USA

Introduction: Several reports indicate that EVs are associated with cytokines. However, it remains unclear if cytokines are true EV components or co‐isolates. Using an ultrasensitive multiplexed ELISA method, we measured nine cytokines in EV preparations from cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), blood plasma, and cell culture of stem cell‐derived astrocytes and microglia.

Methods: CSF, blood plasma, and saliva were fractionated by differential ultracentrifugation (dUC, including high‐speed pellets and supernatant) and by SEC with 70 nm cutoff and/or 35 nm (CSF only). Induced pluripotent stem cells (iPSCs) were differentiated into astrocytes and microglia and activated or not with LPS. Cell culture EVs were separated by dUC and SEC. EVs were characterized per MISEV2018. Nine cytokines were measured by ultrasensitive multiplexed ELISA (Meso Scale), sensitive to fg/mL levels.

Results: IL‐1beta and IL‐6 were the major detected cytokines in the samples used here and showed large differences in abundance between EV‐depleted and EV‐enriched fractions. Levels in UC pellets and SEC EV fractions were much lower; e.g., 70 nm SEC EV fractions had ∼1.6% on average, across samples, of the level in protein fractions, and 4.0% for 35 nm SEC. Across all sample types, these differences were smallest for plasma fractions. In saliva samples, IL‐1beta was the central detectable cytokine, with least abundance in the high‐speed UC pellet. IL‐6 was quantifiable in all astrocyte releasate fractions, including EVs, and LPS stimulation increased IL‐6 concentration in EV and protein fractions by 7.5 and 2.0‐fold, respectively. In contrast, released very little IL‐6 in EV fractions compared with protein fractions, with no differences under LPS stimulation.

Summary/Conclusion: Ultrasensitive multiplexed ELISA revealed that IL‐6 and IL‐1beta were the main measured cytokines in EV fractions from three human biofluids and several cell types. Some signal was associated with EVs, but at levels much lower than those in EV‐depleted fractions. Protease protection assays are currently underway to determine if these cytokines are located inside or outside EVs.

Funding: This work is supported by The Michael J. Fox Foundation

Keywords: cytokine, extracellular vesicles, IL‐6, IL‐1b, microglia, astrocytes, plasma, CSF,

PS12.05.

## Extracellular Vesicle‐Enriched miRNA‐Biomarkers Show Improved Utility For Detecting Alzheimer's Disease Dementia and Medial Temporal Atrophy
Gunnar H. Poplawski1; Yuek Ling Chai2; Lea Strohm2; Yanan Zhu2; Rachel Chia2; Joyce Ruifen Chong2; Li Han Zhou3; Heng Phon Too2; Saima Hilal2; Edward Koo2; Christopher Chen2

1Duke‐NUS, Singapore, Singapore; 2National University of Singapore, Singapore, Singapore; 3MiRXES, Singapore, Singapore

Introduction: It is hypothesized that extracellular vesicles (EVs) protect circulating miRNA‐biomarkers from degradation within the patient blood. To test this hypothesis we compared miRNAs isolated from EVs and from serum from the same AD and control patient cohorts. miRNA profiling revealed a significant diagnostic advantage of utilizing EV‐derived miRNAs.

Methods: A total of 25 patients with AD dementia and 34 controls with no cognitive impairment (NCI) from a Singapore‐based clinical cohort were included in this study. miRNA profiling was performed on both serum and blood‐derived EVs from the same patient cohort, followed by association analyses with AD dementia and brain medial temporal atrophy.

Results: We identified almost twice as many EV‐miRNAs with strong associations and diagnostic ability in detecting AD dementia and medial temporal atrophy, as compared to serum‐miRNAs. We further developed combination panels of serum‐ miRNAs and EV‐miRNAs with improved performance in identifying AD dementia from patient samples.

Summary/Conclusion: These data support the further utilization and development of EVs as carriers for disease predicting biomarkers for neurodegenerative diseases and provide potentially new biomarkers for the diagnosis of AD.

Funding: This work was supported by the National Medical Research Council (NMRC/CSA‐SI/007/2016 to CPC and MKPL; NMRC/CG/M006/2017,
NMRC/CG/013/2013 and NMRC/CG/NUHS/2010 to CPC), OFYIRG19MAY‐0024, The miRNA discovery work was conducted at the NUS ncRNA Core Facility and supported by the joint NUSMed‐MiRXES ncRNA Core Service Grant (2018).

Keywords: Alzheimer's disease, dementia, microRNA, extracellular vesicle, medial temporal atrophy, blood biomarker, diagnosis

PS12.06.

## The first ToF‐SIMS‐based studies on glycan composition of endothelial cell EVs under normoglycemic and hyperglycemic conditions
Magdalena E. Marzec1; Carina Rząca2; Małgorzata Przybyło3; Ewa Ł Stępień2

1Department of Medical Physics, M. Smoluchowski Institute of Physics, Faculty of Physics, Astronomy and Applied Computer Science, Jagiellonian University in Krakow, Poland, Krakow, Poland; 2Department of Medical Physics, M. Smoluchowski Institute of Physics, Faculty of Physics, Astronomy and Applied Computer Science, Jagiellonian University, Kraków, Poland, Kraków, Poland; 3Department of Glycoconjugate Biochemistry, Institute of Zoology and Biomedical Research, Faculty of Biology, Jagiellonian University in Krakow, Poland, Krakow, Poland

Introduction: Secondary ion mass spectrometry with a time‐of‐flight analyzer (ToF‐SIMS) is the method of choice for the analysis of biomolecules in tissues, cells and membranes. It enables the qualitative semi‐native examination without extraction, fixation or marking of target elements with simultaneous high‐resolution 2D imaging. Due to the analyzed mass range, the research objects are mainly amino acids and lipids, e.g. sphingolipids. As glycosylation is not a specific template process, the final glycan formation is determined by the expression of genes and proteins involved in glycan biosynthesis pathways, as well as the availability of sugar donors, to name these major factors. It is scarcely proven whether external sugar concentrations can modify glycoaminoglycans (GAGs) and glycolipids. Here, we explore the GAGs changes by comparative ToF‐SIMS analysis in extracellular vesicles (EVs) derived from TIME cells culture in normoglycemic and hyperglycemic conditions.

Methods: Cells were exposed to normoglycemic and hyperglycemic (HG) conditions at 35 mM glucose. A Low‐Vacuum Filtration (L‐VF) and ultracentrifugation were used to separate different subpopulations of EVs: exosomes and ectosomes . For measurements, EVs were applied in PBS solution on cleaned silicon surfaces. TOF‐SIMS equipped with the Bi3+ gun was used.

Results: Differences in the composition of glycosphingolipids, as well as glycans coupled with ceramide, in EVs were observed in EVs. Culturing cells in HG caused changes in the glycosaminoglycans profile, such as heparin, chondroitin sulphates and heparan in EVs.

Summary/Conclusion: The modified composition of the specific pattern of sugar antigens on the surface of exosomes and exosomes may interfere with basic processes related to cell behaviour, recognition and cell adhesion, thereby disrupting the proper flow of information and physiological intercellular interactions. The examined glycans on the surface of EVs derived from HG cells distinguishes them from normoglycemic EVs and indicate that they could be used to detect, determine the advancement of, among others, diabetes as well as be the target of personalized therapy.

Funding: This work was supported by the National Science Center (NCN), grant OPUS 17 to prof. E. Stępień (No. 2019/33/B/NZ3/01004).

Keywords: ToF‐SIMS; glycan; hyperglycemia; endothelial cell

PS12.07.

## EV‐miRNA sequencing with IsoSeek for response prediction in patients with refractory Multiple Myeloma
Cristina Gómez‐Martín1; E.E.E. Drees2; M.A.J. van Eijndhoven3; Nils Groenewegen2; Ernesto Aparicio‐Puerta4; E.J. Bradley1; Leontien Bosch3; C. Scheepbouwer1; Karin Oudshoorn5; Johan de Rooij6; Michael Hackenberg7; Niels van der Donk8; Dirk M. Pegtel3

1Amsterdam University Medical Centers, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 2amsterdam university medical centers, amsterdam, Netherlands; 3Department of Pathology, Cancer Center Amsterdam, Amsterdam UMC location Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, The Netherlands, Amsterdam, Netherlands; 4Chair for Clinical Bioinformatics, Saarland University, Saarbrücken, Germany; 5Department of Health Technology and Services Research, University of Twente, Twente, Netherlands; 6ExBiome B.V., Amsterdam, Netherlands; 7Department of Genetics, University of Granada, Granada, Spain; 8Department of Hematology, Amsterdam University Medical Centers, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Introduction: Patients diagnosed with Multiple Myeloma as incurable cancer of the bone marrow, have many treatment options but personalizing treatment is difficult. The only current method to assess response to therapy in clinical routine is measuring M‐protein levels in either the blood or urine, despite a suboptimal specificity and sensitivity. It is well known that miRNAs are shed by cancer cells and their microenvironment into the circulation. MiRNA profiling with sequencing is the preferred method but challenges for their faithful detection have hampered implementation in routine diagnostics. Especially when input is limited ligation/amplification bias and generation of artificial variants (isomiRs) may occur. We developed an unbiased, UMI‐informed and low‐input optimized IsoSeek protocol that measures miRNAs with single‐nucleotide accuracy in EV‐enriched plasma fractions and applied this method to predict response to therapy in MM patients.

Methods: We developed ‘IsoSeek’ a method that diverges from prior protocols combining randomized adapters and 10N unique molecular identifiers. Upon exhaustive rounds of optimization, we show extensive isomiR detection and strongly reduced PCR‐bias compared to commercial protocols. We sequenced 71 samples from MM patients undergoing 2 types of treatment with the IsoSeek method. Lasso regression models (glmnet R package) were built with the data to obtain prognostic miRNA signatures to distinguish responders from non‐responders.

Results: We found that a 9 miRNA model can discriminate MM patients with progressive disease from those responding to treatment (Based on M protein the current gold standard) with an AUC of 0.89 when tested in an independent dataset. More complex signatures with 179‐499 features using canonical miRNA sequences only or 3’end isomiRs (non‐templated additions), enhanced the AUC to 0.91 (0.8‐1.00) and 0.92 (0.82‐1.00) respectively. These data are currently being validated in plasma EVs from an independent MM cohort.

Summary/Conclusion: We present here a tentative model based on 9 miRNAs that can be used to predict the response to treatment in Multiple Myeloma patients with high sensitivity and specificity and that has been validated in an independent test dataset. IsoSeek advances our knowledge of miRNA diversity and supports the development of small RNA sequencing as a robust platform for liquid biopsy.

Funding: AQrate, KWF‐5510, CCA2021‐9‐77, Amgen

Keywords: miRNAs, biomarkers, Multiple Myeloma

PS12.08.

## Estrogen deficiency caused by menopause blunts acute exercise‐induced circulating microRNA response in extracellular vesicles
Sira M. Karvinen1; Tia‐Marje Korhonen1; Tero Sievänen1; Jari Karppinen1; Hanna‐Kaarina Juppi1; Veera Jakoaho1; Urho Kujala2; Jari Laukkanen3; Maarit Lehti2; Eija Laakkonen4

1Gerontology Research Center and Faculty of Sport and Health Sciences, University of Jyväskylä, Jyväskylä, Finland; 2Faculty of Sport and Health Sciences, University of Jyväskylä, Jyväskylä, Finland; 3Institute of Clinical Medicine, University of Eastern Finland, Jyväskylä, Finland; 4Gerontology Research Center and Faculty of Sport and Health Sciences, University of Jyväskylä,, Jyväskylä, Finland

Introduction: Menopausal decrease in circulating estrogen levels affects metabolic health. However, the detailed mechanisms underlying this process remain unclear. Both estrogens and exercise improve metabolic health, which may be partly mediated by small non‐coding RNAs, such as microRNAs (miRs). In recent years, extracellular vesicles (EVs) have attracted interest in the field of tissue crosstalk. However, in many studies on EVs, the co‐isolation of high‐density lipoprotein (HDL) particles has not been considered. This study investigated the role of EVs as estrogen and exercise‐responsive small RNA (sRNA) carriers.

Methods: We utilized plasma samples from a subset of the Estrogen, microRNAs and the risk of metabolic dysfunction (EsmiRs) study consisting of 14 postmenopausal women. Of them, seven used estrogen‐based hormonal therapy (HT) and seven were nonusers. Groups were matched for age, peak aerobic capacity (VO2peak), body mass index, and body fat percentage. Subjects performed a maximal incremental VO2peak test on a bicycle ergometer and had plasma samples harvested PRE, POST and 1 h POST exercise test. EVs from plasma were isolated using sequential ultracentrifugation followed by size‐exclusion chromatography to separate HDL particles from EVs. We carried out a Next‐Generation Sequencing (NGS) analysis for the EV samples. The resulting sequences were categorized by sRNA origin and differential expression analysis was conducted by DESeq2.

Results: When examining HT users and nonusers together, we observed an increase in the relative abundance of miRs (+3.1% points) and transfer RNA‐derived sRNAs (+2.1% points), while the relative amount of ribosomal RNA‐derived sRNAs decreased (‐5.0% points) immediately after exercise (POST‐PRE). When investigating the differentially expressed sRNAs, miRs 191‐5p, 223‐3p, and 146b‐5p were significantly upregulated in HT users immediately after exercise (POST vs. PRE, p< 0.05). In addition, miR‐486‐5p was upregulated and miR‐340‐5p downregulated in HT users 1h POST vs. POST (p< 0.05). There were no significant findings in the studied time points in nonusers.

Summary/Conclusion: Our results highlight that the estrogen deficiency caused by menopause diminishes the acute exercise‐induced miR‐response in EVs.

Funding: This study was funded by the Academy of Finland (grants 332946, 275323, 309504, 314181, and 335249).

PS12.09.

## Electrically evoked contractions in the C2C12 myotubes increase miR‐1‐3p packing into extracellular vesicles
Juulia H. Lautaoja1; Sira M. Karvinen2; Juha J. Hulmi1; Satu Pekkala1

1Faculty of Sport and Health Sciences, University of Jyväskylä, Jyväskylä, Finland; 2Gerontology Research Center and Faculty of Sport and Health Sciences, University of Jyväskylä, Jyväskylä, Finland

Introduction: Exercise‐induced health benefits are thought to be mediated partly via skeletal muscle‐derived signaling molecules, which are carried via extracellular vesicles (EVs). However, only in vitro approaches enable exclusive examination of the EVs and their cargo originating from the skeletal muscle. Myotube contractions evoked by exercise‐like electric pulse stimulation (EL‐EPS) in vitro induce many key physiological responses both intra‐ and extracellularly as in vivo exercise. This model can be used to verify whether the contracting skeletal muscle cells release specific micros‐RNAs (miRs) within the EVs as has been shown after in vivo exercise.

Methods: The 24‐hour low‐frequency EL‐EPS (1 Hz, 2 ms, 12 V) was applied on the murine C2C12 myotubes under serum‐free conditions via carbon electrodes attached to C‐Pace EM stimulator (Ionoptix Corporation). The electrodes were placed on all plates to exclude possible electrode‐mediated effects. The media were collected immediately after EL‐EPS and the EVs were isolated by using exoRNeasy serum/plasma kit (Qiagen). Nanoparticle tracking analysis (NTA) and electron microscopy (EM) were used to validate successful EV isolation. Real‐time qPCR was used to analyze the EV miR content.

Results: Based on the NTA and EM analyses, myotube‐derived EVs were successfully isolated and of expected size (∼100 nm). The content of miR‐1‐3p increased in the EVs after EL‐EPS (P < 0.05), while miR‐133a‐3p also increased by several fold, but due to high variation, this result was non‐significant (P = 0.242).

Summary/Conclusion: In agreement with the in vivo exercise studies, we showed that myotube contractions increased the packing of miR‐1‐3p, and possibly miR‐133a‐3p, into the released EVs. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to examine in vitro the contraction‐induced effects on the EV miR content released by the myotubes.

Funding: The Academy of Finland (grant no. 332946 to S.K. and 308042 to S.P.) and the Finnish Cultural Foundation (to J.H.L) funded this work.

Keywords: Exercise, skeletal muscle, myotube, contraction, microRNA

PS12.10.

## Proteomic profiling of small extracellular vesicles from head and neck squamous cell carcinoma cells uncovers signatures for tumor hypoxia and angiogenesis
Alicja Głuszko1; Mirosław J. Szczepański1; Theresa L. Whiteside2; Torsten E. Reichert3; Jacek Siewiera4; Nils Ludwig5

1Chair and Department of Biochemistry, Medical University of Warsaw, 1 Banacha St., 02‐097 Warsaw, Poland, Warsaw, Poland; 2UPMC Hillman Cancer Center, University of Pittsburgh, Cancer Institute Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, USA, Pittsburgh, USA; 3Department of Oral and Maxillofacial Surgery, University Hospital Regensburg, 93053 Regensburg, Germany, Regensburg, Germany; 4Department of Hyperbaric Medicine, Military Institute of Medicine, 04‐141 Warsaw, Poland, Warsaw, Poland; 5Department of Oral and Maxillofacial Surgery, University Hospital Regensburg, 93053 Regensburg, Germany, Regensburg, Poland

Introduction: Molecular and functional alterations in head and neck squamous cell carcinomas (HNSCCs) are a result of commonly observed tissue hypoxia and it is well known that exposure to hypoxic conditions triggers the formation of pathological vasculature, which ultimately compromises patient outcome.

Objectives: The aim of this study was to characterize the influence of hypoxic vs. normoxic conditions on the release and proteomic content of tumor‐derived small extracellular vesicles (sEVs).

Methods: Material & Methods: HNSCC cells (FaDu, PCI‐30, SCC‐25) and HaCaT keratinocytes were exposed to 21, 10, 5, 1% oxygen supply. sEVs were isolated from supernatants using size exclusion chromatography (SEC) and characterized by nanoparticle tracking analysis, electron microscopy, immunoblotting, and high‐resolution mass spectrometry.

Results: Results: Isolated sEVs ranged in size from 125–135 nm and carried CD63 and CD9 but not Grp94. HNSCC cells adapted to hypoxic conditions with increased release of sEVs, which were enriched in unique protein profiles. The hypoxic profile of HNSCC parent cells was reflected in the proteome cargo of sEVs: about 15% of the total detected proteins in sEVs were unique for hypoxic cells. Seven hypoxia‐related proteins (KT33B, DYSF, STON2, MLX, LIPA3, NEK5, P12L1) represented a common signature of hypoxic sEVs, which were also enriched in pro‐angiogenic proteins.

Summary/Conclusion: Conclusion: The degree of tumor hypoxia was reflected in protein profiles of sEVs, therefore, sEVs could potentially serve as clinical biomarkers for hypoxic conditions.

Funding: This work was partially funded from the National Science Centre, Poland UMO‐2017/26/M/NZ5/00877# to M.J.S. and from Medical University of Warsaw MB/M/48(79)# to A.G., T.L.W. was supported in part by the NIH grant U0‐1 DE 029759. N.L. was supported by the Walter Schulz Foundation.
